Efficient Differentiable Rendering:

ii
Abstract
Rendering is traditionally seen as a forward process that generates an image from a
description of a virtual scene. In recent years, this scope was significantly broadened
and rendering algorithms have been used to solve several challenging problems across
computer vision, medicine, and other domains. This development is based on the ability
to differentiate rendering algorithms, which means being able to quantify how variations
of the virtual scene affect a generated image. Among others, this allows solving inverse
problems, i.e., finding a representation of an environment that explains given (image)
observations, automatically using gradient-based methods.
In rendering, one typically distinguishes between physically-based algorithms that
are costly but accurately simulate light transport, and rendering algorithms that are focused on interactive performance but use various approximations for light transport.
The reality of differentiable rendering has shown that both classes have their merit, and
even further that inverse applications have brought up rendering algorithms that are
specifically tailored to certain optimization tasks. Regardless of the application, efficiency is a universal goal in differentiable rendering, among others, because it enables
faster exploration of larger solution spaces and even broader applicability. In this thesis,
we tackle efficiency in three ways unique to differentiable rendering.
First, we make building blocks from real-time rendering available to differentiable
rendering by ensuring that derivatives are well-defined while maintaining efficiency.
This enables using cheap approximations for certain light transport effects in differentiable renderers. For one, we show that one of the oldest approximations for shadows,
shadow maps, can be adapted to work in differentiable rendering frameworks. And further, we introduce a more general family of moment-based approximations to differentiable rendering. We provide a robust and fast implementation that is based on a proof
of differentiability, and show that the resulting techniques yield significantly higher accuracy than existing approaches while being equally efficient.
Second, we reduce the computational cost and memory requirements of differentiating physically-based rendering algorithms. This not only improves efficiency but also
broadens their applicability, making them practical in new application domains. More
specifically, we revisit an algorithm for efficient reverse-mode differentiation, and introduce a variant that correctly accounts for derivatives with respect to scene geometry. We
then turn our focus towards time-resolved rendering and show how the same algorithm
can be extended beyond the steady-state setting, introducing time-resolved path replay
backpropagation. We demonstrate that this algorithm has broad applications beyond
graphics, enabling efficient and fully-differentiable path tracing for geometric acoustics.
Lastly, we develop a rendering algorithm that exploits the structure of inverse applications, using efficient approximations of light transport tailored to the task. We demonstrated this with the example of 3D surface reconstruction from images, by combining
efficient triangle rasterization with the expressiveness of neural rendering. This design
enables efficient 3D reconstruction in environments with arbitrary materials and illumination, without having to resort to costly light transport simulations.
iv
Zusammenfassung
Rendering wird traditionell als ein Vorwärtsprozess betrachtet, der ein Bild aus der
Beschreibung einer virtuellen Szene generiert. In den vergangenen Jahren hat sich der
Anwendungsbereich deutlich ausgeweitet und Rendering-Algorithmen wurden eingesetzt, um verschiedenste Probleme im maschinellen Sehen, in der Medizin, oder in anderen Feldern zu lösen. Diese Entwicklung basiert hauptsächlich darauf, RenderingAlgorithmen differenzieren zu können, also quantifizieren zu können, welche Auswirkungen Veränderungen der virtuellen Szene auf das generierte Bild haben. Dies ermöglicht
unter anderem, inverse Problemstellungen – also das Finden einer Szene, die eine vorgegebene Beobachtung (z. B. ein Bild) besonders plausibel wiedergibt – automatisiert
mit Hilfe von Gradientenabstiegsverfahren zu lösen.
Typischerweise wird beim Rendering zwischen zwei Klassen von Algorithmen unterschieden: physikbasierte, die kostspielig sind, aber dafür genaue Ergebnisse liefern, und
solche mit Fokus auf interaktive Anwendungen, die zwar sehr effizient sind, dafür aber
nur approximative Ergebnisse erzeugen. Die Vergangenheit hat gezeigt, dass für differenzierbares Rendering beide Klassen eine Daseinsberechtigung haben, und darüber
hinaus sogar gänzlich neue Rendering-Algorithmen entwickelt wurden, die an gewisse
Problemstellungen gekoppelt sind. Unabhängig von der Anwendung ist Effizienz ein
allgemeines Ziel, denn dadurch werden das noch schnellere Ergründen noch größerer
Lösungsräume und ein weitreichenderer Einsatz ermöglicht. In dieser Arbeit beschäftigen wir uns auf drei Arten mit der Effizienz von differenzierbarem Rendering.
Zuerst ermöglichen wir den Einsatz diverser Komponenten aus der Echtzeitgrafik in
diesem Kontext, indem wir deren Differenzierbarkeit und Effizienz sicherstellen. Das erlaubt es, günstige Approximationen gewisser Lichttransporteffekte in differenzierbaren
Renderern einzusetzen. Genauer zeigen wir zunächst, dass einer der ältesten Algorithmen zur Erzeugung von Schatten, „Shadow Mapping“, für das differenzierbare Rendering
angepasst werden kann. Des Weiteren führen wir eine allgemeinere Familie von Approximationen, basierend auf Momenten, ein. Wir stellen eine robuste und performante
Implementierung bereit, welche auf einem theoretischen Beweis der Differenzierbarkeit
basiert. Wir zeigen, dass diese Techniken im Vergleich zu existierenden Ansätzen eine
deutlich höhere Genauigkeit liefern, bei vergleichbaren Kosten.
Zweitens reduzieren wir den Rechenaufwand und den Speicherverbrauch, die zum
Differenzieren physikbasierter Algorithmen benötigt werden. Das erhöht nicht nur die
Effizienz dieser Algorithmen, sondern erweitert auch deren Anwendbarkeit und Praktikabilität in neuen Feldern. Wir schauen uns zunächst einen existierenden Algorithmus
für effizientes Differenzieren im Rückwärtsmodus an und führen eine neue Variante ein,
die korrekte Ableitungen bezüglich der Szenengeometrie erzeugt. Wir wenden uns dann
dem zeitaufgelösten Rendering zu und zeigen, dass derselbe Algorithmus dahingehend
erweitert werden kann. Wir zeigen, dass unser Algorithmus, Time-Resolved Path Replay
Backpropagation, ein breites Anwendungsspektrum außerhalb der Computergrafik hat
und für effizientes, differenzierbares Path Tracing im Kontext von geometrischer Akustik
verwendet werden kann.
Schließlich entwickeln wir einen Rendering-Algorithmus, der die Struktur eines in-
vi
versen Problems ausnutzt und effiziente, anwendungsspezifische Approximationen für
Lichttransport verwendet. Wir zeigen das am Beispiel der 3D-Oberflächenrekonstruktion
aus Bildern und kombinieren effiziente Dreiecksrasterung mit der Ausdruckskraft von
neuronalem Rendering. Dieses Verfahren ermöglicht eine effiziente 3D-Rekonstruktion
in Umgebungen mit beliebigen Materialien und Beleuchtungsbedingungen, ohne dass
kostspielige Lichttransportsimulationen erforderlich sind.
Acknowledgements
First and foremost, I am deeply grateful to my advisor Marc Alexa. For the trust he put
in me, the constant support, and the guidance he provided over the past few years. I do
not take it for granted that he gave me the freedom to pursue the research questions I
was most excited about, despite them slightly deviating from typical questions in geometry. His knowledge and the many insightful perspectives contributed substantially to
the success of my research projects.
I would like to thank Wenzel Jakob, Michael Wimmer, and Guillermo Gallego for serving
on the thesis committee and dedicating their time, not only by reviewing my thesis but
also by physically attending the defense.
I was fortunate to spend my journey almost in equal parts between two labs. In the
IMC group at Fraunhofer HHI, I took my first steps in research. I am grateful to have
been given the opportunity to contribute to various research projects with immediate
real-world impact, especially those related to cultural heritage. I would like to thank
Rodrigo Diaz, Marcus Zepp, Mykyta Kovalenko, Weiwen Hu, and Decai Chen for the
many fun moments and insightful discussions, as well as Oliver Schreer, Ingo Feldmann,
and Peter Eisert for their guidance. In the Computer Graphics group at TU Berlin, I matured as a researcher. Being surrounded by so many talented colleagues and students
has been an incredible learning experience. Thanks to Anna Koch for involving me in
my first research project at TU Berlin – witnessing the collaboration and submission
cycles from the inside was an invaluable lesson. Thanks to Ugo Finnendahl, Maximilian
Kohlbrenner, Tobias Djuren, Hendrik Meyer, Dimitris Bogiokas, and Gabriel Dogadov
for making this an enjoyable time, the fun moments we had on our journeys around
the world, and, of course, the many deep discussions about research and life. Thanks to
Gaëlle Fer-Arslan for always having our back on the administrative side of things.
Thanks to the collaborators I have been fortunate to work with over the years, in particular Ugo Finnendahl, Tobias Jüterbock, Rodrigo Diaz, Weiwen Hu, and Daniel Wujecki,
without whom many of the projects in this thesis would not have been possible. Apart
from my colleagues and advisors, various researchers from the graphics community have
influenced my approach to research, visualization, and writing, including but not limited
to Alec Jacobson, Baptiste Nicolet, Daniele Panozzo, Delio Vicini, Hsueh-Ti Derek Liu,
Keenan Crane, Merlin Nimier-David, Silvia Sellán, Tzu-Mao Li, and Wenzel Jakob. I am
also grateful to the European Union for funding part of the research in this thesis.

Tiefsten Dank an meine Eltern Petra und Frank für ihre Unterstützung in all den
Jahren und die Freiheit, jederzeit meinen eigenen Weg gehen zu können, und natürlich
an meinen Bruder Thomas, von dem ich schon früh viel über Computer lernen konnte.
Ich bin unendlich glücklich, dass ich diesen Weg zusammen mit Isabelle gehen durfte.
Ohne ihre Liebe, ihre unermüdliche Unterstützung und ihr Verständnis in stressigen
Phasen wäre diese Reise nur schwer zu bewältigen gewesen.

Chapter 1
Introduction
Computer graphics has traditionally seen rendering as a forward process: generate an
image from a description of a virtual scene, which includes the geometry of objects,
their materials, and camera parameters. The main artifact of interest has been the image,
which serves as the final output in typical applications for rendering systems, like movie
production, visualization, or video games.
In recent years, it has become clear that rendering algorithms have a much broader
scope. Instead of merely serving as image factories, they have been used to solve challenging problems across computer vision, medicine, robotics, manufacturing, and computational design more generally. This particular development is based on the ability to
relate the outputs back to the inputs. For rendering, this means being able to quantify
how variations of the virtual scene affect the generated image (see Fig. 1.1). The reason
this is useful is that many tasks in the above domains can be formulated as inverse problems: given an observation, for example, a photograph, what (real-world) environment
has likely led to it? Fundamentally, the idea is to take a virtual scene and adjust its parameters until rendered images match the observations, in which case the virtual scene
provides a possible explanation. A key realization is that when the effect of scene parameters on the generated image can be quantified, this process can be done in a structured
and automated manner.
If seen abstractly, rendering algorithms are simply mathematical functions that map
some inputs (the parameters of the scene) to some outputs (the image). The relation
between inputs and outputs of a function is described by the derivatives. Differentiable
rendering seeks to apply this to rendering algorithms, so that the primary artifact of
interest is not only the image but also its derivatives with respect to scene parameters.
Given the complexity of modern renderers, it might appear infeasible to differentiate
these systems. However, on the lowest level, all of them simply perform a sequence
of numerical computations according to mathematical models and equations, most of
which can be viewed and differentiated in isolation.
The various building blocks of rendering algorithms have traditionally differed depending on the application, although they have been converging in recent years. On the
one hand, physically-based rendering aims at generating images that faithfully reproduce the appearance of the real world. It simulates the propagation of light through a
scene and its interaction with objects by solving the physical equations of light transport.
This enables accurate reproduction of various phenomena, including soft shadows, interreflection, and caustics. The accuracy comes at the price of efficiency, such that these
systems are typically used in offline scenarios, for example, movie production. On the
other hand, various rendering algorithms focus on efficiency and interactive scenarios,
2
Chapter 1. Introduction
Positive sign
Negative sign
Brighter
+
0
Rendered image
Change of color at each pixel
-
Change of intensity Darker
Figure 1.1: Differentiable rendering is concerned with computing derivatives of rendered
images with respect to parameters of a virtual scene. The derivatives capture the effect
of infinitesimal perturbations of the scene on each pixel, here shown for a rightward
movement of the teapot (middle). The signed quantity is usually averaged over channels
and displayed using color mapping to visualize the change of intensity (right).
which typically requires several approximations of light transport. The aim is to generate images that appear plausible in real-time applications like video games or interactive
visualization.
The reality of differentiable rendering has shown that both classes have their merit,
and the appropriate balance between physical accuracy and efficiency strongly depends
on the specific inverse application. Even further, many solutions rely on rendering algorithms that are tailored to the requirements of the task at hand. While these methods
often build upon components of traditional rendering systems, they may also introduce
new approximations or techniques that are primarily motivated by the optimization objectives rather than forward image synthesis.
Regardless of the application, improving the efficiency of differentiable rendering
algorithms in terms of runtime and memory requirements is a universal goal. First, reducing computational cost lowers energy consumption, which is increasingly important
in the context of climate change and power-efficient computing. Second, as improvements in hardware continue to slow down, progress increasingly depends on developing
more efficient algorithms and software. Third, even with current hardware capabilities,
efficient methods enable more rapid exploration of a larger space of possible solutions.
Lastly, efficiency is a deciding factor in the practicability of solutions, so increasing efficiency also broadens the range of potential applications.
While efficiency in differentiable rendering shares the challenges of traditional forward rendering, as it also involves image synthesis, it comes with its own set of challenges and opportunities. In this thesis, we focus on the latter and tackle efficiency in
three ways specific to differentiable rendering:
1. We make building blocks from real-time rendering available by ensuring that derivatives are well-defined while maintaining efficiency. This enables using cheap approximations for certain light transport effects in a differentiable renderer.
2. We reduce the computational cost and memory requirements of differentiating
physically-based rendering algorithms. This not only improves efficiency but also
broadens their applicability, making them practical in new application domains.
1.1. Overview and Contributions
3
3. We develop rendering algorithms that exploit the structure of inverse applications,
using efficient approximations of light transport tailored to the task. This solves
inverse problems in complex environments without explicitly simulating light.
1.1
Overview and Contributions
This thesis contributes to efficient differentiable rendering in various ways. We will
contextualize the different contributions in the separate chapters and only provide a
brief overview here:
Chapter 3: An approach for differentiating approximations of cast shadows from
point light sources.
Chapter 4: An approach for differentiating approximations of cast shadows and
volumetric transmittance from point light sources.
Chapter 5: An algorithm for efficient reverse-mode differentiation of light transport simulations with parameters that potentially affect the scene geometry.
Chapter 6: An algorithm for efficient reverse-mode differentiation of energy transport simulations that are resolved in time, and an approach for fully-differentiable
geometric acoustic simulations.
Chapter 7: A rendering algorithm tailored to 3D multi-view reconstruction that
efficiently approximates light transport effects using machine learning techniques.

Chapter 1. Introduction
Chapter 2
Background
Images of virtual scenes can be generated in different ways: physically-based rendering
(Section 2.1) simulates light according to physical principles, whereas efficient real-time
systems commonly make simplifying assumptions to achieve interactive performance
(Section 2.2). While these approaches differ in accuracy and computational cost, both
ultimately produce images as the result of a numerical computation. Differentiable rendering (Section 2.4) uses this perspective and applies techniques for differentiating programs (Section 2.3) to traditional rendering algorithms to be able to compute derivatives
of rendered images with respect to arbitrary scene parameters.
2.1
Physically-based Rendering
Physically-based rendering (PBR) is the process of simulating light transport in virtual
scenes to synthesize images that reproduce the appearance of the real world. While light
has particle and wave characteristics, simulations often ignore wave effects, following
the principles of geometric optics: light travels along straight lines in homogeneous media, it can be absorbed or reflected, and it refracts when experiencing a change in velocity. Many effects of light transport can be convincingly reproduced using this model
(Fig. 2.1), and it also serves as the theoretical framework for this thesis. In this section,
we will provide a brief overview of relevant concepts and refer readers to Pharr et al.
[2016] for a more comprehensive treatment.
To simulate light transport, the virtual environment, or scene, first requires a mathematical and digital representation (Section 2.1.1). Only then can the physical equations
of light transport (Section 2.1.2) be solved computationally (Section 2.1.3).
2.1.1
Representation of a Virtual Scene
In a common abstraction of the real world, a virtual environment, or scene, is assumed
to consist of surfaces, i.e., infinitely thin shells that represent the boundaries of objects,
which are made of materials that determine how a surface interacts with incident light.
Emitters radiate light while sensors register it to produce an image or any other form of
measurement. This thesis is most concerned with surface light transport, so we do not
discuss participating media (e.g., fog or fire). The aforementioned concepts are briefly
introduced in the following.
8
Chapter 2. Background
Figure 2.1: An image generated with physically-based rendering (geometric optics).
Surface Geometry
Objects in a scene that interact with or emit light are commonly represented by surfaces,
i.e., a representation of their boundary. There are many different ways to describe surfaces mathematically and represent them in a computer. We will only introduce those
that are most relevant for this thesis.
Triangle Meshes Triangle meshes are piecewise linear representations where the surface is explicitly described by an assembly of flat, triangular facets. They are most ubiquitous in computer graphics and rendering, among others, because graphics processing
units (GPUs) possess specialized rasterization hardware to efficiently display them to a
𝑛 and
screen. Mathematically, they can be described by vertex positions V = (v𝑖 ∈ R3 )𝑖=1
a set of index tuples T = {(𝑖, 𝑗, 𝑘) ∈ N3 }, where each represents a triangle by the vertex
indices of its vertices. The surface normal is constant over a triangle (𝑖, 𝑗, 𝑘), and it is
given by n = (v 𝑗 − v𝑖 ) × (v𝑘 − v𝑖 ) (the index order determines the orientation).
Implicit Surfaces Surfaces can also be represented implicitly as the zero-level set of
some function 𝜙 : R3 → R, such that the set of all surface points is {x ∈ R3 | 𝜙 (x) = 0}.
In contrast to triangle meshes, this representation is more challenging to render because
displaying it effectively amounts to solving a root-finding problem. This generally has to
be done numerically (e.g., using variants of ray marching [Tuy and Tuy, 1984]). Signed
distance functions (SDFs) are a special class of implicit surfaces where the value 𝜙 (x)
is the signed distance from x to the closest point on the surface. The surface normal is
∇ 𝜙
given by the spatial gradient n = ∥∇xx𝜙 ∥ 2 . For signed distance functions, this is simply
n = ∇x𝜙 as they fulfill the Eikonal equation ∥∇x𝜙 ∥ 2 = 1 where 𝜙 is differentiable.
Emitters
Emitters generate the light that is simulated. Generally, objects in the scene or parts of
them can be characterized as emitters, in which case they are referred to as area emitters.
2.1. Physically-based Rendering
9
Interactive rendering often uses emitters that are not tied to specific objects in the scene
(Section 2.2.1), e.g., directional emitters as an approximation for the sun.
Light itself is electromagnetic radiation that can be perceived by the human visual
system, where different wavelengths are generally seen as different colors. While precisely handling the spectral characteristics of light is crucial for physical accuracy, we
restrict our computations to a tristimulus representation, with red, green, and blue components, as is common in many rendering systems.
The emitted light is described in terms of radiance 𝐿e (p, 𝝎), which is the radiant
flux (or radiant power) per unit area and solid angle. Notice that the emission is spatiodirectional, so it can vary for each point p on the emitter surface and direction 𝝎.
Materials
Materials determine how objects, or more precisely, their surfaces, interact with incident
light, including how it is reflected in different directions or transmitted through the
surface. The choice of material influences an object’s appearance, for example, whether
it appears to be made of brushed metal or wood. Generally, this behavior is described
by a bidirectional scattering distribution function (BSDF), and for the case of opaque
objects, by a bidirectional reflectance distribution function (BRDF):
𝑓 (p, 𝝎, 𝝎 𝑖 ).
(2.1)
At any point p on a surface, the BSDF (or BRDF) represents the ratio of infinitesimal
outgoing radiance in direction 𝝎 to the infinitesimal incident irradiance (i.e., radiance
incident over an infinitesimal projected solid angle) from direction 𝝎 𝑖 . Eq. (2.4) shows
how this fits into the light transport framework.
Example: Lambertian The Lambertian BRDF is one of the simplest models and describes a perfectly diffuse surface, where incident light from direction 𝝎 𝑖 is reflected
uniformly so that the surface appearance is independent of the viewing direction 𝝎:
𝑓 (p, 𝝎, 𝝎 𝑖 ) =
𝜌
.
𝜋
(2.2)
The reflectance 𝜌 ∈ [0, 1] determines the fraction of reflected incident light, and the
normalization by 𝜋 ensures energy conservation (no more light is reflected than what is
incident). While this is an idealized model, i.e., no real-world material behaves exactly
this way, it is commonly used throughout this thesis in synthetic experiments.
Sensors
Light can be scattered by objects in a scene, and ultimately it is measured by a sensor.
While sensors can generate any kind of measurement, the most commonly used in this
thesis are cameras that generate an image.
A single measurement can be described by the measurement equation [Veach, 1998]:

2.1.2
The Rendering Equation
In Section 2.1.1, we have covered how light is represented, emitted, and measured. We
now consider how light is transported through the scene to reach the sensor, starting
from an emitter. As noted before, we assume that the scene does not contain any participating media, so light travels through the scene in a vacuum and interacts only with
object surfaces. Under this assumption, radiance remains constant along straight lines
through empty space. For the most part of this thesis, we will also assume a steady state,
that is, the energy distribution in the scene has reached an equilibrium. In Chapter 6 we
will temporarily lift this last assumption, but discuss the implications there.
The relationship between incident radiance and outgoing radiance for any point in
the scene is described by the rendering equation [Kajiya, 1986]:
∫

2.1.3
Monte Carlo Methods
Simulating light transport amounts to solving a recursive integral equation (Section 2.1.2),
which effectively means that radiance is carried from the emitters to the sensor along potentially infinitely many light paths: recursively substituting 𝐿o using Eq. (2.7) expands
it into a sum of integrals over light paths of increasing length, where each additional surface interaction introduces another integral. Except for the most simplistic scenes, one
cannot hope to find closed-form solutions for these high-dimensional integrals, which
motivates numerical approaches.
Since the computational complexity of deterministic quadrature rules grows exponentially with the dimensionality of the integration domain, rendering typically relies
on Monte Carlo integration (Fig. 2.2): the integral is approximated by randomly sampling points in the high-dimensional integration domain, the samples, whose number is
independent of the problem dimensionality.
Monte Carlo Estimator To illustrate the approach consider the following integral
over a bounded domain D ∈ R𝑛 :
∫

It is easy to verify that the above estimator is unbiased, which means that it returns
the true value of the integral on average. Unbiasedness is expressed using the expected
value:
E[𝐹 𝑁 ] = 𝐹 .

Unbiasedness will play a central role in the second part of this thesis (Chapter 5, Chapter 6), not primarily of light transport simulations, but of their derivatives (Section 2.4).
In forward rendering, biased estimators will result in visible, yet sometimes tolerable,
artifacts. But, as we will see, bias in the derivatives can cause an optimization to fail,
even when the forward process is unbiased.

average. Estimators with high variance occasionally return poor estimates of the true
integral, even though they might be unbiased. Using the properties of variance, assuming the x𝑖 are independent and identically distributed, we get

which shows that there are two ways to decrease variance: (1) increasing the sample
count, or (2) using a probability density that aligns well with the shape of 𝑓 : if it were
possible to sample exactly proportional to 𝑓 , then the ratio would be a constant and the
variance would be zero.
In the context of rendering, sample generation (and evaluation) is a costly process
because each sample effectively corresponds to a light path through the scene. A significant part of rendering research is concerned with designing importance sampling
strategies to reduce variance at low sample counts; yet, exactly sampling proportional
to 𝑓 is typically infeasible. However, the integrand in rendering is a product of several
factors (e.g., incident radiance and BSDF), where individual components can often be
importance-sampled. A combination of different sampling strategies leads to multiple
importance sampling, for which we refer to [Pharr et al., 2016, Section 13.4].
2.2. Real-Time Rendering Techniques
13
Path Tracing Monte Carlo integration can be directly used to solve the rendering
equation. A simple and common algorithm that connects the pieces is path tracing (see
[Pharr et al., 2016, Section 13.2] for details). The high-level idea is to compute the integrals in Eq. (2.3) and Eq. (2.4) by gradually constructing light paths through the scene,
starting from the sensor: (1) sample a direction, often guided by importance sampling
(e.g., the BSDF), (2) trace a ray to determine the next intersection point, (3) evaluate
and add the path contribution, and (4) continue with (1). Each light path corresponds
to a single sample in a high-dimensional integration domain and contributes to the final estimate. To keep the computation finite while preserving unbiasedness, paths are
terminated probabilistically using Russian roulette [Pharr et al., 2016, Section 2.2.4]
2.2
Real-Time Rendering Techniques
Accurate simulation of light transport (Section 2.1) has traditionally been used in “offline” settings, such as movie production, due to the high computational cost. Applications targeting interactive or real-time performance, for example, visualization or video
games, instead rely on simplifying assumptions and approximations of light transport.
In these contexts, it is often sufficient to use techniques that produce images that appear
convincing to human observers, even if they are not physically accurate, yet are orders of
magnitude more efficient. Many of these approaches are shaped by the historical development of real-time rendering, which has traditionally relied on rasterization of triangle
meshes. Instead of tracing rays for individual pixels of the camera, rasterization takes
a primitive-centric perspective and efficiently determines pixel coverage by projecting
triangles to the image plane of the camera, a process that is highly optimized on modern
GPUs. As a result, numerous lighting and visibility approximations have been designed
specifically to integrate well with rasterization-based pipelines.
2.2.1
Point Light Sources
Physically accurate emitters are typically modeled as area light sources that emit radiance from a finite area in the scene, which belongs to the surface of some object. While
this representation closely follows the nature of real light sources, evaluating their contribution generally requires integrating incident radiance over the emitter area. Many
real-time systems approximate illumination using simplified models that assume light is
emitted from a single point in space1 . This greatly simplifies computations and is easy
to integrate into rasterization-based rendering.
A possible way of interpreting these non-physical light
sources in the context of light
transport (Section 2.1) is in terms
of Dirac distributions that colArea light
— Limit →

Figure 2.3: An object rendered using different types of point light sources. Directional
lights approximate infinitely distant light sources (e.g., the sun). Omnidirectional point
lights emit light in all directions and are commonly used to model small light sources
such as light bulbs. Spot lights emit light within a cone-shaped region and are often
used to model flashlights or stage lighting.
gles. Depending on how the emission is distributed over directions, this leads to different
types of light sources (see Fig. 2.3). A discussion of visibility is deferred to Section 2.2.2.
Directional Lights Directional lights are emitters located at a single point at infinity.
Hence, their light rays are parallel and illuminate the scene from a single direction 𝝎 𝑙 .
They are typically used to approximate the sun or other distant light sources whose
distance is large relative to the scale of the scene. The contribution of a directional light
with radiance 𝐸 to the outgoing radiance at a point p into direction 𝝎 is

Figure 2.4: Shadow mapping uses a depth map rendered from the perspective of the
light to determine if a point in the scene is visible to a point light source. If the depth
from a point in the scene to the light source is larger than the queried distance stored
in the shadow map, the point is in shadow. Because the shadow map is a raster image,
the visibility test suffers from self-shadowing artifacts (“shadow acne”). This can be
alleviated by biasing the depths towards the light source for the visibility test.
2.2.2
Shadow Mapping
In our discussion of point light sources (Section 2.2.1), we have so far neglected light
source visibility: if the path between a light source and a point in the scene is obstructed,
the point should be in shadow. In rasterization-based pipelines, light source visibility is
typically approximated using some variant of shadow mapping [Williams, 1978].
Shadow mapping is based on the observation that points in a scene are lit by a (point)
light source if they are closest to the light source. The shadow map is a depth image from
the perspective of the light source. If a point in the scene is further from the light than
the value stored in the shadow map, it is in the shadow. Otherwise, it has the value
stored in the shadow map, is closest to the light source, and is lit by it (Fig. 2.4)
Formally, let the scene be given as the set X ⊂ R3 . A point light source is defined by
its position x𝑙 . We assume a projective model and represent positions in homogeneous
coordinates, so x𝑙 can be at infinity, creating a directional light source, in which case x𝑙 is
the direction vector 𝝎 𝑙 (Section 2.2.1). Denote the distance of x ∈ R3 to the light source
as 𝑑 : R3 ↦→ [0 : 𝑑 max ], where the distance to a directional light source is measured from
a plane outside of X so that distance values are finite and non-negative.
The shadow map is parameterized
over the space of rays emanating from x𝑙 .
For simplicity we assume that the rays
from x𝑙 to any point in the scene can be
parameterized over a single plane and we
assume a rectangle U on this plane to be
the parameter space for the rays; if the
light source is placed so that this condition is violated, several planes (i.e., arranged
in the form of a box) may be used. The mapping from scene points x ∈ R3 to U is the
linear projective camera transformation 𝐿 : R3 ↦→ R2 . In this setup, the shadow map can
16
Chapter 2. Background
be expressed as
𝑓 : U ↦→ R,
𝑓 (u) = min {𝑑 (x) : 𝐿(x) = u, x ∈ X}
(2.17)
Given the shadow map, we get the visibility of any point in the scene with respect to the
light source as
{︄
0 𝑑 (x) > 𝑓 (𝐿(x))
𝑉 (x) =
(2.18)
1 else.
It is important to note that the “else” case really reduces to 𝑑 (x) = 𝑓 (𝐿(x)) because 𝑓
had been constructed to store the smallest possible value of 𝑓 in direction 𝐿(x).
The usefulness of shadow maps arises from the fact that they can be efficiently approximated using graphics hardware: simply render the scene from the perspective of
the light source, disabling all lighting computations and storing only the depth values.
However, a naive implementation will suffer from severe artifacts. For example, the condition for a point to be lit is 𝑑 (x) ≤ 𝑓 (𝐿(x)) in finite precision arithmetic, which may
or may not happen for visible points on a smooth surface because of the different discretization of the rendered image and the shadow map. The resulting problem is referred
to as “shadow acne" and is commonly tackled by introducing a bias 𝛽 for the depth comparison, considering points with 𝑑 (x) ≤ 𝑓 (𝐿(x)) + 𝛽 to be lit (Figure 2.4). However,
estimating a suitable bias can be difficult for some scenes [Dou et al., 2014]. While suitably chosen bias attenuates artifacts along smooth surfaces, it cannot remedy aliasing
across shadow boundaries. We will defer a discussion of how to mitigate shadow aliasing using “soft” shadows to Chapter 3, because the underlying visibility discontinuities
also pose a significant challenge for differentiation.

If the components of f are simple functions based on elementary operations, there
is a good chance that we can easily derive an expression for each element of the Jacobian by manually applying differentiation rules. However, the functions encountered
throughout this thesis are generally a more complex composition of individual functions:
f = g𝑑 ◦ · · · ◦ g1 .
(2.22)
While it is often infeasible to derive a single expression for the derivative of the composite f, one can typically derive expressions for the individual functions {g𝑖 }, such that
the Jacobian is obtained using the chain rule:
Jf = Jg𝑑 · . . . · Jg1 .
(2.23)
While tedious, manually differentiating functions and computing derivatives by explicitly evaluating the resulting expressions can have practical benefits in terms of computational efficiency, memory requirements, or other means [Kerbl et al., 2023; Müller
et al., 2021] (also see Chapter 4). Although it was shown that this approach to differentiation scales even to entire path tracing simulations [Li et al., 2018a], it is error-prone
and inflexible when system requirements change.
Automatic Differentiation
In many cases, one would much rather use automatic differentiation (AD). This not only
avoids error-prone and tedious derivations but also greatly improved accessibility in recent years: several modern libraries (e.g., PyTorch [Paszke et al., 2019], JAX [Bradbury
et al., 2018], or Dr.Jit [Jakob et al., 2022a]) expose automatic differentiation tools for
machine learning and other optimization tasks. Notice the distinction between automatic and symbolic differentiation: symbolic methods apply differentiation rules to the
mathematical expression of a function to obtain an expression for the derivative, while
automatic differentiation evaluates derivatives of a function implemented as a computer
program. The details of automatic differentiation have been covered by many textbooks
(e.g., by Griewank and Walther [2008]), so we only give a high-level overview here.
First, notice that the decomposition into functions (Eq. (2.22)) is generally not unique.
In the case of manual approaches it is often somewhat arbitrarily chosen to coincide with
the function definitions in the program code. A key principle of automatic differentiation
is decomposing functions at a much finer resolution so that each component of the {g𝑖 }
is a small atomic operation whose analytic derivatives are known (e.g., addition or the
sine). For example, take the scalar-valued, multi-variate function
𝑓 (𝑥 1, 𝑥 2 ) = sin(𝑥 1 ) + 𝑥 1𝑥 2 − 𝑥 2 .
(2.24)
18
Chapter 2. Background
A possible decomposition that follows the above principle would be
𝑓 (𝑥 1, 𝑥 2 ) = 𝑔3 (g2 (g1 (𝑥 1, 𝑥 2 ))),
(2.25)

The relationship between variables is better visualized by the computational graph:
It is important that this decomposition into atomic operations does not need to be carried out explicitly, but it naturally follows from the primitives used to write numerical
computer programs and the program control flow. The functions g1 , g2 , and 𝑔3 therefore
merely serve the mental model – knowing the computational graph suffices to calculate
the Jacobian according to Eq. (2.23).
However, computing with the full Jacobians is neither required in many applications,
nor is it feasible: (intermediate) functions in machine learning and inverse rendering can
map millions of parameters to millions of outputs, making this approach prohibitively
expensive. Instead, it is sufficient to compute a vector-Jacobian product or a Jacobianvector product (i.e., a directional derivative). In the following we will introduce specific
algorithms for carrying out these operations.
Reverse-mode Reverse-mode differentiation, commonly known as backpropagation,
computes the vector-Jacobian product v⊤ Jf with a vector v = (𝑣 1, . . . , 𝑣𝑚 ) ⊤ . It is particularly useful for differentiating functions with many input variables and few outputs, for
example, for computing the gradient of an objective function (Section 2.4): the partial
derivatives of a single output with respect to any number of inputs can be computed
simultaneously, with the same time complexity as the function evaluation.
Considering the decomposition of the Jacobian in Eq. (2.23), the idea is to evaluate
the product from left to right. The computations performed by this backward pass (or
alternatively adjoint pass) are in reverse order to the primal pass (i.e., the function evaluation), effectively traversing the computation graph from the outputs back to the inputs.
At a node with output 𝑧, the chain rule is applied locally to propagate the sensitivity

Generally, the adjoint of a single variable is the sum of the contributions from its dependent variables, all of which can be computed using the principle above:
A common approach to perform this automatically is recording the computational
graph with intermediate results in a data structure, the tape or Wengert list [Wengert,
1964], during the primal pass, and then traversing it in reverse order. Storing the computational graph with intermediate values can result in significant memory requirements
for complex programs. A possible remedy is “checkpointing”, where only a subset of
intermediate states are stored while others are recomputed. However, determining optimal checkpoint placements is generally non-trivial. As we will show in Chapter 4, it
can be beneficial in terms of runtime and memory requirements to resolve computations
at a coarser resolution locally and interweave manual differentiation with the automatic
traversal. Specialized algorithms tailored to specific problem structures can also improve
the efficiency (Section 2.4.3, Chapter 5 and Chapter 6).
Forward-mode Forward-mode differentiation computes the Jacobian-vector product
Jf v – the directional derivative of f in direction v = (𝑣 1, . . . , 𝑣𝑛 ) ⊤ . In contrast to reversemode, it is useful if a function has few input variables and many outputs: the partial
derivatives of any number of outputs with respect to a single input variable can be computed simultaneously, with the same time complexity as the function evaluation. In
this thesis, we are mostly interested in gradient-based optimization, and forward-mode
differentiation is primarily used for visualization and validation: when differentiating
rendering algorithms, it is common to show the effect of a single parameter (e.g., the
translation of an object along a given direction) on the output image – this can be efficiently computed using forward-mode.
Considering the decomposition of the Jacobian in Eq. (2.23), the general idea is to
evaluate the product from right to left. The computational graph is traversed forward,

One way to implement automatic forward-mode differentiation is through dual numbers: each variable 𝑧 tracks its value and its directional derivative 𝑧̇ . This requires no
computational graph and can be carried out alongside the primal computation.
Finite Differences
Finite differences (FD) are a common numerical method for approximating directional
derivatives. The general idea is to estimate derivatives by evaluating the function with
slightly perturbed inputs. For example, the derivative in direction v can be approximated
using central differences:
f (x + ℎv) − f (x − ℎv)
(2.34)
2ℎ
where ℎ is a small step size that controls the amount of perturbation. While finite differences are easy to implement because they do not require knowledge about the internals
of a function, they come with several limitations. For one, the choice of step size ℎ requires an intricate balance: if ℎ is too small, floating point rounding errors dominate the
result due to catastrophic cancellation, but if ℎ is too large, the approximation is inaccurate. Moreover, approximating gradients of scalar-valued functions with many inputs
is prohibitively expensive because the derivative for each input would require at least
one2 function evaluation. In this thesis, finite differences will primarily serve as a tool
for validating derivatives computed using automatic or manual differentiation.

Differentiable Rendering
The tools developed in this thesis are primarily aimed at solving optimization problems
with an objective, or loss, function
(︁
)︁
L 𝑅(𝜽 ) ∈ R,
(2.35)
where 𝑅(𝜽 ) generates an image, or another form of measurement, by rendering a virtual
scene. Typically, the scene parameters 𝜽 ∈ R𝑛 characterize the virtual scene by defining
cameras, light sources, materials, and geometry (see Section 2.1.1), yet, despite the name,
they may include any parameters that affect the rendering output (e.g., parameters of
the rendering algorithm). Most often, L computes the difference between the rendering
output and some target – as done for inverse rendering problems – but generally it can
be any problem-specific, scalar-valued function, which may also take multiple images
or measurements generated through rendering (see Section 2.4.1).
We are interested in finding parameters 𝜽 ★ that minimize the objective:
(︁
)︁
𝜽 ★ = arg min L 𝑅(𝜽 ) .
(2.36)
𝜽 ∈R𝑛
Generally, the parameter space is high-dimensional, so an exhaustive search is infeasible.
Additionally, one cannot hope to directly solve for 𝜽 ★ due to the complexity of 𝑅(𝜽 ).
Instead, these problems are commonly addressed using gradient descent: starting from
some initial state 𝜽 0 , perform iterative updates of the form
𝜽 𝑘+1 ← 𝜽 𝑘 − 𝜆

where 𝜆 is a sufficiently small step size. Although it is difficult to give exact convergence guarantees for arbitrary functions L, gradient-based methods have been shown
to reliably converge to (local) minima in practice.
If the rendering output is an image, i.e., 𝑅(𝜽 ) = I(𝜽 ), as will be the case for most
parts of this thesis, then from the chain rule, the gradient follows to be

The particular challenge is differentiating the image I with respect to scene parameters
𝜽 , which means quantifying how the scene parameters affect the image.
Differentiable rendering 3 is concerned with computing such derivatives, i.e., differentiating the rendering output with respect to the parameters 𝜽 . More specifically, since
problems usually involve millions of parameters (e.g., textures or vertex positions), the
goal is to compute gradients in reverse-mode (see Section 2.3, Reverse-mode). Although
it is possible to use automatic differentiation (see Section 2.3, Automatic Differentiation)
to differentiate the implementation of a rendering algorithm, this requires some care:
(1) automatic derivatives of numerical computations do not necessarily correspond to
the actual derivatives of the underlying mathematical expression (Section 2.4.2); and (2)
3 We refer interested readers to the survey by Kato et al. [2020] for a general introduction to the topic.
22
Chapter 2. Background
the more complex 𝑅(𝜽 ) becomes, the larger the computational graph stored for (tapebased) automatic differentiation. The potentially prohibitive memory requirements can
be avoided using specialized differentiation algorithms (Section 2.4.3). Without loss of
generality, we will often just consider a single scene parameter 𝜃 throughout the thesis.
2.4.1
Applications
The applications of differentiable rendering are too numerous to cover exhaustively. Instead, each of the main chapters will address the relevant background separately, so we
will only briefly discuss the general context here. Broadly speaking, the applications in
this thesis fall somewhere between computer graphics and 3D computer vision, many of
which have the same general structure: given a reference image Iref (or measurement),
find a representation of the environment that gives rise to this observation.
With some rendering algorithm 𝑅(𝜽 ) at hand, this task may be viewed as inverse
rendering, i.e., inverting the rendering process to obtain parameters for a virtual scene as
a representation of the environment. However, 𝑅 is generally not invertible, so instead,
this is cast as a minimization problem of the form
𝜽 ★ = arg min ∥𝑅(𝜽 ) − Iref ∥ 22 .
(2.39)
𝜽 ∈R𝑛
Notice that this is a special case of Eq. (2.36), and the objective could really be any function that measures the difference between the rendering output and the reference (including neural network-based objectives [Johnson et al., 2016]). This approach to the
above task is also known as analysis-by-synthesis.
It naturally generalizes to multiple references, and a recurring application in that
regard will be multi-view 3D reconstruction: given multiple photographs of a scene
(typically with known or estimated camera parameters), recover the scene geometry.
With known camera parameters, one can render images from the reference perspectives,
compare the rendered images to the reference photographs, and update a representation
of the scene geometry (e.g., a triangle mesh) using gradient descent (also see Chapter 7).
In general, it is common for some of the scene parameters to be fixed (e.g., known or
estimated beforehand), and only a subset is optimized.
Apart from inverse applications, we will occasionally cover others that are not based
on matching a given reference. For example, in Chapter 6, scene geometry is optimized
to improve certain acoustic characteristics of a room (in that context, 𝑅(𝜽 ) generates an
acoustic measurement from the scene).
Unfortunately, in most of the applications, we cannot hope to find a global minimum because the objectives are generally non-convex. Even worse, many of the local
minima found using differentiable rendering are undesirable: for example, if the task
is to reconstruct the surface of an object from real-world images, the renderings might
agree with the reference images, but the recovered surface is physically implausible. The
application-side drives a significant part of the research on better-suited objectives and
robust optimization techniques (e.g., for the evolution of geometry [Luan et al., 2021;
Nicolet et al., 2021])
Differentiable rendering comes in many flavors, from general differentiable renderers to application-specific systems. For the most part of the thesis, we will be concerned
2.4. Differentiable Rendering
23
with general, application-independent techniques, but we will always relate back to applications by evaluating our methods and algorithms in respective settings.
2.4.2
Discontinuities and Visibility
There is often a distinction made between physically-based differentiable renderers based
on ray tracing with Monte Carlo methods (Section 2.1) [Nimier-David et al., 2019; Jakob
et al., 2022b; Yan et al., 2020; Li et al., 2018b], and rasterization-based differentiable renderers with approximations (Section 2.2) [Ravi et al., 2020; Fuji Tsang et al., 2022; Laine
et al., 2020; de La Gorce et al., 2008]. Regardless of whether one is interested in one or
the other, a major challenge shared by both are parameter-dependent discontinuities 4 .
Discontinuities arise because several computations within a renderer involve discrete
decisions. For example, a triangle either covers a point in the image or it does not, and
a point in the scene is either visible to a point light source, or it is not. Special care
is required when differentiating with respect to parameters that affect these decisions.
Unfortunately, this case is common: many interesting applications involve optimization
of geometry, for example, the vertices of a triangle mesh for 3D reconstruction – and the
vertex positions directly affect occlusion relations in the scene.
Example: Primary Visibility To demonstrate why parametric discontinuities are
problematic in differentiable rendering, we will use the case of primary visibility, in
particular, the visibility of a single triangle to a pinhole camera. While in general multiple
primitives may occlude each other, visibility in this simple case is entirely determined by
coverage: a point in the image plane of the camera is either covered by the triangle, or it
is not. Let 𝜃 ∈ R be an offset to the triangle vertices leading to a horizontal translation
in the image plane. The triangle visibility over the continuous image plane then is:
{︄
1 (𝑥, 𝑦) ∈ T (𝜃 )
(2.40)

0 else,

constant with discontinuities, the resulting scene function has the same discontinuities.

the points of discontinuity, i.e., the silhouette points of the triangle, “move” over the image plane with 𝜃 .

Rasterization Rasterization effectively point-samples the scene function at discrete

u)︁i = (𝑥𝑖 , 𝑦𝑖 ) in the image plane, each being a pixel in the output image
(︁

of 𝐼 (𝑥, 𝑦, 𝜃 ) for visibility changes by 𝜃 . In the above example with constant colors, this
results in pixel derivatives 𝜕𝜃 𝐼 (u𝑖 , 𝜃 ) that are either zero or undefined, which makes it
impossible to optimize the triangle position.
Various works have tackled this limitation for differentiable rasterization [de La Gorce
et al., 2008; Kato et al., 2018; Liu et al., 2019a; Chen et al., 2019; Laine et al., 2020; Petersen
et al., 2022; Cole et al., 2021; Pidhorskyi et al., 2025]. Common strategies include blending multiple triangles with soft visibility per pixel [Liu et al., 2019a; Chen et al., 2019;
Petersen et al., 2022] or “reattaching” the value of point samples to the underlying geometry after rasterization [Laine et al., 2020] (see Fig. 2.5). While several techniques
are only approximations, in practice, this often suffices to solve optimization problems
involving visibility.

assume that each pixel value effectively is a point-sample of an integrated scene function

For the above primary visibility example, the interior term is zero because 𝜕𝜃 𝑓 is zero on
I \ 𝜕I (𝜃 ), so automatic differentiation simply produces zero derivatives for the image.
Instead, one would need to explicitly compute the boundary term, which, when done
using Monte Carlo estimation, means sampling the discontinuities. In the simplified
setting above, this corresponds to integrating over the boundary of the triangle T (𝜃 )
in image space. In general scenes, however, this is significantly more difficult because
discontinuities arise at silhouettes, which are determined by the occlusion relation of
primitives. Even worse, this affects not only primary visibility but also the integrals
appearing in the rendering equation (2.4).
2.4. Differentiable Rendering
27
A significant part of the research on physically-based differentiable rendering is
therefore concerned with efficiently handling visibility discontinuities. Directly estimating the boundary term by sampling discontinuities has been investigated for unidirectional path tracing [Li et al., 2018a; Soroka et al., 2025] and the path integral form [Zhang
et al., 2020, 2023]. A fundamental observation, also discussed in Chapter 5 and Chapter 6,
is that the “parameter-dependence” of discontinuities depends on the parameterization
of the integration domain. Reparameterization techniques attempt to construct parameterizations where discontinuities are static [Loubet et al., 2019; Bangaru et al., 2020; Xu
et al., 2023; Wu et al., 2025], which effectively moves all contributions into the interior
term. The same approach works for various surface representations, for example, signed
distance functions [Bangaru et al., 2022; Vicini et al., 2022]. For a more comprehensive
and recent overview of the topic, interested readers are referred to the survey by Zeng
et al. [2025a].
2.4.3
Path Replay Backpropagation
When the task is to differentiate physically-based renderers (Section 2.1), a natural choice
would be automatic differentiation (Section 2.3) because even if parameter-dependent
discontinuities are present, automatic differentiation can be used to compute the interior term of the derivative (Section 2.4.2). However, tape-based automatic differentiation
requires immense amounts of memory to store the computational graph of complex light
transport simulations. In practice, this limits its applicability to simple scenes, short light
paths, and low sample counts.
Nimier-David et al. [2020] and Stam [2020] showed that the derivatives of light transport simulations can be computed using another form of transport simulation. Building
on this idea, Vicini et al. [2021] proposed path replay backpropagation (PRB), a (reversemode) differentiation algorithm that computes derivatives using constant memory and
linear time in path length. This approach makes it possible to differentiate complex
simulations without storing large computational graphs spanning the full computation.
Path replay backpropagation is an important component in Chapter 6, where we
extend the same algorithm to time-resolved energy transport. Our derivation is based on
a recursive formulation introduced in the following. Besides supporting the derivation
in Chapter 6, we believe it also provides another helpful perspective on the algorithm.
Preliminaries Assume without loss of generality that we want to differentiate an image with respect to a single parameter 𝜃 : One has to differentiate the radiance measured
at the sensor, which in turn leads to differentiating the equations defining light transport
(Section 2.1.2):
∫
𝜕𝜃 𝐿o = 𝜕𝜃 𝐿e +

(2.48)
where it is assumed, for now, that the integrand is continuous in 𝜃 .
The first part of the equation is similar to the standard rendering equation, but instead of radiance, differential radiance is collected. The second term inside the integral
describes how 𝜃 affects the BSDF and therefore the contribution of all remaining incoming radiance to the derivative. Nimier-David et al. [2020] observe that, if one can
28
Chapter 2. Background
obtain an estimate of 𝐿i (all remaining incoming radiance), at each point, the derivative
can be computed similar to the radiance in forward rendering. They approximate 𝐿i
by a nested Monte Carlo simulation, leading to quadratic complexity, as two rays are
spawned at every hit: one for collecting further differential radiance and one for the
remaining incoming radiance. Path replay backpropagation [Vicini et al., 2021] avoids
the nesting by computing 𝐿i from the total incoming radiance, the radiance collected at
the sensor, which can be precomputed in the forward simulation. In the following, we
derive formulas for calculating 𝐿i that capture the core idea.
Recursive Formula for the Total Incoming
Radiance The total incoming radiance is usually approximated using Monte Carlo integration:
given a simulation depth 𝑁 , draw 𝑁 direction samples (𝝎 i1 , . . . , 𝝎 i𝑁 ) and trace the path consisting of
multiple surface interactions (bounces). At each
bounce the directions of the incoming and outgoing rays are known, so the emission 𝐿e𝑘 and BSDF
value 𝑓𝑘 can be computed (see inset).
The contribution of this sampled path to the total radiance is given by (division by the probability density function (pdf) omitted)

(for 𝛽𝑘 and 𝐿𝑘 ), independent of depth, simply by looping over all bounces.

Non-Static Scenes The static scene assumption underlying this derivation has two
implications: first, the integrand is continuous in 𝜃 ; second, the position of each intersection point on a light path is independent of 𝜃 . When considering non-static scenes,
discontinuities in the integrand can be handled using numerous existing techniques (Section 2.4.2). However, as we will show in Chapter 5, the second implication, i.e., path
geometry moving in 𝜃 , leads to an additional, non-local term in the recursive formula
for the derivatives. This term is not handled by existing implementations, such that, at
the time of writing, no implementation simultaneously computes unbiased derivatives
with (P)RB and handles discontinuous integrands. We will introduce an implementation
that combines PRB with discontinuity handling in Chapter 5.

Chapter 2. Background
Chapter 3
Differentiable Shadow Mapping
Rendering
Numerical Derivatives
Our Derivatives
Figure 3.1: Complex scene (330k triangles) rendered in real time with differentiable
shadow mapping. It shows self-shadowing of objects, shadowing between objects, and
colored surfaces. Finite differences and our automatic derivatives with respect to movement of the light (note the non-zero derivatives at shadow boundaries).
Many inverse rendering applications are concerned with recovering scene information
solely from images captured in the real world. If this is to be solved using differentiable
rendering, one seeks a renderer that can reproduce the various effects of physical light
transport, as the goal is to match real-world images with renderings of a virtual scene.
A natural choice are physically-based differentiable renderers [Li et al., 2018a; NimierDavid et al., 2019; Zhang et al., 2020; Jakob et al., 2022b] as they adhere to the equations
of light transport (Section 2.1). However, as in forward rendering, the accuracy of these
sophisticated systems comes at the price of efficiency.
The full picture shows that inverse rendering and other applications require differentiable renderers that strike a good balance between accuracy and efficiency, for example,
when the renderer is only one of many components in a larger machine learning pipeline
[Gao et al., 2022; Lattas et al., 2022]. Unsurprisingly, many techniques from real-time
rendering have found their way into differentiable rendering, as highly realistic images
can be efficiently generated by approximating light transport.
Despite the advances of modern real-time systems, and the potential to leverage additional information in optimizations, surprisingly little effort has been spent on approximating global effects like shadows or indirect illumination in differentiable rendering:
The predominant approach for scenes with triangle meshes is differentiable rasterization
with purely local shading models [Loper and Black, 2014; Chen et al., 2019, 2021]. While
32
Chapter 3. Differentiable Shadow Mapping
this might lead to acceptable outcomes in some applications, ignoring effects like shadows in inverse settings can easily degrade solutions [Munkberg et al., 2022] and is generally only valid in very restrictive setups (e.g., with co-located camera and light [Zhang
et al., 2022; Luan et al., 2021]).
In this chapter, we show how shadows can be efficiently generated in differentiable
rendering of triangle meshes (Fig. 3.1). Our central observation is that one of the oldest
and most widely used approximations, shadow maps [Williams, 1978] (Section 2.2.2), can
be adapted to work in differentiable rendering frameworks. Our main idea is similar to
the original one: exploit efficient rasterization from the light’s point
of view. For differentiable shadows, this means that existing differentiable rasterizers handle discontinuities of primary visibility along
primitive borders; we use this machinery to handle discontinuities of
secondary visibility along shadow borders. In contrast to similar work
Primary
[Lyu et al., 2021; Hasselgren et al., 2022; Gao et al., 2025], our approach Discontinuities
(1) computes shadows directly from the triangle mesh instead of proxy
geometry, (2) differentiates visibility with respect to movement of geometry, and (3) supports high-frequency shadows from point light
sources. We demonstrate at several inverse graphics problems that
differentiable shadow mapping is orders of magnitude faster than dif- Secondary
ferentiable ray tracing, with similar accuracy – while differentiable Discontinuities
rasterization without shadows often fails to converge.
3.1
Method
Given a point light source located at position x𝑙 (possibly at infinity), our goal is to compute and differentiate the shadows it casts. Shadows are fully characterized by visibility:
if the path between the light source and a point x ∈ R3 in the scene is obstructed, the
point is in shadow. Since visibility is entirely geometric, it only depends on the location
of x and the scene geometry, which includes the pose of the light source:
dependency on scene geometry
↓
Visibility 𝑉 (x, 𝜃 )
(3.1)
We use the scene parameter 𝜃 from Section 2.4 to highlight this dependency and the
desire to differentiate visibility not only with respect to x but also geometry.
The main difficulty with light visibility is its piecewiese constant nature: it is either
zero or one, so the derivative is zero almost everywhere, both for x and 𝜃 – the shape
of shadows cast by a point light provides no additional cues to an inverse rendering
optimization. Importantly, this is an artifact of (non-physical) point lights as the Dirac
distribution collapses the integral that yields continuous visibility for (physical) area
lights (see Section 2.2.1). Before we present our solution (Section 3.1.3), we first investigate the challenges with differentiating shadow mapping more closely (Section 3.1.1),
and introduce the general technique underlying our approach (Section 3.1.2).
3.1. Method

l
3.1.2
An Inspiration from Real-Time Rendering
Smoothing (hard) shadow boundaries is commonly encountered in traditional real-time
rendering as a way to (1) increase the realism, since most real-world shadows have some
amount of softness, and (2) remedy aliasing at shadow boundaries resulting from the
discretization of the shadow map (see [Hasenfratz et al., 2003] for an overview). While
our goal is the same, the motivation is different: in differentiable rendering, smoothing
is not optional, but a necessity for non-zero spatial and geometric shadow derivatives.
Regardless, our idea is to reuse the same machinery.
1 Remember that 𝑑 (x, 𝜃 ) is the distance and 𝐿(x, 𝜃 ) is the direction from the light to x
2 Some concurrent, sigmoid-based constructions [Karunratanakul et al., 2023; Wang et al., 2023a] also
consider a smoothing of shadow boundaries, but their derivatives are incorrect and have the same limitations as non-smoothed versions (see Section 3.2).

as approximations of percentage

shadow map and return the percentage of the rays to which x is visible. In a slight generalization of the original formulation, we assume the neighborhood to∫ be implicitly defined by a continuous (compactly supported) kernel 𝑘𝐿(x) (u) satisfying 𝑘𝐿(x) (u) du = 1.

The visibility function is taking on values in [0, 1], and, in particular, the boundaries of
shadows are smoothly transitioning between the two extremes.
Derivatives of Percentage Closer Filtering. Despite being based on a binary test,
differentiating the visibility function of percentage closer filtering yields the expected
shadow derivatives. However, this must be done with care: simply applying automatic
differentiation to the discretized integral (e.g., as in Karunratanakul et al. [2023]) misses
the derivatives for shadow boundaries. Instead, the integral must be differentiated in its
continuous form – one of the reasons we have not yet limited 𝑓 to be a raster image. This
is because the boundary of the integration domain 𝑑 (x, 𝜃 ) ≤ 𝑓 (u, 𝜃 ) depends on x and 𝜃 :
when differentiating visibility with respect to either parameter, one must consider their
effect on the integration domain, which involves an integral over its boundary [Reynolds
et al., 1903]. Automatically differentiating the discretized integral, i.e., “Discretize-thenDifferentiate” [Bangaru et al., 2021], is known to miss these boundary derivatives.
Conceptually, the problem is similar
Light
to visibility handling in differentiable rendering (Section 2.4.2), but with a key difx
ference: while the integration domain

can include (subsets of) object silhouettes, but generally they are curves on the objects.
Even if one could integrate over these boundaries, percentage closer filtering is costly:
not only the integral but also its derivative need to be evaluated for every point x visible
to the camera. One would much rather pre-filter the shadow map instead of the comparison to 𝑑 (x). Yet, simply convolving 𝑓 with 𝑘 and then evaluating the smoothed shadow
map is different – the result would take only the discrete values 0 and 1.
3.1.3
Differentiable Pre-Filtered Shadow Mapping
A practical solution to differentiable shadow mapping would generate soft shadow boundaries such that visibility can be differentiated (Section 3.1.1), but, at the same time, does
not involve an integral for every point x that is costly to compute and even more costly
to differentiate. While percentage closer filtering [Reeves et al., 1987] does not meet the

Then, an approximation to Eq. (3.4) is built from the set of pre-computed functions:
)︁ (︁
)︁
)︁
(︁
(︁
𝑉PCF (x) ≈ 𝑉Approx x, 𝑓1 𝐿(x) , 𝑓2 𝐿(x) , . . . .
(3.6)
This structure is so useful in forward rendering because the integral is independent of
x, allowing it to be evaluated using hardware texture filtering; and 𝑉Approx is typically
a simple function. The structure is equally advantageous in differentiable rendering:
under the mild assumption that 𝑘 u and 𝑔𝑖 are continuous, the only discontinuities in the
integrand relevant for differentiation are those of the shadow map 𝑓 at object silhouettes.
This effectively reduces the problem to visibility handling (Section 2.4.2).
Discretize and Smooth with Differentiable Rasterizers
Like the shadow map 𝑓 , the pre-filtered maps 𝑓𝑖 are not evaluated at query-time but
precomputed by rendering from the perspective of the light. In practice, this means the
functions 𝑓𝑖 will be approximated by discrete versions f𝑖 . The main challenge is to ensure
that the derivatives 𝜕𝜃 f𝑖 account for changes of geometry, including object silhouettes.
Similar in spirit to the original shadow map idea [Williams, 1978], we can use existing
rasterizers. While the original motivation was efficiency, and this is also true in our
case, a central point is that significant effort has been spent on visibility handling when
rendering from the perspective of the viewer [Laine et al., 2020; Liu et al., 2019a; Loper
and Black, 2014; Kato et al., 2018; Chen et al., 2019, 2021; de La Gorce et al., 2008]. We
can readily exploit any existing solution and render from the perspective of the light.
This works because the output of differentiable rasterizers is, effectively, a smooth
point-sampled version of the function in screen space: the output values vary continuously with the scene geometry. This means, f𝑖 can be generated by rendering 𝑔𝑖 (𝑓 ) with
a differentiable rasterizer, optionally followed by a (discrete) convolution with any filter
kernel. Since pre-filtered shadow mapping requires smoothing to attenuate aliasing, the
smoothing by differentiable rasterizers is complementary in this setting.
Variance Shadow Maps
While our approach works with any pre-filtering technique, we demonstrate it at the example of variance shadow maps (VSM) [Donnelly and Lauritzen, 2006]. The approximation is built by taking the view that the local shadow map may be considered a probability
distribution. This makes sense because it is irrelevant what points are in the integration
domain in Eq. (3.4) as long as the measure of the integration domain is correct.
Pre-filtering is used to generate the first and second moments of the local distribution

First, notice that the elements in 𝑉VSM depending on the shadow map 𝑓 are 𝜇x and 𝜎x2 .

3.2
Implementation and Parameter Exploration
Our reference implementation is in Python3 and builds on Nvdiffrast, the differentiable
rasterization primitives by Laine et al. [2020], for rendering and smoothing the shadow
map. The derivatives 𝜕𝜃 f̃ and 𝜕𝜃 f˜2 (see Section 3.1.3) are not explicitly computed. Rather,
we use reverse-mode differentiation to obtain the gradients of an objective function. The
relevant derivatives are computed automatically [Paszke et al., 2019]. The implementation currently supports directional lights and spot lights (a single shadow map per
light), and has experimental support for omnidirectional point lights (six shadow maps
per light). Area lights or environment lighting would have to be approximated by point
light sources – we leave a more accurate treatment for future work. Fig. 3.2 illustrates
the light sources supported in our current implementation. For all experiments – except
those explicitly stating otherwise – we use a shadow map resolution of 256 × 256.
3 An
open-source implementation
differentiable-shadow-mapping
is
available
at
https://github.com/mworchel/
38
Chapter 3. Differentiable Shadow Mapping
By building on an existing differentiable rasterizer and automatic differentiation, our
code becomes rather straightforward. The pseudo-code below is provided for a single
light source – keep in mind that the methods have to be executed for each light. The
pre-computation of the pre-filtered depth images m1 and m2 is described in Alg. 1 below.
Here, we assume that the RenderScene function returns the smoothed discrete depth
image f̃ as well as f˜2 . The modular design of Nvdiffrast implements this in two steps,
first providing the discrete values f and f 2 = f ⊗ f, which are then smoothed using the
antialias function. We implement the filtering as a convolution with a kernel 𝑘 of
configurable size (e.g. a Gaussian). Once the discrete smoothed shadow maps have been
computed, the visibility for a point x ∈ X can be computed with Alg. 2.
Algorithm 1 Render a variance shadow map for a light source
Inputs: scene geometry X, light source position x𝑙 , filter kernel 𝑘
Output: a variance shadow map (m1 , m2 )
function RenderShadowMap(X, x𝑙 , 𝑘)
f̃, f˜2 ← RenderScene(X, x𝑙 )
m1 ← Filter(f̃, 𝑘)
m2 ← Filter(f˜2, 𝑘)
return m1 , m2
Algorithm 2 Compute the visibility using variance shadow mapping
Inputs: a point in space x, light source position x𝑙 , variance shadow map (m1 , m2 )
Output: the visibility 𝑉VSM (x)
function ComputeVisibility(x, x𝑙 , m1 , m2 )
u ← 𝐿(x, x𝑙 )
𝑚 1 ← SampleBilinear(m1, u)
𝑚 2 ← SampleBilinear(m2, u)
𝑑 ← Distance(x, x𝑙 ), 𝜇 ← 𝑚 1, 𝜎 2 ← 𝑚 2 − 𝑚 21
if 𝑑 ≤ 𝜇 then return 1
else return 𝜎 2 /(𝜎 2 + (𝑑 − 𝜇) 2 )
Optimization without Smoothed Shadow Map
We test our hypothesis that the smoothing of the differentiable rasterizer, giving f̃ and
f˜2 , is essential for geometry derivatives. This is facilitated by the modularity of Nvdiffrast that allows disabling the smoothing, thus working only with the non-smoothed
but point-sampled versions f and f 2 .
Our setup is as simple as possible: a single square occluder casts a single shadow on a
single square receiver. We optimize the position of one displaced vertex of the occluder
to reproduce the shadow in a reference configuration (see Fig. 3.3). The optimization
minimizes the mean squared error to the reference shadow image.
We find that the target configuration is not attained when using the discontinuous discrete shadow map for generating the visibility (i.e., without using Nvdiffrast’s
antialias function). Importantly, this result is independent of the kernel 𝑘 used for

pre-filtering to generate the moments m1 and m2 . In other words, filtering f and f 2 cannot recover the connection between the scene parameters and the shadow boundaries.
Using the smooth functions (i.e., applying antialias to f and f 2 ) shows the expected
convergence across variations of all other parameters.
Visibility Derivatives in Concurrent Work
Objective
Overview (Opt.)
In concurrent work, Wang et al. [2023a] (“SunStage”) and Karunratanakul et al. [2023]
(“HARP”) propose differentiable shadow mapping for facial capture and hand reconstruction, respectively. Both constructions use a sigmoid-based visibility test, giving
as a reason that this ensures “smooth gradients”, and Karunratanakul et al. [2023] explicitly note that the shadow mapping implementation is differentiable with respect to
geometry. However, as we have discussed in Section 3.1.1, a sigmoid test amounts to radially smoothing visibility: the resulting visibility remains discontinuous across shadow
boundaries, and the derivatives with respect to scene geometry do not reflect the variation of the shadow outline. Interestingly, in their implementation, both methods also use
another form of smoothing, which, visually,

leads to soft shadow boundaries: Karunratanakul et al. [2023] use percentage closer
filtering [Reeves et al., 1987] and Wang et al.
[2023a] spatially filter visibility in the image

be difficult to correctly differentiate either approach: for percentage closer filtering, one
would need to sample boundaries that depend on the evaluation point (see Section 3.1.2),
and for image-space filtering, one would need to sample arbitrary cast shadow boundaries in image space, which appears similarly challenging.
Shadow Map Resolution and Filter Kernel
The shadow map resolution and the filter kernel size are key parameters as they directly
affect the quality of the shadow approximation (see Fig. 3.4). Instead of investigating
the forward pass of shadow map rendering, we focus on the backward pass, because it
influences the gradient computation and any optimization based on it.
Consider a scene similar to the one above, now with a highly tessellated and slightly
rotated occluder. High tessellation (relative to shadow map pixels) results in sparse
shadow map derivatives for occluder geometry because the anti-aliasing misses primitive boundaries. This might occur for other differentiable rasterizers relying on explicit
boundary detection. The filter applied to recover the moments (Eq. (3.11)) is also ap-

visibility function (see Fig. 3.5). While the filter kernel size is merely a visual effect in
the forward pass, it can indeed affect the optimization: Fig. 3.6 shows that larger filter
kernels increase the robustness of gradient descent, both for stochastic gradient descent
and momentum-based variants. However, larger kernels also increase the runtime.
Low shadow map resolution may also lead to occluders being missed completely by
the visibility test (see Fig. 3.4, top row). These issues can be alleviated by increasing the
shadow map resolution; again at the cost of runtime.
In the following, we consider a more complex setting to test different shadow map
resolutions and filter kernel sizes: an object is placed on a rectangular floor plane and
illuminated by 𝑛 directional lights (Fig. 3.7). Given a single reference image 𝐼 ref , we wish
to recover the 𝑛 light directions {l𝑖 }, minimizing the mean squared image error.
We run the optimization for different objects and varying number of lights 𝑛. For
each configuration of filter size or shadow map resolution, we repeat the optimization
150 times with randomized target and initial conditions (the randomization is deterministic, so all configurations use same set of conditions), and average the results. We report
qualitative results in Fig. 3.7 and quantitative results in Fig. 3.8. As expected, increasing
the shadow map resolution or the filter kernel size increases the runtime. The optimization behaves robustly with high accuracy and consistent error bounds across resolutions
and filter sizes. Again, the accuracy improves slightly with larger filter size, albeit this
