stays constant. We observe that the optimization for one light can get stuck in local
minima where the shadows do not align but the shade of the floor plane matches that in
the reference image. For multiple lights, shadows dominate the image, so this scenario is
less likely; this could explain the different error bounds. Conversely, recovering multiple
lights is more difficult, hence the lower average accuracy. These challenges are specific
to the task and, in general, we observe that our method works robustly with different
sets of parameters.
3.3
Applications
We run all experiments on a workstation with an NVIDIA 1070 Ti GPU with 8 GB VRAM.
3.3.1
Monocular Pose Estimation
Estimating the orientation and position of an object from images is a classic computer
vision task. The problem becomes easier for sequences of images [Lepetit and Fua, 2005]
or if priors apply, such as in pose estimation of human body parts [Sarafianos et al.,
2016; de La Gorce et al., 2008]. Given only a single (monocular) image makes retrieving
the rigid transformation of an known object ill-posed. Shafer and Kanade [Shafer and
Kanade, 1983] noticed that the shadow of an object in the image provides viable cues for
reducing the ambiguity. We consider a simplified version of the 6D problem, allowing
only 2 translational and one rotational degree of freedom. We place an object in front of a
receiver plane and a directional light orthogonal to the plane. In this setting, we recover

(bottom row).

We report quantitative results in Table 3.1 and qualitative results in Fig. 3.9. Mitsuba 3 recovers the rotation and translation most accurately, which could be attributed to
its accurate gradient computation, using multiple sub-pixel samples. However, it should
be expected that the results are biased towards the method used for generating the reference images. We show that this is the case by using our renderer as a reference for
one experiment (see Table 3.1) and finding our method to yield the most accurate results
in this case. Across all experiments, not using shadows at all and relying purely on the
shading of the object surface performs worst, yet still manages to converge to reasonable
solutions in some instances.
Unsurprisingly, rasterization is magnitudes faster than path tracing, both with and
without shadows. Shadow mapping roughly doubles the runtime in rasterization, however, the pose estimation accuracy is significantly increased: our method consistently
yields accuracy similar or close to path tracing. Modern GPUs are equipped with hardware units that significantly increase ray tracing performance. We include scores for a
system with such a GPU (NVIDIA RTX 3080 Ti). It improves the path tracing runtime

3.3.2
Face Reconstruction from Profile Shadows
While the variation within certain classes of objects is governed by pose, such as in articulated objects, deformations are best described by morphable models. A prominent
example for morphable models is face geometry [Egger et al., 2020]. Our idea is to reconstruct face geometry from one (or more) shadows of the face. We base the experiment
on the publicly available morphable model assembled by Li et al. [2020] (other models
would work as well).
The first experiment uses synthetic reference shadows that are generated by rendering a known state of the morphable model. This allows comparing the optimization
result not just based on images but also geometry (see Fig. 3.10). We find that using more
shadows only slightly increases the runtime (the time is not linear in the number of light
sources, because we have one base pass and multiple shadow passes) but significantly
improves the accuracy (see Table 3.2).
We have also performed reconstructions of face geometry from “real” silhouettes,
created manually by artist Charles Burns using scissors and black paper [Burns, 2011]
(the process is shown here [Alive Network, 2016]). We ignore regions appearing to be

3.3.3
Shadow Art
Shadow Art [Mitra and Pauly, 2009; Hsiao et al., 2018] is the design of a 3D object based
only on the shadows it casts. When solving this design problem computationally, it is
commonly simplified by assuming that the shadows are cast to planar surfaces orthogonal to directional lights, reducing the shadow images to silhouettes. In the traditional approach [Mitra and Pauly, 2009], the object is represented on a volumetric grid and voxel
lines outside the shadow regions are removed – similar in spirit to visual hulls [Laurentini, 1994] or space carving [Kutulakos and Seitz, 2000]. Sadekar et al. [2022] perform
46

co-located light and camera (left), for more complex settings with perspective cameras
that observe the shadow receiver from any direction (middle), and for complex receiver
geometry (right).
3.4. Conclusion
47
Ini�al
Reference Optimized Mesh
(Vertex Positions)
Optimized Objects
Optimized Objects
(Position + Scale) (Position + Scale + Rotation)
Figure 3.13: Shadow art with two views. Our method can be used in any setting compatible with differentiable rendering, for example optimizing vertex positions of a mesh
(left) or optimizing the parameters of multiple geometric objects (middle and right).
Shadow Art with differentiable rendering of meshes, but the limitation of co-located
cameras and lights remains.
In our framework, lights and cameras can be placed arbitrarily. Also, curved shadow
receivers work out-of-the-box (Fig. 3.12). The only (minor) constraints we impose are
gradient pre-conditioning [Nicolet et al., 2021] and a smoothness term between the normals of neighboring faces [Luan et al., 2021] (Chapter 7) to avoid arbitrary vertex movements during optimization.
A single triangle mesh is only one possible representation of the generated geometry.
The combination of automatic differentiation and gradient descent allows optimizing
arbitrary scene parameters. For example, we can also represent the object as a collection
of shapes (see Fig. 3.13). Beyond our reference implementation based on triangle meshes,
the same approach readily works with any geometry representation as long as it can be
differentiably rendered (e.g., signed distance functions [Vicini et al., 2022; Bangaru et al.,
2022] or spheres [Lassner and Zollhöfer, 2021]).
3.3.4
Interactive Modeling from Shadows
Our rasterization-based implementation generates shadows and – most importantly –
the gradients of the objective function in real time. We demonstrate this by turning the
shadow art setting, which is usually concerned with static reference shadows, into a
“shadow modeling” setting, where a user can interactively paint the target shadows and
an appropriate deformation is applied to the initial geometry in real time (see Fig. 3.14).
Note that using co-located camera and light is a design decision since painting silhouettes feels intuitive – the method is not limited to this setup.
3.4
Conclusion
In this chapter, we have shown that shadow mapping, one of the most common realtime algorithms for rendering shadows, can be made differentiable. Contrary to popular
belief, we have demonstrated that the binary test in shadow mapping is not the critical
factor, but rather visibility discontinuities at object silhouettes. Similar to the original
48
Ini�al
Chapter 3. Differentiable Shadow Mapping
User Input Op�mized
Figure 3.14: Shadow art from one view, with co-located camera and light is used as an
interactive modeling tool. Users can modify the shadow of the reference object (gray)
and an appropriate deformation is applied to the mesh in real-time (green).
shadow mapping idea, our solution uses existing rasterizers to render from the perspective of the light: rendering pre-filtered shadow maps with differentiable rasterizers
results in shadow derivatives that properly track their variation with scene geometry.
Our method takes a step in closing the gap between efficient, rasterization-based differentiable renderers and physically-based differentiable renderers. While our approach
is only approximate, the level of realism will suffice for various problems (e.g. shadows
cast by the sun). We believe that having the ability to render and differentiate images
with shadows magnitudes faster than Monte Carlo methods provides a new incentive
for widespread use in applications, particularly ones based on machine learning.
Limitations So far, we have considered only point light sources instead of area lights,
and focusing on direct shadows neglects other effects of global illumination. It is also
well known that variance shadow maps [Donnelly and Lauritzen, 2006] – our particular
choice of pre-filtered shadow maps – suffer from light leaking in regions of high depth
variance (see inset). While we did not observe an immediate impact in the experiments
in this chapter, it might be desirable to reduce light leaking by using heuristics [Nguyen, 2007] or implementing other pre-filtered shadow
maps [Peters and Klein, 2015; Annen
et al., 2007, 2008] in our framework.
In Chapter 4, we further investigate
this direction and show that moment
shadow maps [Peters and Klein, 2015] can indeed mitigate adversarial effects of light
leaking in inverse settings. Independent of the illumination effects, we have so far only
3.4. Conclusion
49
considered triangle meshes for the representation of geometry.
Possible Generalizations In addition to shadows, various effects of global illumination have been recreated by efficient real-time approximations. Approaches very similar
to ours may readily work with ambient occlusion [Miller, 1994], reflections and refractions [Heidrich and Seidel, 1999], or indirect illumination [McGuire et al., 2017].
Other geometry representations that are popular in the vision community, such as
signed distance fields [Jiang et al., 2020] or NeRFs [Mildenhall et al., 2020], should work
with shadow maps as well – although it is not clear if the same efficiency can be achieved,
as the fast computation is mainly achieved by rasterizing triangle primitives.

Figure 4.1: We prove that moment-based bounds are differentiable. This enables various applications of approximate methods from real-time graphics in differentiable rendering: we show differentiable transmittance mapping (“TM-𝑛”) akin to volumetric
shadow mapping and differentiable shadow mapping for triangle meshes. The suggested moment-based techniques (e.g. Moment Shadow Mapping), are more principled,
more accurate, and similarly efficient as existing approaches (e.g. Variance Shadow
Mapping). The (forward) derivatives show the effect of moving the directional light.
In Chapter 3, we have shown that shadows can be efficiently generated and differentiated
using the framework of pre-filtered shadow mapping. This also came with a realization:
arbitrary real-time rendering algorithms do not produce useful derivatives in the general case, and even when they do, automatic differentiation is not guaranteed to give the
right answer. This means that, even though forward rendering systems have recently
been differentiated using differentiable shading languages [Bangaru et al., 2023] or with
stochastic gradient estimates [Deliot et al., 2024], the burden of carefully examining the
underlying theory for potential pitfalls (piecewise definitions, integrals with discontinuous integrand, etc.) remains. While it is easy to verify that many techniques are free
of such issues (e.g., approximations for image-based lighting [Munkberg et al., 2022]),
for other techniques this is less obvious. This naturally leads to the question: which
rendering approximations are differentiable?
In this chapter, we seek an answer to the question for a family of approximations that
build on the theory of moments to bound measures, which, in the simplest case, can be understood as non-negative, non-decreasing functions. Various problems in graphics can
be cast in this framework: Donnelly and Lauritzen [2006] introduced moment bounds to
graphics for shadow mapping, with extensions explored by Salvi [2008], and finally introduced in the general form by Peters and Klein [2015] with moment shadow mapping.
52
Chapter 4. Differentiable Moment Bounds
Further, moment-based approximations are used for volume transmittance [Peters et al.,
2016] and order-independent transparency [Münstermann et al., 2018; Sharpe, 2018].
The central algorithm underlying most of these techniques computes bounds from
a finite set of power moments and is derived from the conditions in the classical literature [Akhiezer and Kreı̆n, 1962; Akhiezer, 1965; Kreı̆n and Nudel’man, 1977]. Due to
its complexity in comparison to other rendering techniques, potential pitfalls for differentiation are less obvious. More so, because it is known that the representation has
singularities – those could easily represent points of discontinuity. To our knowledge,
we are the first to prove that the lower and upper bounds, as stated by Tari [2005] for the
infinite case, are continuously differentiable functions in all parameters: many momentbased approximations can be readily combined with differentiable rendering. The proof
generalizes the result from Chapter 3, where we have shown that the visibility function
of variance shadow mapping is continuously differentiable (Section 3.1.3).
While the representation is differentiable, the aforementioned singularities have another adversarial effect in practice: in their vicinity, evaluating the bound becomes numerically unstable. Our study of differentiability reveals the behavior of the bound and
its derivatives near singularities – we use these insights to derive an efficient and robust
implementation of differentiable moment bounds.
Our results not only justify using moment-based approximations for differentiable
rendering, but we also show that the new techniques improve over existing ones in
inverse settings: For differentiable shadow mapping (Section 4.4.1), we use moment
shadow mapping [Peters and Klein, 2015] and show that it is an efficient and more accurate approach than variance shadow mapping [Donnelly and Lauritzen, 2006], as used in
Chapter 3. For differentiable transmittance in volume rendering (Section 4.4.2), we use
classical work on moment-based transmittance estimation [Münstermann et al., 2018;
Peters et al., 2016] and show that even the lowest quality approximation is as fast as
existing ad-hoc approximations while being significantly more accurate.

This task may be approached using different classical techniques, including the representation of 𝐹 or 𝑓 in a (polynomial) function space. For many techniques, it is unclear how the essential properties of 𝑓 (non-negative) or 𝐹 (non-decreasing) could be
preserved: transmittance ranges from 0 to 1, but approximations could yield values beyond 1 (approximation of 𝐹 is negative) or decrease along the ray. This causes (visual)
artifacts in the best case but generally leads to undefined behavior as assumptions about
𝑇 do not hold.
A suitable tool for studying and approximating integrals of non-negative functions

We will later show that it is natural to identify the 𝑥𝑖 as the zeros of a polynomial,
hence the name. The importance is that any point m ∈ R2𝑛+1 strictly inside the conic hull

example above, the theorem states that there are infinitely many ways of concentrating
mass in at most 𝑛 + 1 points on the curve, which result in the same center of mass. The
infinite set is a one-parameter family:
Theorem 2 ([Akhiezer and Kreı̆n, 1962, Theorem 3c, p.8]). If m is strictly positive, for
any value 𝜂 ∈ R \ 𝐴, there is a unique canonical representation of m where 𝜂 is one of the
𝑛 + 1 roots.

𝑥𝑖 (𝜂;m)<𝜂
where the 𝑤𝑖 are the weights and the 𝑥𝑖 are the points of the unique canonical representation. The upper bound includes the weight of 𝜂
𝑈 (𝜂; m) = 𝑤 0 (𝜂; m) + 𝐿(𝜂; m)
(4.19)
If one intends to use the lower or upper bounds in a differentiable setting, one may
ask if these functions are differentiable in m and 𝜂. It turns out that the answer to this
question is difficult to find in the given form, in particular, because the number of terms
in the sum in 𝐿 varies depending on both, m and 𝜂.
In Chapter 3, we have investigated the visibility function of variance shadow mapping [Donnelly and Lauritzen, 2006] and shown that it is continuously differentiable.
It turns out that this function, based on Chebyshev’s inequality, is a special case of the
lower bound for probability measures and m = (1, 𝑚 1, 𝑚 2 ). We now generalize this result
to sequences with an arbitrary number of moments (see the example in Fig. 4.2).
The proof can be broken down into the following high-level parts:
58
Chapter 4. Differentiable Moment Bounds
1. Section 4.2.1: we partition the space R × R2𝑛+1 into regions where 𝐿 is defined and
regions where it is not, and show that 𝐿 is trivially differentiable in the regions
forming its domain. A key observation is that the number of terms in the bound,
i.e., the number of summed weights (c.f. Eq. (4.18)), remains constant inside the
valid regions.
2. Section 4.2.2: we then analyze the behavior of the bound as one approaches the
boundary between two regions. The main insight is that at each transition between
distinct regions only one weight is included or excluded from the sum in 𝐿.
3. Section 4.2.3: finally, we show that not only the weight but also its derivative with
respect to m and 𝜂 vanish: the limit exists, so the singularities at region boundaries
are removable, which makes 𝐿 (and 𝑈 ) continuously differentiable in their domain
and across the singular points.
Since the position of 𝜂 among the other roots is relevant in the following, we assume,
for this section, that all roots, including 𝜂, are ordered from lowest to highest, i.e., 𝑥 0 <
· · · < 𝑥 𝑗 (= 𝜂) < · · · < 𝑥𝑛 .
4.2.1
Partitioning the Domain
The main difficulty with the bound 𝐿 and its derivative is the varying number of summed
weights (c.f. Eq. (4.18)): including a weight is a binary decision that can lead to discontinuities in the function or its derivative. The central idea is to first dissect the space
R × R2𝑛+1 into regions with a constant number of terms. Inside of these regions, differentiability can be easily investigated in terms of the 𝑤𝑖 .
Regions with Constant Number of Terms
According to Theorem 1, canonical representations, in particular the unique one that
defines the bound 𝐿, only exist for strictly positive moments m. Moreover, Theorem 2
indicates that there is the additional restriction of 𝜂 ∉ 𝐴: 𝐴 contains the 𝑛 simple roots
of a special degree-n polynomial 𝑃𝑛 (𝑥; m). This characterizes the domain of 𝐿:
Ω = {(𝜂, m) ∈ R × R2𝑛+1 : positive(m) ∧ 𝑃𝑛 (𝜂; m) ≠ 0}.
(4.20)
At any given point (𝜂 0, m0 ) ∈ Ω the bound 𝐿 will be a sum over some number of
terms, so, under which conditions does the number change if one moves to another point
(𝜂 1, m1 ) ∈ Ω, on a continuously differentiable path? The answer is our first result:
Theorem 5. If the number of terms in the bounding functions differs between two points
(𝜂 0, m0 ) ∈ Ω and (𝜂 1, m1 ) ∈ Ω, any continuously differentiable path connecting the points
crosses at least one point (𝜂 ′, m′) ∈ R × R2𝑛+1 where either 𝜂 ′ is a root of 𝑃𝑛 (𝑥; m′) or the
sequence of moments m′ is not strictly positive.
The proof can be found in Appendix A.3, Section A.3.3. It exploits that the number
of terms changes if and only if one of the {𝑥𝑖 } changes its place with 𝜂 on the real
line. The points {𝑥𝑖 } and 𝜂 are the roots of an orthogonal polynomial 𝜓𝑛+1 (𝑥; 𝜂, m),
4.2. Differentiating Moment-based Bounds
59
which has 𝑛 + 1 real and simple roots in the domain Ω. Since the roots are continuously
differentiable functions in m and 𝜂, the {𝑥𝑖 } and 𝜂 can only change their order outside of
the domain Ω. The inset visualizes the theorem and shows the evolution of the roots on
a continuously differentiable path 𝜸 (𝜏) ∈ R×R2𝑛+1
from a point (𝜂 0, m0 ) ∈ Ω to a point (𝜂 1, m1 ) ∈ Ω.
Red trajectories cannot be taken in Ω.
Geometrically, strict positivity can be violated
on a path when moments m cross through u(𝜂) on
the boundary of the moment curve (more generally
boundary regions on the cone) or due to numerical
inaccuracy. But, strict positivity can be enforced by biasing [Peters and Klein, 2015]: the
moments m are slightly “pulled” inside of the conic hull, away from its boundary.
Proposition 1. If 𝛼 ∈ (0, 1] and m★ ∈ R2𝑛+1 is a bias vector such that (1 − 𝛼)m + 𝛼m★
is strictly positive, the number of terms in the bounding functions changes only in points
where 𝜂 is a root of the polynomial 𝑃𝑛 (𝑥; (1 − 𝛼)m + 𝛼m★).
Therefore, one can see the domain Ω as a partition of regions where 𝜂 is not a root
of 𝑃𝑛 , and inside of each region, the number of terms in 𝐿 remains constant.
Differentiable Weights
Each region of the domain Ω has a fixed number of terms, so, inside of it, the derivative
of 𝐿 simply reduces to the sum of derivatives of the weights 𝑤𝑖 , and we observe:
Theorem 6. For (𝜂, m) ∈ Ω the weights 𝑤 0, . . . , 𝑤𝑛 associated with all points of the unique
canonical representation are continuously differentiable in m and 𝜂.
See Appendix A.3, Section A.3.4 for the (short) proof; it follows:
Corollary 1. The bounding functions 𝐿(𝜂, m) and 𝑈 (𝜂, m) are continuously differentiable
in their domain Ω.
4.2.2
Approaching Singularities
Although having established differentiability on the domain of 𝐿 in Section 4.2.1, the
result does not rule out discontinuities at points in
Λ = {(𝜂, m) ∈ R × R2𝑛+1 : positive(m) ∧ 𝑃𝑛 (𝜂; m) = 0},
(4.21)
which is the set of points with strictly positive m but 𝜂 is a root of 𝑃𝑛 . Recall that the
bounds are undefined at these points. In the following, we investigate the behavior of
the {𝑥𝑖 } as a point (𝜂, m) ∈ Ω in the domain of 𝐿 approaches such singularities.
The polynomial 𝑃𝑛 (𝑥, m) is part of a sequence of polynomials {𝑃𝑖 }, which are pairwise orthogonal under measures that are representations of m. As an orthogonal polynomial, 𝑃𝑛 has 𝑛 real and simple roots {𝑦𝑖 }𝑛1 : at a point (𝜂, m) ∈ Ω, 𝜂 must lay between
at most two roots of 𝑃𝑛 , in either of the intervals (−∞, 𝑦1 ), (𝑦 𝑗 , 𝑦 𝑗+1 ), or (𝑦𝑛 , ∞).

Figure 4.3: Geometric relationship between the points of the canonical representation 𝜂
and 𝑥 1 for a moment sequence m = (1, 𝑚 1, 𝑚 2 ) and the singularity point 𝑦1 , a root of 𝑃𝑛 .
The first dimension is omitted. First row: 𝜂 approaches 𝑦𝑖 . Second row: 𝑦𝑖 approaches 𝜂
as the moments are varied.
Geometrically, one can think of the roots as 𝑛 distinct points u(𝑦𝑖 ) on the moment
curve, with u(𝜂) between (at most) two of them. The situation 𝑃𝑛 (𝜂; m) = 0 can now
arise in different ways: (1) the point u(𝜂) moves towards one of the two roots on the
curve, (2) one of the two points moves towards u(𝜂) on the curve, or (3) a combination
of the two. Fig. 4.3 visualizes the behavior of the roots:
Theorem 7. If 𝑥 𝑗 (= 𝜂) < 𝑥𝑛 approaches 𝑦 𝑗+1 on a continuously differentiable path, then
𝑥𝑛 → ∞. If 𝑥 𝑗 (= 𝜂) > 𝑥 0 approaches 𝑦 𝑗 on a continuously differentiable path, then 𝑥 0 →
−∞.
The proof is found in Appendix A.3, Section A.3.5. It exploits that the points {𝑥𝑖 }𝑛0 (including 𝜂) strictly interlace with the roots {𝑦𝑖 }𝑛1 of 𝑃𝑛 in
the domain Ω. At singularities, 𝑛 of the points {𝑥𝑖 }
collapse to the 𝑛 roots {𝑦𝑖 } of 𝑃𝑛 (see inset). As the
{𝑥𝑖 } and the roots {𝑦𝑖 } are continuously differentiable functions in (𝜂, m), a path 𝜸 (𝜏) from a point
(𝜂 0, m0 ) ∈ Ω to a singularity point (𝜂 1, m1 ) ∈ Λ is reversible, so each {𝑦𝑖 } at the singularity corresponds exactly to one {𝑥𝑖 }. This leaves one of the outer points 𝑥 0 or 𝑥𝑛 (≠ 𝜂)
without a corresponding root at the singularity, and it must go to ±∞. Since the theorem
considers both directions, we can state the following:
Corollary 2. If 𝑥 𝑗 (= 𝜂) > 𝑦 𝑗 follows a continuously differentiable path through 𝑦 𝑗 such
that after the singularity 𝑥 𝑗−1 (= 𝜂) < 𝑦 𝑗 , then the point 𝑥 0 goes to −∞ and reappears as 𝑥𝑛
from ∞. The other direction of 𝑥 𝑗 approaching 𝑦 𝑗+1 behaves analogously.
4.3. Implementation
61
Therefore, when crossing a singularity, exactly one root 𝑥𝑖 changes its place with 𝜂,
so for the sum in 𝐿 (c.f. Eq. (4.18)) the following holds:
Corollary 3. As 𝜂 follows a continuously differentiable path through a singularity, only
the weight associated with 𝑥 0 or 𝑥𝑛 is included in or excluded from the sum in the bounding
functions.
The last observation is essential as it allows studying only one of the weights and its
derivatives to determine the limit behavior for the bounding functions 𝐿 and 𝑈 .
4.2.3
Weight (Derivative) at Singularities
In Section 4.2.1 we have shown that 𝐿 is continuously differentiable on its domain Ω
but the domain is a collection of distinct regions, connected by singularities at which 𝐿
is undefined, not ruling out discontinuous behavior across singularities. We have then
observed in Section 4.2.2 that when singularities are crossed, exactly one of the points
𝑥𝑘 ∈ {𝑥𝑖 } switches places with 𝜂, and therefore only its weight 𝑤𝑘 is included or excluded
from the sum in 𝐿 (recall Eq. (4.18)). It remains to investigate how the weight and its
derivative, and therefore 𝐿 and its derivative, behave when a singularity is approached,
which is equivalent to studying their limit as 𝑥𝑘 → ±∞ (c.f. Theorem 7).
The possible outcomes are apparent: if the weight 𝑤𝑘 , in the limit, goes to zero,
𝜕𝑤𝑘
𝑘
the singularities in 𝐿 are removable. If additionally the derivatives 𝜕𝑤
𝜕𝜂 and 𝜕m go to
𝜕𝐿
zero, the singularities in the derivatives 𝜕𝐿
𝜕𝜂 and 𝜕m are removable. Otherwise, 𝐿 or its
derivatives will be discontinuous. We make the following two observations (proven in
Appendix A.3, Sections A.3.6 and A.3.7):
Lemma 1. If 𝑥𝑘 is the point that goes to ±∞, then the associated weight 𝑤𝑘 goes to zero.
Lemma 2. If 𝑥𝑘 is the point that goes to ±∞, then the associated derivatives of the weight
𝜕𝑤𝑘
𝜕𝑤𝑘
𝜕𝜂 and 𝜕m go to zero.
The proofs use the fact that both the weight and its derivatives are rational functions
in 𝑥𝑘 , where the denominator has a (much) higher degree than the numerator, so they
vanish as 𝑥𝑘 → ±∞. We conclude this section with our main result:
Theorem 8. The bounding functions 𝐿(𝜂; m) and 𝑈 (𝜂; m) are continuously differentiable
on their domains Ω and the singularities are removable so that 𝐿 and 𝑈 are continuously
differentiable over the set Ω ∪ Λ ⊂ R × R2𝑛+1 , with all strictly positive moments.
4.3
Implementation
Algorithm 3 computes the moment bounds (the primal algorithm) and loosely follows
Münstermann et al. [2018, Algorithm 1], where 𝛽 blends between the lower and upper
bound. We have not yet materialized all operations (e.g., root finding) because, as will
be shown, the choice significantly affects the stability of the primal algorithm and its
derivative, which is calculated by the adjoint algorithm.

𝑤 0, . . . , 𝑤𝑛 ← vandermonde_solve(𝜂, 𝑥 1, . . . , 𝑥𝑛 , m̄)

Automatic Differentiation Algorithm 3 can be readily implemented in an automatic
differentiation (AD) framework like PyTorch [Paszke et al., 2019]: a Cholesky solver is
available, the roots can be determined in closed-form or, for high degrees, as the eigenvalues of the companion matrix (as in numpy [Harris et al., 2020]), and a generic linear
solver can be used for the Vandermonde system.
While this straightforward implementation is a useful reference, it is numerically unstable and inefficient, both in terms of memory consumption and runtime. Improving the
stability requires careful consideration of the numerically critical parts (Section 4.3.1).
The derived primal and adjoint algorithms can be efficiently implemented by fusing all
operations, avoiding the construction of a computation graph as in automatic differentiation (Section 4.3.2).
4.3.1
Explicit Algorithm and Reverse-Mode Differentiation
In this section, we will gradually materialize Algorithm 3, one operation at a time, and
derive expressions for reverse-mode derivatives. This leads to concrete primal and adjoint algorithms. We will use the notation 𝛿𝑎 to denote the adjoint of a variable 𝑎, i.e.,
the derivative 𝜕L
𝜕𝑎 w.r.t. a scalar function L (𝑎), which could be the loss function in a
gradient-based optimization. We assume adjoints with the same “shape” as the primal
variable, i.e., if b ∈ R𝑚 then 𝛿b ∈ R𝑚 .
Solving for the Kernel Polynomial Since the Hankel matrix H of the biased moment
vector is positive-definite and symmetric,
Hc = 𝜼
(4.22)
(c.f. Eq. (4.16)) can be solved stable by performing the Cholesky decomposition of H,
followed by forward and backward substitution.
The output of this operation is the vector c, so, in reverse-mode differentiation, the
adjoint operation uses the input 𝛿c to compute 𝛿H and 𝛿𝜼. For a linear system Ax =

b with invertible A, one can derive the equalities defining the adjoints [Strang, 2007,
Section 8.7]
𝛿b = A−⊤𝛿x
𝛿A = −𝛿b x⊤,
(4.23)
which leads to explicit expressions for the outputs
𝛿𝜼 = H−1𝛿c
𝛿H = −𝛿𝜼c⊤,
(4.24)
since H is symmetric. Therefore, 𝛿𝜼 is obtained by solving another linear system involving the Hankel matrix, with the same Cholesky decomposition, and the adjoint 𝛿H is
simply an outer product.
Finding the Kernel Roots By the structure of the problem, and critical for our proofs
in Section 4.2, we know that one of the roots {𝑥𝑖 }𝑛1 of the kernel polynomial is unbounded
when the query point 𝜂 approaches a singularity, because its leading coefficient 𝑐𝑛 vanishes. Therefore, besides efficiency, numerical stability and adequate precision in 32-bit
arithmetic are desired properties.
The cases 𝑛 = 1 (3 moments) and 𝑛 = 2 (5 moments) lead to kernel polynomials
of degree 𝑑 = 1 and 𝑑 = 2, respectively, whose roots can be found using closed-form
expressions. For the quadratic equation, we have implemented a robust solver following
Kahan [2004]. Although closed-form expressions exist for degrees 3 and 4, we opt for a
fast and more accurate Newton-style approach for degrees > 2 [Yuksel, 2022]. Notably,
vanishing 𝑐𝑛 is naturally handled in our implementation by returning one root with value
inf instead of branching to a different version of the algorithm or trying to explicitly
avoid this case by comparing 𝜂 to the singularity points (the roots of 𝑃𝑛 , Section 4.2.1).
All subsequent operations gracefully handle roots at infinity by returning the limit value.
If {𝑥𝑖 }𝑛𝑖=1 are the roots of the kernel polynomial 𝐾 (c, 𝑥) with coefficients c, the adjoint
operation computes 𝛿c, given {𝛿𝑥𝑖 }:

Solving the Vandermonde System Instead of solving the Vandermonde system with
a generic approach, we use the iterative algorithm by Björck and Pereyra [1970] as we
found it to be efficient, stable, and simple to implement. The algorithm is derived from
interpolation with divided differences, which is also the suggested method by Münstermann et al. [2018]. Additionally, we observe that it gracefully handles inputs close to
or exactly at infinity: if a point 𝑥𝑖 is at infinity with value inf, the associated solution 𝑤𝑖 becomes zero. This corresponds exactly to the expected limit behavior (Section 4.2.3). The stability of the algorithm generally depends on the order of the inputs
{𝜂, 𝑥 1, . . . , 𝑥𝑛 } [Higham, 1987]. We found that ordering the roots ascending by absolute
value drastically improves the precision (this is effectively pivoting), and only then the
limit value is obtained.
The inputs to the algorithm are the roots 𝑥 0 (= 𝜂), 𝑥 1, . . . , 𝑥𝑛 and the first 𝑛+1 entries of
the moment vector m, which we denote by m̄ = (𝑚 0, ..., 𝑚𝑛 ) ⊤ . The adjoint of the partial
moment vector is obtained by re-using the equalities for the linear system (Eq. (4.23)):
𝛿m̄ = V−⊤𝛿w,
(4.28)
where w = (𝑤 0, 𝑤 1, . . . , 𝑤𝑛 ) ⊤ is the vector of weights and its adjoint 𝛿w is an input. We
obtain 𝛿m̄ with an algorithm for solving the dual Vandermonde system by Björck and
Pereyra [1970]. Intuitively, the solution is a polynomial interpolating the values {𝛿𝑤𝑖 }
at points {𝑥𝑖 }. If a root 𝑥𝑖 is at infinity, its value 𝛿𝑤𝑖 does not contribute to the interpolation (this can be seen by expressing the derivative in Eq. (A.18) with the Vandermonde
matrix), so the resulting polynomial has one less degree, i.e., 𝛿m̄𝑛 = 0. This is exactly
the output of the algorithm for an 𝑥𝑖 at infinity.
The same equalities provide an expression for the adjoint matrix
𝛿V = −𝛿m̄w⊤,
(4.29)
which leads to the root adjoints

handled in the adjoint root finding (see discussion around Eq. (4.27)).
Remaining Operations The moment biasing, Hankel matrix construction, and weight
summation require no special considerations.

and an NVIDIA RTX 3090 GPU (24 GB VRAM). The PyTorch implementation is based on
automatic differentiation applied to Algorithm 3 (Section 4.3, Paragraph Automatic Differentiation), while the C++/CUDA implementation follows Section 4.3.1. The Retained
Roots mode stores the roots of the kernel polynomial between the forward and backward pass.
only leads to excessive storage requirements but also results in expensive reads from
(GPU) memory. Just-in-time (JIT) compilation can mitigate these issues but comes at
the cost of losing control over the generated kernels and might not lead to the expected
improvements: for example, we have JIT-compiled the AD implementation (Section 4.3,
Automatic Differentiation) using PyTorch 2.x’s compile feature, measuring either negligible improvements (𝑛 = 1, 2) or even worse runtime (𝑛 > 2), because some operations
are currently unsupported (e.g. finding eigenvalues of a matrix).
Instead, we have implemented the primal and adjoint algorithms from Section 4.3.1
entirely in C++, only depending on a small subset of the standard library. The floating
point type and 𝑛 are known at compile time, leading to the generation of highly optimized code. We instantiate and test variants for 32-bit float and 64-bit double, and
𝑛 = 1, . . . , 5. GPUs are targeted using CUDA, such that all operations of the primal algorithm are compiled into a single CUDA kernel, and similarly, a single kernel is compiled
for the adjoint algorithm. Global memory accesses are often the bottleneck of a GPU implementation, so we ensure that intermediate values can reside in registers. Specifically,
we avoid register spilling to (local) memory by only statically indexing into arrays, for
example during the root finding procedure [Peters, 2023].
The C++/CUDA implementation is exposed to Python using nanobind [Jakob, 2022],
so it readily works with array programming frameworks like PyTorch, numpy, or JAX.
66
Chapter 4. Differentiable Moment Bounds
Accuracy Verification We first verify the accuracy of the C++ implementation by
comparing it to the PyTorch (AD) implementation for 1 million random samples. The
average accuracy in the computed roots and weights is comparable (Table A.1, Appendix A.2). The gradients of the bound computed by the adjoint procedure are verified by comparing them to finite differences. The C++ implementation produces errors
almost equal to the PyTorch AD implementation, differing in the 3rd to 4th digit (Table A.1). We exclude samples with 𝜂 near a singularity, so this merely serves as a sanity
check and the limit behavior is studied separately (Paragraph Stability Analysis)
Performance We compare the runtime and memory consumption of the C++/CUDA
implementation to the PyTorch-based AD implementation, on the GPU, using 2 million
randomly sampled moments {m𝑖 } and points {𝜂𝑖 } for varying 𝑛 (Fig. 4.4). Across all tests,
the custom CUDA implementation is considerably faster, both for the primal (forward)
and adjoint (backward) computations as well as in 32-bit and in 64-bit precision. The over
1000× increase in runtime (𝑛 = 3, 32 bits) is related to root finding using the companion
matrix for 𝑛 > 2, because PyTorch seemingly does not solve for eigenvalues on the GPU
(the CPU runtime is similar). Surprisingly, the corresponding function is also faster for
64-bit input, which explains why the 64-bit primal algorithm for 𝑛 > 2 is faster than the
32-bit variant. We have no conclusive explanation but this behavior is consistent across
different machines, operating systems, and PyTorch versions.
Regardless, the variants 𝑛 = 1 and 𝑛 = 2 use closed-form expressions for the roots, so
they are unaffected by this anomaly. In 32-bit, the CUDA implementation is up to three
orders of magnitude faster, and in 64-bit up to two orders. The default variant of the
CUDA implementation recomputes all intermediate values from the forward pass in the
backward pass, which makes it at least as expensive as the primal algorithm. Still, the
runtime is much lower than the AD graph traversal. The observed performance benefit
in an application will certainly be lower because a differentiable rendering pipeline consists of various costly operations and the GPU might not be saturated by the number of
computed bounds. If the algorithm is used for shadow mapping, 2 million bounds correspond roughly to rendering a 1k image with two light sources. Even for 50× larger input
(∼ 100 Million bounds), for 𝑛 ≤ 2, the 32-bit primal algorithm runs in less than 5 milliseconds and the adjoint algorithm in under 20 milliseconds (Fig. 4.5, left column). The
double-precision runtime is significantly worse and roughly aligns with the theoretical
performance of the tested GPU (64× higher FP32 throughput).
The memory footprint of the CUDA implementation is very predictable as storage
from global memory is only allocated for the output. If 𝑁 is the number of bounds to
compute, the primal algorithm generates an array of size 𝑁 and the adjoint algorithm
an array of size 𝑁 (2𝑛 + 2) (for 2𝑛 + 1 moment adjoints and the adjoint of 𝜂), therefore
the memory requirements are linear (Fig. 4.5, right column, mind the log-scaled x-axes).
Specifically, in contrast to the PyTorch AD implementation, no computation graph is
constructed during the primal algorithm, which leads to a significantly smaller memory footprint (Fig. 4.4). For the adjoint computation, the AD implementation also only
allocates memory for the outputs.

Figure 4.6: Evaluating the bound near singularities can be numerically unstable, leading to undefined output in the forward and backward computation (top). The implementation of an algorithm that considers the properties of the bound at singularities
(“C++/CUDA”, Section 4.3.1) is more robust than the naive implementation based on

Retaining Roots The default C++/CUDA implementation outputs only the bounds
from the primal kernel. In the adjoint kernel, the required quantities (e.g., the Cholesky
factorization and the roots) are recomputed by re-running the primal algorithm. For 𝑛 >
2, root finding is an iterative procedure (Section 4.3.1), which can become the dominating
operation during the adjoint phase, in terms of runtime. We have therefore implemented
a “retained roots” variant that returns the roots from the primal computation and re-uses
them during the adjoint computation. The variant has a similar computation time for the
primal part and an increased memory complexity, which is still significantly less than
AD, but a considerably lower runtime for the adjoint part for 𝑛 > 2 (Figures 4.4 and 4.5).
Stability Analysis The bounding functions are undefined at a finite set of points,
where 𝜂 takes the value of one of the roots of the polynomial 𝑃𝑛 (and this is not a pathological case, as shown in Section 4.4.1). As 𝜂 approaches a singular point, one root 𝑥𝑖
tends to infinity, which can compromise the accuracy of the bound and its derivatives,
specifically in 32-bit precision.
We use the following procedure to study the implementations near the singularities:
for moments m, we generate points 𝜂𝑘 inside an 𝜖-neighborhood (𝜖 = 8e-6) around each
singularity 𝑦 𝑗 (𝑗 = 1, . . . , 𝑛), and compute the bound and its derivatives. We also evaluate
the bound and the derivatives exactly at the singular point (up to floating-point precision). At each 𝜂𝑘 , two traits are desirable : (1) the bound and the derivatives remain finite
since undefined values can severely compromise a gradient-based optimization, and (2)
their values tend towards the value at the singularity.
4.4. Applications

For 𝑛 = 1, . . . , 5 we randomly generate moments, then compute the number of NaNs
and the (absolute) difference to the value at the singularity (Fig. 4.6). Results are for
32-bit precision and the difference is reported as the maximum across all 𝑛 and moment
vectors. The PyTorch (AD) implementation generates almost no usable values near a
singularity, which, to a large part, is due to failures in its root-finding procedure. The
C++ implementation generates no NaN output since its operations are carefully designed
around the limit case (Section 4.3.1). This is also reflected by the values tending to the
value at the singularity (Fig. 4.6, second row).

4.4.1
Differentiable Shadow Mapping
In rendering, shadows from point lights (including those at infinity) are often approximated with shadow mapping [Williams, 1978] (see Section 2.2.2), and, as shown in Chapter 3, pre-filtered shadow mapping is a suitable framework for differentiable rendering.
The general idea behind pre-filtering techniques based on moments [Peters and Klein,
2015; Donnelly and Lauritzen, 2006] is to obtain a set of moment maps by filtering the
shadow map 𝑓 with a compact kernel:

Light Leaking in Appearance Decomposition
Variance shadow mapping suffers from light leaking: if the depth has high variance in
the filter support, the computed bound is not sharp and visibility is overestimated. The
adverse effect of light leaking is more significant in inverse settings: in forward render-

sharper bound to the visibility (Fig. 4.1).
Faithful estimation of visibility is critical when the appearance of an object is decomposed into the material and the illumination, e.g., as a post-processing step of a (neural)
3D reconstruction pipeline. Jointly optimizing the light direction and albedo texture
from a single reference image yields significantly more accurate results with moment
shadow mapping (Fig. 4.7): while VSM and MSM obtain the correct light direction, the
VSM albedo texture shows traces of the shadow because it must account for areas with
reduced shadow intensity. Our MSM implementation not only generates higher quality textures, but is as fast as the VSM implementation from Chapter 3 (Fig. 4.8). The
C++/CUDA implementation (Section 4.3.2) is critical to matching the performance: a
PyTorch (AD) implementation of MSM is observed to have twice the VSM runtime, and,
more importantly, does not converge due to NaN outputs (Section 4.4.1).
Comparison to Ray Traced Shadows
Instead of approximating visibility, it can be accurately determined using ray tracing,
and, more generally, realistic images can be synthesized with systems for physicallybased, differentiable rendering. The impact on runtime is significant: an approximate,
differentiable renderer with moment shadow mapping finishes an image-based reconstruction task in
seconds, while the physically-based system Mitsuba 3 [Jakob et al., 2022b] requires minutes (Fig. 4.9).
Importantly, the visibility gradients generated by
Geometry (Opt.)
MSM lead to a successful reconstruction: if the visibility is detached from the AD graph, the reconstruction fails (see inset). With significant contribution from indirect illumination, approximating only shadows would not be
sufficient, and additional techniques are required.

Numerical Considerations
The PyTorch AD implementation, in contrast to the C++/CUDA implementation, generates undefined outputs and gradients if the query point is close to a singularity (Section 4.3.2, Paragraph Stability Analysis). In forward rendering, this merely leads to visual
artifacts, but in inverse rendering, this can corrupt a (potentially long-running) optimization. Fig. 4.10 shows that this is not a pathological case: almost all surfaces directly
visible to a light will result in queries close to singularities. The reason is that the moments are generated by filtering over a compact region in the shadow map (Eq. (4.31)).
Except at discontinuities, the depth values in the filter support will be correlated and only
subtend a small section of the moment curve with concentrated singularities. The depth
values of visible points will be close to the depths in the filter support, so fall within that
same segment of the moment curve and near the singularities. For this reason, we were
unable to achieve a stable convergence using the PyTorch AD implementation.
Moment Biasing A central feature of moment shadow mapping is the ability to exactly reconstruct the visibility if the filter support region contains no more than two
surfaces (perpendicular to the light direction) [Peters and Klein, 2015]. For such cases,
the moments are slightly biased to ensure that the Hankel matrix is non-singular. We
use the bias vectors by Münstermann et al. [2018] and test the
n=3
recommended bias for 𝑛 = 2 (𝛼 = 5 · 10−7 ), but singular matrices
are still occasionally encountered. We use a slightly higher bias
(as a rule of thumb, at least twice the recommended value).
Some experiments run successfully with the bias recommended by Münstermann et al. [2018, Table 1, Supplementary MaGeometry (Opt.)
terial] (e.g., Section 4.4.1), so it is worth testing different values to
strike the right balance between accuracy and robustness in an application. With stable

biasing, the number of moments can be increased from 4 (𝑛 = 2) to 6 (𝑛 = 3) and further,

Quality of Rasterization Derivatives.
The quality of the approximated visibility decreases with the shadow map resolution.
Low-resolution shadow maps also harm the inverse setting (Fig. 4.11). First, they cannot
reproduce fine structures in the target shadow, and second, they are limited by the differentiable rasterizer: the implementation uses nvdiffrast [Laine et al., 2020], which
differentiates discontinuous coverage by anti-aliasing silhouettes. If pixels are larger
than (projected) triangles, the silhouette detection can fail and coverage is not differentiated (Fig. 4.12). As a result, shadow boundaries carry no gradients w.r.t. the geometry.
We have made the same observation in Chapter 3, Section 3.2, but, until now, have not
yet shown the adverse effect in a reconstruction task. However, this is not a conceptual
issue of differentiable shadow mapping, and it can likely be mitigated by using other
approaches to differentiable rasterization [Liu et al., 2019a; Pidhorskyi et al., 2025].

When volume rendering is combined with a physically-based image formation model,
e.g., to decompose the appearance into material and illumination or to use shadows
as additional reconstruction cues, one must compute the transmittance from a light
source to each point on a primary ray. Numerically estimating the transmittance can
lead to quadratic complexity, which, even for small images, quickly becomes infeasible,
especially when storing a computation graph for reverse-mode differentiation. Several
ways of approximating the transmittance have been proposed, one of which is applying shadow mapping by rendering and storing the expected termination depth from the
perspective of a light

the approximation is efficient and induces dense gradients in a volume, it is difficult
to achieve shadows with reasonable intensity (Section 4.4.2) and the approach is not
volume-based, i.e., it does not properly handle empty space, which is desirable when
combining multiple volumes or different scene representations (Section 4.4.2).
Transmittance Mapping Inspired by classical work in real-time
graphics [Peters et al., 2016; Jansen and Bavoil, 2010; Lokovic and
Veach, 2000; Delalandre et al., 2011] we propose a principled approach to transmittance approximation for differentiable volume
rendering. Similar to how a shadow map stores the minimum depth
along a ray emanating from the light source, a transmittance map
stores a compact representation of the transmittance function along
a ray (see inset).

Visibility Through Empty Space
The issue arising from shadow mapping not being a principled method for approximating transmittance is best shown by testing a trivial property of transmittance: if the
density in a volume is zero, the transmittance is one, because no light is absorbed. The
test setting is inspired by shadow art [Mitra and Pauly, 2009]: planes (triangle meshes)

TM-4
TM-5
Figure 4.15: Performing shadow art by using differentiable volume rendering. The
shadow receiver planes are triangle meshes and a signed distance function, defined on
a grid, is optimized such that its cast shadows resemble target shadows on the planes.
Methods based on shadow mapping (by Tiwary et al. [2022] and Variance Shadow Mapping (VSM) [Donnelly and Lauritzen, 2006]), do not handle empty space because they
are not volume-based. Using a moment-based approach to approximate the transmittance (TM-𝑛) yields results close to the shadow ray reference, with the same runtime.
are placed around a grid representing an SDF. The SDF and the density-controlling variable 𝑠 are optimized to cast given target shadows on the planes, by using an objective
function that compares renderings of the cast shadows to the targets. The topological flexibility of the SDF is beneficial for this task and volume rendering ensures dense
gradients. To maintain a valid SDF, the function is redistanced by solving the Eikonal
equation in each iteration [Detrixhe et al., 2013; Vicini et al., 2022].
Shadow rays do not have quadratic complexity in this setting because the receiver is
rasterized, not volume-rendered, so they can serve as a reference. While transmittance
maps generate results close to the shadow ray solution, shadow mapping fails (Fig. 4.15):
since the receiver planes are not volumes, they are not considered in volume rendering,
so, whenever a light ray misses the implicit surface, the expected terminated depth will
be zero (compare Eq. 4.35). Shadow mapping behaves as if an object directly in front
of the light source obstructs the view. Therefore, the unobstructed parts of the receiver
plane will appear in shadow, and the obstructed parts will, too. Since the shadow intensity in the method by Tiwary et al. [2022] depends on the distance between the occluder
and receiver some areas in shadow appear grayish. The optimization behaves contrary
to the expectation and recreates the bright parts of the target image using the volume
(the sphere emerges) while the shadow is recreated by generating free space. Transmittance maps handle free space correctly by reporting constant 1 transmittance, while
being about 4× faster than shadow rays – as fast as shadow mapping.

Shadow mapping does not necessarily fail and has been successfully used in differentiable volume rendering: if 𝑠 is high, the volume density spikes at the surface, and vol-

4.5. Conclusion
81
ume rendering collapses to surface rendering. Since 𝑠 (c.f. Eq. 4.39) is often optimized
with the volume, from low to high [Wang et al., 2021; Li et al., 2023], the point at which
shadow mapping becomes a good approximation depends on the optimization schedule.
This leads to an intricate balance: starting with a very low 𝑠 induces dense gradients,
which more robustly localize the surface, independent of the initial state. However, the
inaccurate visibility approximation at earlier iterations increases the risk of becoming
stuck in suboptimal local minima.
This effect is observable in 3D reconstruction from multi-view images (Fig. 4.16).
Shadow mapping (VSM) navigates into an unrecoverable state by decreasing the target
density until the volume dissolves and the implicit surface disappears, while transmittance mapping obtains a reasonable reconstruction of the target shape. The visibility
gradients are substantial in this setting: with visibility detached from the AD graph, e.g.
as in nvdiffrecmc [Hasselgren et al., 2022, Sec.3.1, Shadow Gradients], the reconstruction fails.
We also want to highlight that this optimization, in each iteration, renders all views
(each 190 × 256 pixels) using 128 samples while maintaining a performance of one iteration per second and reasonable memory consumption (Fig. 4.16). Approaching this task
with shadow rays would be difficult without stochastic ray sampling.
4.5
Conclusion
In this chapter, we have shown that measures can be bounded in a differentiable manner by proving that the lower and upper bounds arising from the truncated Hamburger
moment problem are continuously differentiable in their parameters. As a consequence,
the use of various approximate rendering techniques, which are based on these bounds,
has been justified in differentiable rendering. We have shown that this contributes new
approaches for differentiable shadow mapping and volume transmittance, which are not
only more principled than existing ones, but also more accurate and equally efficient.
Limitations and Future Work The techniques proposed for differentiable shadow
mapping and transmittance inherit the classical limitations of shadow mapping: the approach makes simplifying assumptions about the light sources, such that area lights or
image-based lighting are not supported, and the quality of the approximation depends
on the spatial discretization. If the shadow/transmittance map is too coarse, small structures are missed; one can increase the resolution, but the memory requirements increase
as well. Adaptive shadow mapping techniques might be applicable in this context [Fernando et al., 2001; Lefohn et al., 2007].
Numerical stability is a major challenge of moment-based bounds, especially for
higher orders, as the relevant matrices become increasingly ill-conditioned and robustly
finding polynomial roots more difficult. We have shown that implementations benefit
from being designed around these constraints, but our analysis is certainly not exhaustive. For the forward direction, numerical stability has mainly been investigated for
quantization [Peters and Klein, 2015; Peters, 2017] and similar exploration could be useful for the backward direction.
82
Chapter 4. Differentiable Moment Bounds
Lastly, we have not yet covered applications that exploit the full potential of higherorder bounds. For example, the transmittance mapping framework can be used for differentiable order-independent transparency based on the work by Münstermann et al.
[2018]. Subsequent work has picked up and investigated this idea: Müller et al. [2025]
explore differentiable moment-based order-independent transparency for 3D Gaussian
splatting [Kerbl et al., 2023] as an alternative to ray tracing or per-pixel sorting.
Chapter 5
Radiative Backpropagation with
Non-Static Geometry
In the previous chapters, we have approached efficient differentiable rendering by investigating how certain techniques from real-time graphics can be applied in this context.
While real-time techniques are the foundation of many practical differentiable rendering
systems, their approximate nature remains a fundamental limitation. Various applications require a more faithful simulation of light transport, for example, multi-bounce
light paths in the design of optical lenses [Sun et al., 2021; Teh et al., 2025]. Even though
real-time approximations exist for more complex phenomena, most techniques are illsuited as they strive more for plausible appearance than for physical accuracy – this can
easily steer optimizations to undesirable solutions.
Generally, such applications are better approached with physically-based differentiable rendering, as it adheres to the equations of light transport (Section 2.1.2). When
it comes to improving efficiency, one typically wants to maintain physical accuracy and
avoid approximations. Other measures are taken instead, such as using specialized tooling for differentiable rendering [Jakob et al., 2022a; Bangaru et al., 2023] or decreasing
the required sample budget through variance reduction [Wang et al., 2023b; Belhe et al.,
2024; Su and Gkioulekas, 2024; Yan et al., 2025]. Alternatively, the efficiency of differentiation might be approached on an algorithmic level: Gradient-based optimization of an
objective function commonly relies on reverse-mode derivatives (see Section 2.3), which,
in the case of light transport, can be efficiently computed using radiative backpropagation (RB) [Nimier-David et al., 2020; Stam, 2020] (see Section 2.4.3). Instead of building and storing a large computation graph for automatic differentiation (AD), radiative
backpropagation explicitly computes derivatives using a differential light transport simulation. By replaying primal light paths [Vicini et al., 2021], this requires only constant
memory and linear time in path length.
A subtle detail in the original formalism is that radiative backpropagation was derived under the assumption of static geometry, which means that a scene parameter 𝜃 ,
among others, does not alter the shape or position of surfaces, lights, and sensors. Many
applications of differentiable rendering violate this assumption (see Section 2.4.1). Surprisingly, to our knowledge, the work by Zeltner et al. [2021] is the only one investigating
the effects of non-static geometry, that is, the parameter 𝜃 is allowed to affect geometry,
on radiative backpropagation – almost all other research of the non-static case focuses
on handling discontinuous visibility (e.g., [Li et al., 2018a; Loubet et al., 2019; Zhang
et al., 2020, 2023]). This is likely because the boundary term resulting from discontinuous visibility is unique to non-static geometry and generally challenging to compute,
84
Chapter 5. Radiative Backpropagation with Non-Static Geometry
whereas the interior term can be computed using either radiative backpropagation or
automatic differentiation. However, we find that critical implications for radiative backpropagation are missed by the work of Zeltner et al. [2021]. This renders efficient and
correct computation of interior derivatives infeasible in the non-static case.
In this chapter, we revisit radiative backpropagation and re-derive the theory without assuming static geometry. A central observation is that differential radiance, the
quantity that governs the gradient simulation, is not transported in the same manner as
normal radiance. This deviation from the original formalism and its consequences are
not commonly discussed. In fact, we demonstrate through simple experiments that existing implementations of radiative backpropagation are biased when geometry is nonstatic, and that this compromises even the simplest inverse rendering tasks. The reason
is that these implementations do not conform to the theory, accounting only for a subset
of the newly arising derivatives. When including the “missing term”, we find that the
parameterization of the rendering integral matters: only surface integrals lead to a simple, local formulation of derivatives, i.e., one in which changes in geometry do not affect
the entire path. A path replay implementation based on our derivation leads to systematic convergence to the reference solution in the test settings, and provides efficient and
unbiased estimates of the interior derivatives.
5.1
Rederiving the Differential Radiance Equations
In physically-based rendering, one is interested in differentiating the rendering equation [Kajiya, 1986] (Section 2.1.2) with respect to an arbitrary scene parameter 𝜃 . Here,
we explicitly allow 𝜃 to affect scene geometry, such that, for example, it may alter the
shape of surfaces. While the case of non-static geometry is often discussed in the context of handling discontinuous visibility, this is orthogonal to our main aim. A scene may
well have occlusion boundaries that do not affect the integrand, or that only marginally
contribute to the derivatives when the geometry changes. For now, we assume such
scenes and defer a discussion of discontinuities.
The central observation by Nimier-David et al. [2020] and Stam [2020] is that the
derivatives for 𝜃 can be explicitly computed by another form of light transport simulation
involving differential radiance 𝜕𝜃 𝐿. This leads to radiative backpropagation (also see
Section 2.4.3): the gradient of an objective function 𝜕𝜃 L can be computed by measuring
differential radiance with the adjoint radiance 𝜕L
𝜕𝐿 during the simulation. The equations
for differential radiance were originally derived under the assumption of static geometry,
so in the following, we rederive them without this assumption.
5.1.1
Differential Scattering
Differential radiance is governed by differential scattering, which is obtained by differentiating both sides of the rendering equation (2.4):

• Term 2: Differential radiance scatters like normal radiance according to the BSDF 𝑓
• Term 3: Differential radiance is emitted when the BSDF changes with 𝜃 (e.g. if 𝜃
affects the albedo of a surface).
This equation remains unchanged and is exactly the one by Nimier-David et al. [2020].
5.1.2
Differential Transport
The transport from one surface to another is described by differential transport, which is
obtained by differentiating the transport equation (2.6):
𝜕𝜃 𝐿i (p, 𝝎) = 𝜕𝜃 𝐿o (r(p, 𝝎), −𝝎).
(5.2)
This is the final form presented by Nimier-David et al. [2020], in which case differential
radiance would be transported just like normal radiance. However, this misses a subtle
relation: when geometry is non-static, the intersection point r(p, 𝝎, 𝜃 ) can depend on 𝜃 .
Notice that the points p and r are to be understood as points in a (hypothetical) global
parametrization M ⊂ R2 of the surface, which is embedded in the ambient space R3 . We
denote the mapping from a point in the parameterization p ∈ M to the ambient space
by 𝑆 (p, 𝜃 ) ∈ R3 , which naturally depends on the geometry. A change of the intersection
point r ∈ M with 𝜃 can now arise in two ways: (1) the surface at p evolves in the ambient
space, such that the origin 𝑆 (p, 𝜃 ) of the ray cast to determine r moves; or (2) the surface
at r evolves in ambient space.
Zeltner et al. [2021] have previously investigated a combination of radiative backpropagation and non-static geometry with reparameterization-based discontinuity handling [Loubet et al., 2019; Bangaru et al., 2020]. However, they only transform the differential scattering equation (5.1) to account for the reparameterization, but do not consider
the relation above and its implications on the derivatives.
The potential movement of the intersection point with 𝜃 must be considered when
differentiating the transport equation (5.2). Applying the multivariate chain rule to the
equation, with an explicit dependency on 𝜃 for completeness, yields:

where 𝜃 0 = 𝜃 . Differential radiance is not just transported like normal radiance (first
term) but differential radiance is also generated through the transport (second term).
For example, consider an area light source with an emission texture, and let 𝜃 be the
position of the light. At each point on the light source, the emission is spatially varying
because of the texture (𝜕p𝐿o ≠ 0), and the intersection point of a ray with the light
source can depend on 𝜃 (𝜕𝜃 r ≠ 0). Therefore, any path segment that terminates on the
light could generate differential radiance. Importantly, transport between any surfaces,
including non-emissive ones, could generate differential radiance:
Differential radiance is emitted through transport from and to geometry that moves
with 𝜃 .
To see why, we differentiate the scattering equation (2.4) w.r.t. the position p

Term C
Given that 𝜃 affects the intersection point, i.e., 𝜕𝜃 r ≠ 0, the three terms in this equation
have the following interpretation regarding the product 𝜕p𝐿o 𝜕𝜃 r from Equation (5.3)
• Term A: Differential radiance is emitted by the transport from light sources if their
emission is spatially varying (e.g., an area light with an emission texture).
• Term B: Differential radiance is emitted by the transport from surfaces if the incoming radiance 𝐿i is spatially varying.
• Term C: Differential radiance is emitted by the transport from surfaces if their BSDF
is spatially varying (e.g., a surface with an albedo texture).
Term B has a recursive nature, revealed by differentiating the transport equation, where
p0 has the value of p:
𝜕p𝐿i (p, 𝝎) = 𝜕p𝐿o (r(p0, 𝝎), −𝝎)𝜕p r(p, 𝝎).
(5.5)
In practice, the incident radiance at every surface point will almost surely be spatially
varying as radiance is reflected towards a point from various other surfaces: as soon
as scene geometry moves with 𝜃 , differential radiance is generated through transport,
regardless of whether there are spatially varying BSDFs or emission.
Therefore, differential transport as presented by Nimier-David et al. [2020] (Eq. (5.2))
only holds if the geometry is static.
5.1.3
Case Study
We observe that existing implementations of radiative backpropagation consider only
Terms A and C and omit Term B. Consequently, the geometry derivatives will be biased,
5.1. Rederiving the Differential Radiance Equations

Figure 5.1: Three scenes with a backward-moving rectangle – spatially-varying emission
(first row), spatially varying BSDF (second row), and indirect illumination (third row)
– are rendered (first column). The derivatives of the red channel are displayed: finite
differences (FD) (second column), PRB (third column), and the PRB variant of projective
sampling (fourth column). All methods based on radiative backpropagation (prb*) as
implemented in Mitsuba 3 (version 3.6.4) compute incorrect, zero derivatives for the last
setting. Automatic differentiation (ad) yields correct derivatives (last column).

Chapter 5. Radiative Backpropagation with Non-Static Geometry
even for surfaces directly observed by a camera. We demonstrate this behavior for the different RB-based integrators in
Mitsuba 3 [Jakob et al., 2022b] (version 3.6.4) using a set of simple examples. In the first setting (inset, top) the camera observes
a slab with a spatially varying emission, which is moved away
from the camera by 𝜃 . In the second setting (inset, middle) the
camera observes the same slab, which now has a spatially varying diffuse albedo and is illuminated from below. In the third setting (inset, bottom), the moving slab has a constant albedo but
is illuminated from below by another slab with spatially varying emission. Even though geometry is allowed to move, the
integrand is free of discontinuities in the first setting, and discontinuities only marginally contribute to the derivative in the
last two settings (the bottom slab is much larger than shown).
For the derivatives w.r.t. 𝜃 , one must account for Term A in the first setting and Term
C in the second setting. Indeed, Mitsuba 3’s RB integrators compute these derivatives
(Figure 5.1, rows 1 and 2). In the third setting, Term B has a noticeable contribution:
the observed slab is illuminated by a spatially varying light, so the incident radiance
changes when moving from one point on the surface to another (𝜕p𝐿i ≠ 0 on the first
slab). The RB integrators do not account for this (Figure 5.1, last row). This even affects
the integrators designed for geometry optimization, such as prb_projective, which
implements RB-style projective sampling [Zhang et al., 2023].
On a technical level, the reason why the implementation considers Terms A and C,
even though the existing theory misses the transport-related differential radiance, is that
the intersection point is attached, which means gradient tracking with respect to the intersected surface is enabled. Since the emission at the intersection and the BRDF are
evaluated with an attached intersection point, the subsequent reverse accumulation automatically includes 𝜕p𝐿e 𝜕𝜃 r (Term A) and 𝐿i 𝜕p 𝑓 𝜕𝜃 r (Term C). However, the ray defining
the next path segment is detached from the intersection point, so changes in incident
radiance are not included (Term B).
PRB and automatic differentiation The result obtained with path replay backpropagation (PRB) [Vicini et al., 2021] (prb), the most common variant of radiative backpropagation, should match automatic differentiation (ad, with detached sampling (Section 5.2.1)) by design, but it does not in the last setting (Fig. 5.1). The reason is that automatic differentiation includes Term B, which closely matches finite differences. This
is not an issue of replaying paths but of the underlying theory for static geometry.
5.2
Non-Static Geometry and Detached Sampling
Radiative backpropagation is typically considered with detached sampling, i.e., the sampling process and probability densities in Monte Carlo estimators are not differentiated.
Vicini et al. [2021] show that this leads to a simple, local approach to differentiation that
does not need to consider variations of path geometry with the scene parameters.
5.2. Non-Static Geometry and Detached Sampling
89
However, the newly derived transport law (Section 5.1.2) reveals that the relevant
derivatives are only local if the geometry is static, which significantly complicates potential implementations. Surprisingly, a truly local approach can be derived even for
non-static geometry, but, as we will show in Section 5.2.2, this requires a theory for detached sampling of positions in the three-point form. This approach allows for a simple
and efficient implementation using the path replay framework [Vicini et al., 2021].
We believe that the lack of a theory for the non-static case has led to current implementations mixing the different perspectives, without consistently following either one,
leading to the behavior observed in Section 5.1.3.
In conclusion, we make two central observations that, to the best of our knowledge,
are absent in the current literature:
1. Section 5.2.1: With non-static geometry, global path geometry must be considered
for direction sampling even with detached sampling; Vicini et al. [2021] state that this
is a unique to attached sampling, but this is only true if geometry is static.
2. Section 5.2.2: When taking the perspective of the three-point form, detached sampling makes the approach local, but one must correctly account for the (derivatives
of the) transformation determinants and the intersection point.
5.2.1
Detached Sampling of Directions
Vicini et al. [2021] introduce the notion of “attached” and “detached” sampling for importance sampling of an integral

Important and maybe surprising: both attached and detached strategies are valid estimators of the derivative as long as all points with 𝜕𝜃 𝐿 ≠ 0 are sampled with nonzero
probability and the integrand is free of Dirac deltas [Zeltner et al., 2021].
Non-static geometry moves a path The
samples generated by the mapping 𝑇 are directions and therefore, in detached sampling,
𝝎 0 = 𝑇 (u, 𝜃 0 ) is a direction independent of 𝜃 .
If directions along a path are sampled independent of any parameter, then the movement of
intersected geometry with (another) parameter
𝜃 can lead to the movement of successive path vertices: assume p is an intersection point
on a surface that moves with 𝜃 . Since the sampled direction 𝝎 0 is constant, the next intersection point r(p, 𝝎 0 ) of a ray originating from p into direction 𝝎 0 will depend on 𝜃 ,
because 𝜃 affects the ray origin in ambient space 𝑆 (p, 𝜃 ). As the next intersection point
depends on 𝜃 , the ones after will as well by recursion (see inset).
Computing the non-local term The connection between the derivative and global
path geometry is captured by Term B in Eq. (5.4) and its recursion in Eq. (5.5), which,
as shown in Section 5.1, is not accounted for by existing implementations. Strictly following the theory of detached direction sampling, one can recover the missing contribution. This leads to a construction very similar to attached sampling, where global path
geometry is relevant, with the main difference being that the probability density and the
sampled direction are not differentiated (see Appendix B, Section B.1).

Contribution of individual samples While it may seem counterintuitive, the derivatives account for the spatially varying albedo and emission in the settings from Section 5.1.3, despite samples being fixed locations on the surface. Take, for example, Setting 2, where the camera observes an object with spatially varying albedo moving with 𝜃 :
when sampling directions, the derivative of a single sample w.r.t. 𝜃 captures the changing
albedo, e.g., from red to green, through the product of 𝐿i 𝜕p 𝑓 (Term C in 𝜕p𝐿o , Eq. (5.4))
and 𝜕𝜃 r (Eq. (5.3)). When sampling positions, the albedo at a single sample is constant,

but its derivative w.r.t. 𝜃 considers the weighting of it through the product of 𝐿i 𝜕𝝎 𝑓 (part
of 𝜕𝝎 𝐿o from Eq. (5.16)) and 𝜕𝜃 [𝑆 (p0, 𝜃 ) → 𝑆 (p, 𝜃 )], as well as through the derivative of
the Jacobian determinant 𝜕𝜃 𝐷. The combination of all sample derivatives then yields a
valid estimate of the differential integral.
Notice that this also leads to different noise patterns and variance, making equal
sample counts not necessarily comparable.

Pseudocode for path replay backpropagation The most common variant of radiative backpropagation is path replay backpropagation (PRB) [Vicini et al., 2021]: it
“replays” light paths from the forward simulation during the simulation of differential
radiance, or, in the case of gradients, its measurement with adjoint radiance.
In Listing 1 we provide pseudocode for detached path replay backpropagation in the
three-point form, following the style of the listings by Vicini et al. [2021]. The function
sample_path computes the radiance 𝐿 measured by a path that originates from the
camera at position pcam in direction 𝝎 (this direction is derived from a sample on the
sensor). The radiance 𝐿 and the adjoint radiance 𝛿𝐿 (the derivative of a loss function
w.r.t. 𝐿) are then passed to sample_path_adjoint to retrieve the derivative of the loss
with respect to 𝜃 . The functions are defined as follows:
• intersect returns the intersection point of a ray with the scene as a point in the
surface parametrization M
• to_ambient transforms a point from the parametrization to ambient space R3 , considering the scene parameters
• detach disables derivative tracking for the given variable
• reparam_det returns the value of the reparameterization determinant 𝐷
• sample_bsdf samples a direction from the current BSDF and also returns the PDF
value of the sample
5.3. Implementation and Experiments
93
• eval_bsdf(p, 𝝎 𝑜 , 𝝎 𝑖 ) computes the cosine-weighted BSDF 𝑓 (p, 𝝎 𝑜 , 𝝎 𝑖 )|n(p) ⊤ 𝝎 𝑖 |
𝜕𝑔
𝜕𝑓 𝜕𝑔
• backward_grad(𝑓 , 𝜕𝑓 ) uses reverse-mode differentiation to evaluate 𝜃 𝜕𝑓
The code has two notable differences from the original, which uses detached sampling
of directions.
First, each sample 𝑇 (u𝑖 , 𝜃 0 ) is a surface point p𝑖 in the 2D parameter space M, independent of 𝜃 . For BSDF importance sampling, this means that one first samples a direction (sample_bsdf(...)), then intersects the next surface but does not differentiate the
operation (detach(intersect(...))). The surface point is transformed back into the
ambient space R3 using the mapping 𝑆 (p, 𝜃 ) (to_ambient(...)); this transformation is
differentiated because it potentially depends on 𝜃 (the sample position in R3 follows if 𝜃
moves the corresponding shape).
Second, the Jacobian determinant 𝐷 from the reparameterization must be differentiated as it might depend on 𝜃 . The BSDF importance sampling PDF contains the factor 𝐷1 ,
but it is detached, so the derivative of their product is generally non-zero (c.f. Eq. (5.18)).
By the product rule, the derivative 𝜕𝜃 𝐷 must be multiplied by 𝐿 and, for reverse-mode
differentiation, also by the adjoint radiance 𝛿𝐿. This leads to another gradient contribution. In sample_path, the product of 𝐷 and 𝐷1 simply cancels, so it can be omitted.
5.3
Implementation and Experiments
We have implemented detached sampling in the three-point form (Section 5.2.2) in Mitsuba 3 [Jakob et al., 2022b] as an automatic differentiation variant (ad_threepoint)
and a path replay variant (prb_threepoint). We have also implemented detached sampling of directions (Section 5.2.1), but only an automatic differentiation variant (ad). All
integrators use multiple importance sampling, combining BSDF and emitter samples.
5.3.1
Case Study – Revisited
We first revisit the case study from Section 5.1.3 and test our integrators in the three settings (Fig. 5.2). In contrast to existing implementations (recall Fig. 5.1), the integrators
based on the three-point form produce non-zero derivatives in the last setting, which
not only agree with the finite difference reference, but PRB and AD match exactly, consistent with the theory. Both, direction and point sampling produce similar results that
differ, for example, by the noise pattern. One reason is that individual samples contribute
differently to the derivative in both forms, so equal sample counts are not necessarily
comparable (see Section 5.2.2, Paragraph Contribution of individual samples).
The unbiased solution (prb_threepoint) has an overhead over the biased solution
(prb), which, however, is expected: the AD graph of a single loop iteration is more
complex as the previous and next surface intersections are now considered.
