5.3. Implementation and Experiments
97
“discontinuity” depends solely on the integration domain: when integrating over directions, the radiance collected along a sampled direction can change discontinuously as an
object moves into or out of the ray. When integrating over surface points, the collected
radiance can change discontinuously as the visibility of a surface point changes. The
two perspectives are not generally interchangeable, and a scene with no discontinuities
in one form may have discontinuities in the other. For example, we initially suggested
that the first case study scene with a moving slab covering the camera’s field of view was
free of discontinuities (Section 5.1.3), but this is only true for the spherical integral over
directions. In fact, all scenes from Section 5.1.3 have discontinuities in the three-point
form, which leads to bias in the results shown in Fig. 5.2.
We have modified all scenes to be free of discontinuities in the three-point form,
which is achieved by reducing the size of the slab and the emitter, making the slab fully
visible to the camera and the emitter fully visible to each point on the slab. In these
settings, the three-point form yields unbiased derivatives (Fig. 5.3). However, Settings
2 and 3 now have discontinuities when sampling the (hemi)sphere, which noticeably
affects the result of the AD integrator (ad) based on detached sampling of directions
(Section 5.2.1). The projective sampling integrator is susceptible to the same discontinuities (see Setting 2).
5.3.3
Radiative Backpropagation for Interior Path Derivatives
Although we have completely ignored discontinuities in the derivations so far, it is
straightforward to combine our approach with discontinuity handling using the framework of path-space differentiable rendering. Zhang et al. [2020] show that the derivatives
of the path-space integral, i.e., the integral over the product spaces of surface integrals,
can be expressed as the sum of an interior term and a boundary term. Our approach for
detached path replay backpropagation in the three-point form (Section 5.2.2) computes
an unbiased estimate of the interior term in the so-called material-form parameterization,
so combining it with discontinuity handling is as simple as choosing a suitable method
for the boundary term and adding the result to our estimate. Previous implementations
attempting to combine the boundary term with radiative backpropagation compute an
incorrect interior term, based on direction sampling, which manifests as severe bias with
inverted derivative signs (Fig. 5.4).
A simple application Despite the presence of discontinuities, the interior component
is central for convergence, even in the simplest inverse rendering applications: we test
implementations on a monocular pose estimation task, where the orientation and position of an object are to be inferred from a single image. The objective is to minimize
the mean absolute error between a target image and the rendering. Current implementations combining a radiative backpropagation-style interior term with the boundary term
(here estimated with projective sampling [Zhang et al., 2023]) often fail to converge in
the given time, whereas our three-point approach converges successfully, even without
explicit discontinuity handling (Fig. 5.5).

Combining boundary and RB interior derivatives As a proof-of-concept, we have
implemented a Mitsuba 3 integrator that combines projective sampling [Zhang et al.,
2023] with our RB interior derivatives (Fig. 5.6). While the combination of derivatives
matches the finite difference reference more faithfully, the result is still slightly biased.
A decomposition into the interior and boundary contributions reveals that a systematic
bias seems to originate from the boundary term, estimated with the official implementation of projective sampling in Mitsuba 3 (see the area around the teapot lid knob in
the Twist scene in Fig. 5.6). It is unclear, whether this is a conceptual issue or related to
the implementation. However, since the boundary term is unrelated to the findings in
this chapter and beyond its scope, we defer further investigation to future work.
5.4
Conclusion
The original derivation of radiative backpropagation assumed static geometry with spherical integrals. We have complemented the theory by deriving the differential radiance
equations for non-static geometry and different parameterizations of the rendering integral. A key observation is that differential radiance is not only transported like normal
radiance, but differential radiance is also emitted from moving geometry. Depending on
the parameterization of the rendering integral, this emission term becomes non-local, so
where previously derivatives could simply be computed from local path geometry, the
motion of the entire light path must now be taken into account. We have shown that the
derivatives become local when the rendering integral is reparameterized over surfaces,
leading to a practical implementation for non-static geometry.
Impact We have shown that the prb and prb_projective integrators in Mitsuba 3
(up to and including version 3.6.4) are biased if geometry is non-static. The deprecated
reparameterization integrator prb_reparam is very likely to be affected as well because
it builds on the findings by Zeltner et al. [2021]: they arrive at a local formulation of
derivatives, which does not correctly account for differential transport. Results that rely
on the affected integrators (e.g., for academic publications) should likely be revalidated.
Physically-based renderers without radiative backpropagation, such as redner [Li
et al., 2018b] (based on work by Li et al. [Li et al., 2018a]) or psdr-jit [Yan et al., 2020], are
unaffected by this bias. While our implementation of radiative backpropagation is based
on Mitsuba 3, we believe it also provides a useful reference point for other frameworks.
In Chapter 6, we extend path replay backpropagation to differentiable time-resolved
rendering. Since path geometry determines the transport time of energy, the time derivatives must correctly account for perturbations caused by non-static surfaces. This is
accomplished using PRB in the three-point form, as presented in Section 5.2.2.

Figure 6.1: Consider the scene on the left with a single spherical emitter. In the acoustic setting, the emitter radiates sound energy, whose time-dependent transport can be
simulated using path-tracing techniques in accordance with geometric acoustics. In
contrast to light simulation, the energy at a single frequency is integrated over the entire spatial domain but resolved in time, resulting in an energy time curve (ETC) (bottom
center). The resulting energy spectrogram (top right) shows the ETC for each frequency
as a heat map. We take advantage of the analogy between light and sound and extend efficient differentiable physically-based rendering to the time domain, allowing us
to compute differential ETCs (bottom right). The differential ETCs show the effect of
translating the sound source towards the microphone.
A central but subtle assumption underlying the previous chapters has been that of a
steady state: the distribution of energy in the scene has reached an equilibrium and
does not change over time. Hence, energy transport can be assumed to happen instantaneously, such that simulations may safely ignore the finite speed of light. In practice,
however, transport time cannot be disregarded in all settings, for example, for the simulation of time-of-flight cameras. This leads to so-called transient rendering [Smith et al.,
2008], where energy is not only resolved spatially over a sensor but also in time.
Similar to the steady-state case, there has been a recent surge in methods that use
differentiable rendering for solving inverse problems in transient rendering [Plack et al.,
2023; Choi et al., 2023; Malik et al., 2025]. While many differentiable renderers are
special-purpose, for example, with a three-bounce assumption [Plack et al., 2023; Choi
et al., 2023], others have adopted physically-based differentiable rendering in the tran-
104
Chapter 6. Time-Resolved Path Replay Backpropagation
sient setting [Yi et al., 2021; Wu et al., 2021]. These physically-based renderers are general and correctly handle discontinuities in transient path integrals, but efficiency is lacking: the systems by Yi et al. [2021] and Wu et al. [2021] use (forward-mode) automatic
differentiation on the CPU, which is only suitable for a few parameters and simple applications. Yet, the practical implications of this limitation are unclear since (1) efficient,
specialized renderers seem to suffice for many applications, and (2) inverse transient
rendering remains relatively niche, measured by the size of the field.
If we look beyond graphics and the simulation of light, we find other areas that would
greatly benefit from general and efficient tools for time-resolved differentiable rendering. One of those is room acoustics, a subfield of acoustics that is concerned with sound
propagation in (partially enclosed) rooms. Since sound travels much slower than light,
numerical simulations in (room) acoustics naturally consider the propagation time. On
a technical level, these simulations are closely related to physically-based rendering in
graphics: sound is often assumed to propagate in straight lines, following the principles
of geometric acoustics, while ignoring wave phenomena like diffraction or interference.
Hence, among other approaches, sound propagation in room acoustics is simulated by
solving a (time-dependent) rendering equation with path tracing [Siltanen et al., 2007;
Kiminki, 2005; Schröder, 2011; Cao et al., 2016] (Section 6.1). Despite the limitations,
geometric acoustics methods are widely used in room acoustics because they offer a
computationally efficient, high-frequency approximation. In contrast, accurate wavebased simulations are prohibitively expensive in memory and computation time when
simulating high frequencies in extended rooms.
As for the case of light, not only is the forward simulation of interest, but also the
solution to acoustic optimization problems. For example, a typical task in room acoustics is the optimization of speaker placement and panel geometry in concert halls to
meet certain levels of listener clarity. Surprisingly, it is still common practice to optimize model parameters manually [Labia et al., 2020] or use time-consuming genetic
algorithms [Christensen et al., 2014; Pilch, 2020]. Although differentiable acoustic rendering has recently appeared in the literature [Zhi et al., 2023; Stanziola et al., 2023], to
our knowledge, there is no general approach for differentiable acoustic path tracing.
In this chapter, we propose methods for efficient and fully-differentiable acoustic
path tracing. This is made possible by exploiting the analogy between light and sound:
we show that many of the techniques developed for (steady-state) physically-based differentiable rendering can be adapted for differentiable acoustic simulations. Our central
contribution is time-resolved path replay backpropagation (TPRB) an extension of path
replay backpropagation [Vicini et al., 2021] (Section 2.4.3) for time-resolved differentiable rendering. Similar to its steady-state counterpart, it is based on the principles of
radiative backpropagation [Nimier-David et al., 2020], and allows for efficient, reversemode differentiation with constant memory and linear time in path length. The decoupling of storage is essential when optimizing hundreds to millions of parameters because
the almost mirror-like reflectivity of many surfaces typically requires acoustic simulations with very long paths. Using existing, automatic differentiation-based systems from
transient rendering [Yi et al., 2021; Wu et al., 2021] would be infeasible in this setting.
The core of our time-resolved extension to path replay backpropagation is correctly accounting for variations of propagation time with scene geometry. We not only extend
6.1. Background: Acoustic Path Tracing
105
the theory of radiative backpropagation in that regard, but also use our insights from
Chapter 5 to account for the relevant derivatives in differential transport. Surprisingly,
despite handling (discretized) functions over time, the required storage can also be decoupled from the time resolution such that the algorithm has only minimal additional
overhead over the steady-state variant.
The resulting system is capable of simulating sound according to the geometric acoustics model and efficiently computing derivatives of the output energy spectrograms with
respect to arbitrary parameters of the scene, including materials, emitters, microphones,
and scene geometry. We believe that this opens up a new direction for acoustic optimization that could be as impactful for the acoustic community as differentiable rendering
was for the graphics and vision communities.
6.1
Background: Acoustic Path Tracing
The rendering equation for light transport [Kajiya, 1986] (Section 2.1.2) provides the
foundation for sound simulation in geometric acoustics, in particular, its temporal extension [Siltanen et al., 2007]. The acoustic rendering equation is derived from the analogy
between visual and acoustic concepts: camera sensors to microphones, light sources to
speakers, and images to energy spectrograms. Unsurprisingly, the equation has a form
similar to the rendering equation for light, only that the energy varies with time 𝑡:

Sensors and Measurement In acoustics simulations, the sensors are typically (omni)directional microphones and the results are stored in energy spectrograms, which hold
the energy at a time 𝑡 (x-dimension) at certain frequencies (y-dimension) (see Fig. 6.1).
Therefore, the output is two-dimensional, like images in light transport. However, the
rows of the energy spectrogram are independent because frequencies are independent in
linear systems. For simplicity, without loss of generality, we consider a single row of an
energy spectrogram, referred to as energy histogram. An energy histogram captures the
energy received at a microphone at position m as a function of time
∫
𝐻 (𝑡) =
𝑊e (𝝎 i )𝐿i (m, 𝝎 i, 𝑡) d𝝎 i,
(6.5)
S2
where 𝑊e (𝝎 i ) is a weighting function expressing the directional sensitivity of the microphone. This resembles the light transport measurement equation, except that the measurement is comparable to a single “pixel” with a time-dependent value. The weighting
allows modeling different directivity patterns, such as Subcardioid, Cardioid, Hypercardioid, or Figure of Eight. For brevity, we assume omnidirectionality in Section 6.2, i.e.,
𝑊e (𝝎 i ) = 1. The applications in Section 6.4 show experiments with directional microphones, where the weights are given by a von Mises-Fisher distribution.
6.2
Method
Going forward, we derive an efficient algorithm for differentiating acoustics simulations based on the time-dependent rendering equation introduced in Section 6.1. Consequently, these simulations may be used as part of gradient-based optimization of arbitrary objective functions. The derivation is comprised of four main parts:
1. Section 6.2.1: We establish a functional perspective that casts energy transport in
acoustics as transport of time-dependent functions; this allows treating acoustics simulations similar to scalar energy transport in light rendering.
2. Section 6.2.2: We introduce the realization of differentiable rendering in the acoustic
setting and show that the functional perspective naturally preserves some properties
of differentiable rendering for light.
3. Section 6.2.3: We differentiate the functional transport equation which leads to additional time-dependent terms unique to non-instantaneous energy transfer; we show
that the derivatives can be computed by replaying transport paths.
4. Section 6.2.4: We point out that a naive implementation based on the functional perspective has storage complexity linear in time resolution. We show that, surprisingly,
backward-mode differentiation can be performed with constant storage complexity,
independent of time resolution.
6.2. Method
6.2.1

Generally, the integrand contains discontinuities in 𝝎 that
move with 𝜃 , so that the derivative cannot simply be moved
inside the integral. In our functional framework, this is perceived by a sudden jump of the energy function along a direction 𝝎 (see inset). The same effect is observed in light
transport, where the incoming radiance abruptly changes
at geometry discontinuities. There is a recent line of work
on handling such discontinuities for light transport [Li et al.,
2018a; Loubet et al., 2019; Bangaru et al., 2020; Zhang et al., 2020] (also see Section 2.4.2).
The same techniques can be applied here because the discontinuities in the timedependent form also arise from moving geometry. Therefore, we can now assume that
the derivative can be moved into the integral, e.g., because the integration domain has
been reparameterized to follow the discontinuities [Loubet et al., 2019; Bangaru et al.,
2020]. This allows for automatic differentiation of the Monte Carlo samples to correctly
estimate the gradient. Figure 6.3 shows experimental verification for the scene in the
inset: reparameterizing the integral yields derivatives that agree with finite differences,
accounting for the energy shift to a later point in time as the first emitter moves out of
the field of view. Without handling discontinuities, the derivatives remain zero.

6.2.4
Chapter 6. Time-Resolved Path Replay Backpropagation
Backwarding with Resolution-Independent Storage
We have not yet covered how to evaluate the integral of adjoint energy 𝛿𝐻 (𝑡) and differential energy 𝜕𝜃 𝐻 (𝑡) in Eq. (6.12). Ideally, one would like to backpropagate the adjoint
energy during an adjoint pass in the style of radiative backpropagation, and we have
shown that all required quantities can be computed by replaying paths.
For light transport path replay backpropagation, Vicini et al. [2021] expand the product of adjoint radiance 𝛿𝐿 and differential radiance 𝜕𝜃 𝐿o as
∫
𝛿𝐿 · 𝜕𝜃 𝐿o = 𝛿𝐿 · 𝜕𝜃 𝐿e +
𝑓 𝛿𝐿 · 𝜕𝜃 𝐿o
(6.23)
S2
⊥
+𝜕𝜃 𝑓 𝛿𝐿 · 𝐿o d𝝎 i ,
and backpropagate at each intersection, effectively accumulating terms contributing to
𝜕𝜃 L (see their Section 4.1 for details).
The adjoint radiance 𝛿𝐿 is the derivative of the objective w.r.t. to the contribution of
a single path, derived from a small region in the adjoint image. A temporal equivalent
to 𝛿𝐿 is difficult to obtain because a single acoustic path contributes to many different
times in an energy histogram (each intersection point has a distinct travel time) and
it would be a function, not a scalar. Instead, we directly “backpropagate” the adjoint
histogram 𝛿𝐻 , shared by all paths, by expanding Eq. (6.12) using the differential transport
equation (6.15):
∫

Path Replay with Measured Energy This change does not directly lead to an efficient algorithm due to the functional perspective: so far, we have treated the transported energies as continuous functions over time, but an implementation must discretize them. A straightforward and common approach is to discretize these functions
using (not necessarily) uniformly-sized time bins, such that the energy histogram 𝐻 is
a one-dimensional array. Each Monte Carlo sample then generates a high-dimensional
quantity – the time-dependent energy – whose size depends on the time resolution. Fortunately, in the primal simulation, instead of storing one (large) array per sample, one
can directly accumulate energy into a single histogram. However, naively implementing
Eq. (6.24) for PRB-style differentiation leads to prohibitive storage requirements: to recover the incoming energy 𝐿o (𝑇 +𝜏) and differential delay energy 𝜕𝑇 𝐿o (𝑇 +𝜏) each Monte
6.2. Method
113
Carlo sample must hold two arrays for the functions 𝐿 and 𝜕𝑇 𝐿. With potentially millions of Monte Carlo samples and thousands of time bins this approach quickly becomes
infeasible. Our key insight is that one can evaluate Eq. (6.24) in a way that decouples the
storage complexity from the time resolution without approximations – storing only two
scalars per path. Underlying is the observation that, for the gradient, one does not need
to store a function 𝐿 but only its measurement ⟨𝛿𝐻, 𝐿⟩.
Similar to the initial transform, one can expand the measured energy ⟨𝛿𝐻, 𝐿o ⟩, part of
the second term in the integral in Eq. (6.24), using the primal transport equation (6.14):
∫
⟨𝛿𝐻, 𝐿o (𝑇 )⟩ = ⟨𝛿𝐻, 𝐿e (𝑇 )⟩ +
S2
𝑓 ⟨𝛿𝐻, 𝐿o (𝑇 + 𝜏)⟩ d𝝎 ⊥
i .
⏞ˉˉˉˉˉˉˉˉˉˉˉˉˉ⏟⏟ˉˉˉˉˉˉˉˉˉˉˉˉˉ⏞
(6.26)
= ⟨𝛿𝐻,𝐿i (𝑇 )⟩
The primal transport equation previously described the transport of functions, while this
equation describes the transport of scalars. Consequently, instead of collecting (functional) energy in the primal pass, it is sufficient to collect (scalar) measured energy; and
the incoming measured energy ⟨𝛿𝐻, 𝐿o (𝑇 + 𝜏)⟩ can be recovered exactly like incoming
energy, by replaying transport paths.
Using the adjoint energy 𝛿𝐻 in the primal pass may seem counter-intuitive at first
but is enabled by the structure of path replay backpropagation [Vicini et al., 2021]: the
forward simulation performs a primal pass; then, for the derivative, another primal pass
follows that decorrelates the gradient simulation from the forward simulation [Azinovic
et al., 2019], followed by the adjoint pass that replays the paths of the second primal
pass. In the gradient primal pass, the adjoint energy is known and we accumulate the
measured energy according to Equation (6.26). This is a notable difference to existing
methods: our primal pass collects a different quantity than the gradient primal pass
(energy vs measured energy).
As for the measured energy, we can expand the measured differential delay energy
⟨𝛿𝐻, 𝜕𝑇 𝐿o ⟩, part of the third term in the integral in Equation (6.24), using Equation (6.16)
∫
⟨𝛿𝐻, 𝜕𝑇 𝐿o (𝑇 )⟩ = ⟨𝛿𝐻, 𝜕𝑇 𝐿e (𝑇 )⟩ +
S2
𝑓 ⟨𝛿𝐻, 𝜕𝑇 𝐿o (𝑇 + 𝜏)⟩ d𝝎 ⊥
i ,
(6.27)
which, like differential delay energy, can be computed using forward-mode differentiation, again in the gradient primal pass.
Pseudocode Listing 2 shows the pseudocode for the algorithm: each sample in the
gradient primal pass computes two scalars, the measured energy and measured differential delay energy, which are then used in the adjoint pass to recover the incoming quantities (function definitions are consistent with Vicini et al. [2021]). The function backward_grad(<expr>, grad_out) computes the product between the Jacobian
of the expression <expr> and the gradient w.r.t. its output grad_out for a parameter 𝜃 . The function forward_grad(<var>, {<expr_1>, <expr_2>, ...}) computes
the Jacobians of all expressions { <expr_i> } w.r.t. the variable <var> and the function
detach(<var>) detaches <var> from the AD graph.
114
Chapter 6. Time-Resolved Path Replay Backpropagation
Listing 2 Pseudocode of temporal PRB with time resolution independent storage.
We run sample_path first to get the total measured energy 𝛿𝐻𝐿 and total measured differential delay energy 𝛿𝐻 𝜕𝑇 𝐿 for a sample; then compute the gradient in
sample_adjoint_path.
def sample_path(ray, 𝛿𝐻 ):
𝛿𝐻𝐿 = 0, 𝛽 = 1, 𝑇 = 0, 𝛿𝐻 𝜕𝑇 𝐿 = 0
for i in range(1, N+1):
𝑇 += time_on_segment(. . . )
# Measured energy
𝛿𝐻𝐿 += 𝛽 * ⟨𝛿𝐻, 𝐿e (𝑇 , . . . )⟩
𝝎 ′ , bsdf_value, bsdf_pdf = sample_bsdf(. . . )
# Measured differential delay energy
𝛿𝐻 𝜕𝑇 𝐿 += 𝛽 * forward_grad(𝑇 , ⟨𝛿𝐻, 𝐿e (𝑇 , . . . )⟩)
𝛽 *= bsdf_value / bsdf_pdf
return 𝛿𝐻𝐿, 𝛿𝐻 𝜕𝑇 𝐿
def sample_path_adjoint(ray, 𝛿𝐻𝐿, 𝛿𝐻 𝜕𝑇 𝐿, 𝛿𝐻 ):
𝛽 = 1, 𝑇 = 0
for i in range(1, N+1):
𝜏 = time_on_segment(. . . )
𝑇 += detach(𝜏)
# Backpropagate from "standard" terms
𝛿𝐻 𝐸 = 𝛽 * ⟨𝛿𝐻, 𝐿e (𝑇 , . . . )⟩
𝛿𝐻𝐿 -= 𝛿𝐻 𝐸
𝛿𝜃 += backward_grad(𝛿𝐻 𝐸, 1)
𝝎 ′ , bsdf_value, bsdf_pdf = sample_bsdf(. . . )
𝛿𝜃 += backward_grad(bsdf_value, 𝛿𝐻𝐿 / bsdf_value)
# Backpropagate from time-dependent term
𝛿𝜃 += backward_grad(𝜏, 𝛿𝐻 𝜕𝑇 𝐿)
𝛿𝐻 𝜕𝑇 𝐿 -= 𝛽 * forward_grad(𝑇 , ⟨𝛿𝐻, 𝐿e (𝑇 , . . . )⟩)
𝛽 *= bsdf_value / bsdf_pdf
return 𝛿𝜃
6.3. Implementation and Validation
115
Continuous Emitter Curves So far, we have not made any assumptions about the
form of the emission curve 𝐿e (x, 𝝎,𝑇 , 𝑡), which is the time-dependent energy radiating from an emitter, except that it needs to be differentiable with respect to time. In
acoustic simulations, in particular when measuring the impulse response of a system,
the emission is assumed to be a Dirac delta, which is constant over the emitter surface
and independent of direction 𝐿e (x, 𝝎,𝑇 , 𝑡) = 𝛿 (𝑡 − 𝑇 ). In this slightly informal notation,
𝛿 (𝑡 −𝑇 ) can be thought of as a single peak located at 𝑇 . The energy histogram 𝐻 can be
interpreted as the energy time curve (ETC), which is a time-discrete and squared version
of the room impulse response. The ETC is the basis for room acoustic metrics used to
assess a room’s acoustic qualities (see Appendix C.3). With Dirac delta emission, however, it is impossible to track changes in energy that arise from moving geometry: the
differential delay energy in Equation (6.16) simply evaluates to zero, because 𝜕𝑇 𝐿e (𝑇 , 𝑡)
vanishes almost everywhere.
We propose instead to use Gaussian emission curves of the form
)︃
(︃
(𝑡 − 𝑇 ) 2
.
(6.28)
𝐿e (x, 𝝎,𝑇 , 𝑡) = exp
𝜎2
This assumption is not only key for differentiation but also grounded in reality: a sound
emission always has a nonzero duration and the emitted energy does not reach its maximum instantaneously. The differential emission 𝜕𝑇 𝐿e (𝑇 , 𝑡) for Gaussian curves is defined
and continuous; this allows to account for changes in transport time when computing
the differential energy.
Gaussians offer an additional practical advantage over arbitrary emission profiles:
the integral of ⟨𝛿𝐻, 𝐿e (𝑇 )⟩ considers the entire time domain, but, while Gaussians have
global support, the contribution beyond a given threshold (e.g. four standard deviations) is neglegible and may be ignored in practice. Effectively, all time integrals for
the measured quantities are localized because the emission curves and their derivative
have (approximately) local support. In practice, we use a very narrow Gaussian with a
standard deviation of 0.25 time bins and therefore still interpret the result as an ETC.
6.3
Implementation and Validation
Our method is implemented on top of Mitsuba 3 [Jakob et al., 2022b], a system for differentiable light transport simulation. We found that most of its components are readily
suitable for the simulation of sound, such as the different shapes and emitters. We implemented a custom sensor, a (possibly omni-)directional Microphone, and a custom film,
a Tape. We model point sources using small spherical (omnidirectional) area emitters;
directional patterns to model speaker membranes are left to future work.
The core of our method is implemented in three custom integrators:
1. AcousticPRB implements time-resolved path replay backpropagation for efficient
gradient estimation of scene parameters using the three-point form parametrization
(see Chapter 5 and Appendix C). It does not handle visibility discontinuities, so the
gradients will be biased when scene parameters change the visibility between surfaces.

Figure 6.4: Energy time curves of the simulated “shoebox” room (top), shown with increasing absorption coefficient 𝛼 and a uniform scattering coefficient of 𝑠 = 0.5. Our
simulation shows good agreement with a path tracing simulation in the software Raven.
2. AcousticAD uses conventional automatic differentiation of an acoustic rendering integrator with detached sampling of surface points (the computed gradients are equivalent to AcousticPRB).
3. AcousticADReparam uses automatic differentiation and includes a reparameterization [Loubet et al., 2019; Bangaru et al., 2020] to correctly account for derivatives of
visibility discontinuities. This is the only integrator that can handle discontinuities.
If not stated otherwise, we use the AcousticPRB integrator. The experiments were
performed on an Intel Core i9-14900KF workstation with 32 GB RAM and a NVIDIA
GeForce RTX 4090. All experiments use Adam [Kingma and Ba, 2017] for optimization.
6.3.1
Verification of Primal Simulation
Before we investigate the derivatives of acoustic simulations, we validate our forward
simulation by comparing its output to Raven [Schröder, 2011], a widely-used simulation
software for room acoustics. The test scene is a “shoebox”, commonly used in acoustic
simulations (Fig. 6.4, top), with a single emitter and an omnidirectional sensor. To ensure
comparability with Raven, the walls are modeled with the classical acoustic BSDF (Appendix C, Eq. (C.7)), where uniform values are assigned to the absorption and scattering
coefficients of all surfaces. The absorption coefficient 𝛼 is varied within the range of 0.1
to 0.9, while 𝑠 spans from 0 to 1.
For each combination of absorption and scattering coefficients, we obtain the energy
time curve from the rendered energy impulse response and subsequently calculate the
reverberation time 𝑇60 and clarity index 𝐶 80 (Appendix C.3).
6.3. Implementation and Validation

Derivative

Figure 6.5: Moving reflector scene, see inset in Section 6.3.2. Our AcousticPRB integrator, based on time-resolved path replay backpropagation (TPRB), correctly handles
time derivatives along the path, matching those computed with finite differences.
Fig. 6.4 shows the energy time curves for one scattering coefficient. The simulations show good agreement with Raven, with reverberation time differences below 8 %,
averaging 2 %, and 𝐶 80 differences below 1.5 dB, averaging 0.34 dB. In addition to path
tracing, Raven uses the image source model (ISM) [Vorländer, 2020, Ch. 11.4] to simulate ideal specular reflections. When the image source model is disabled, the average
differences decrease significantly (𝑇60 : 0.33 %, 𝐶 80 : 0.06 dB). This is no surprise, as Raven
and our implementation now rely on the same physical model.
Nevertheless, the largest deviations are in the range of the just noticeable differences
(JNDs): the ISO 3382-1 [2009] standard reports JNDs of 1 dB for 𝐶 80 , and 5 % for perceived
reverberance (although more recent work suggests that the JND of the reverberation
time might be higher [Blevins et al., 2013]).
6.3.2
Validation of Derivatives
We now move on to a verification and exploration of our differentiation algorithm, timeresolved path replay backpropagation, as this is the main focus of the chapter.
Propagation of Time Derivatives
We first validate that variations in transport time resulting from non-static geometry are correctly handled
by our algorithm. To show that the temporal derivatives not only account for variations locally but also
correctly incorporate multiple bounces, we use a simple scene with three reflecting surfaces and an emitter: the scene parameter 𝜃 translates the reflector positioned next to the microphone away from the microphone, increasing the distance 𝑑 1 (see inset). To compute the derivative 𝜕𝜃 𝐻 (𝑡), the
change in 𝑑 1 must be “propagated” through the second, third, and fourth bounce to the
emitter. Indeed, Fig. 6.5 shows that the derivative computed with AcousticPRB is identical to finite differences, demonstrating that the algorithm correctly handles shifts of
propagation time across multiple bounces.

Figure 6.6: Variance of the energy histogram derivatives (see Section 6.3.2). The top left
image shows the ETC computed with different numbers of samples. The derivatives
w.r.t. an x-scaling of the enclosing room are computed with finite differences and our
time-resolved path replay backpropagation (TPRB) algorithm. The respective results of
the last row, i.e., with the highest sample count, are on the right.
Smooth Derivative Curves
The derivatives depicted so far are smooth curves, which, at first sight, seems surprising
if one is used to derivative images from differentiable light transport, where noise is often
clearly visible. The smoothness is easily explained: the contribution of each path in the
forward simulation is a weighted and translated Gaussian (Paragraph Continuous Emitter
Curves), so the derivative is the sum of weighted and translated Gaussian derivatives.
The variance present in acoustic derivatives becomes visible when repeating the
same computation with varying numbers of samples. We demonstrate this with an
experiment where we enclose the speaker and microphone in a cube, and the parameter 𝜃 scales the cube in 𝑥 direction (paths are limited to five bounces). We compute
derivatives with an increasing number of samples ranging from 10,000 to 49,000,000
and compare them with finite differences (Fig. 6.6). The forward simulation varies only
slightly, while our derivatives, although pointing in the right direction, vary more. This
is partially because importance sampling only considers the primal simulation, not the
derivatives [Zeltner et al., 2021]. We find that the variance increases for later time bins,
which is unsurprising: the early energy is concentrated in relatively few time bins, while
the late energy is diffusely spread across many bins, each containing fewer samples per
(nonzero) time bin. This variance is difficult to reduce because we can only increase the
total sample count, but do not directly sample in the time domain.

Time-Resolved PRB compared to AD
Theoretically, AcousticPRB should produce the same derivative as AcousticAD. To test
this, we set up a complex living room scene consisting of a single spherical emitter in one
corner and a microphone in another corner of the room. The scene is visualized from
the point-of-view of the microphone in Fig. 6.7 (right). We give each surface a random
but smoothly varying absorption and scattering spectrum over 32 different wavelengths.
The derivative is computed with respect to a single scene parameter 𝜃 (with initial value
1). The absorption spectrum of the walls is multiplied by 𝜃 and the absorption spectrum
of the floor is multiplied by 𝜃1 to get derivatives in both positive and negative directions.
As shown in Fig. 6.7, the derivatives of AcousticPRB match those of AcousticAD.

6.4
Applications
6.4.1
Optimization of Material Parameters
A common first step when building a room model for acoustic simulations is fitting the
acoustic properties of the model’s walls (absorption and/or scattering) such that the simulation matches a measurement. In this section, we demonstrate that such a task can be
efficiently performed using time-resolved path replay backpropagation, even for many
material parameters: we will minimize the mean squared error between the estimated
and the reference ETC (see Eq. (6.11)) to optimize material parameters. Since the geometry is fixed, no discontinuities occur, and AcousticPRB computes unbiased gradients.
A Simple Scene Although it may seem simple, finding the material parameters of all
six surfaces (walls, floor, and ceiling) within a shoebox scene is a challenging task when
Frequency
122
Chapter 6. Time-Resolved Path Replay Backpropagation
Target state

Figure 6.10: Optimizing many material parameters given a target spectrogram (lower
left). The scene on the top left is defined by a spherical emitter (white), a directional
microphone (red) without line-of-sight to the emitter, and 225 slabs. The scene is rendered from the perspective of the microphone before (top center) and after (top right)
optimization, by mapping the absorption spectrum of the slabs from Hz to the visual
wavelengths between 300-800 nm. The energy spectrogram after the optimization is
shown at the bottom right.
relying solely on a single measured ETC because the contributions of different surfaces
will overlap in the measurement. We use an artificial reference ETC, ensuring that any
discrepancies arise from the computed gradients rather than measurement-simulation
discrepancies and optimize the absorption factors of the walls. As shown in Fig. 6.9, our
method correctly computes gradients for the individual surface parameters and reliably
converges to the reference.
Many Coefficients To demonstrate that our implementation can handle many absorption coefficients, we set up a scene with 15 by 15 slabs, each with an absorption
spectrum defined by 37 linearly interpolated values, resulting in 15 × 15 × 37 = 8325 parameters. To avoid direct energy, the emitter and sensor do not share a line of sight. All
energy must be reflected by the slabs. As target we use an artificial energy spectrogram
representing the SIGGRAPH logo. The scene is initialized with random, high absorption coefficients. The degrees of freedom are enough to reconstruct the logo reasonably
(Fig. 6.10).
6.4.2
Optimization of Scene Geometry
Apart from selecting surface materials, room acoustic design often involves strategic
placement of reflectors, dividers, or source/listener positions. In new constructions, even

Figure 6.11: The position of an emitter in a shoebox scene is reconstructed, given a

No Discontinuity Handling The gradients of the AcousticPRB integrator are biased when geometry is non-static because it does not handle discontinuous visibility.
For light transport, it is well-known that ignoring the derivatives arising from discontinuities can severely compromise an optimization (also see Section 2.4.2). Yet, for the case
of time-resolved differentiable rendering and respective inverse problems, the implications are generally less studied. An exhaustive study is beyond the scope of this chapter,
but we will demonstrate that optimization of geometry is feasible, even without discontinuity handling. Consider a simple shoebox scene with a vertical wall at its center, an
omnidirectional receiver placed on the left side, and an omnidirectional emitter placed
on the right side, such that there is no direct line-of-sight between the two (Fig. 6.11, top
right). Given the ETC of a target state, the goal is to optimize the position of the emitter
by minimizing the mean squared distance to the target ETC (Fig. 6.11).
Despite having biased gradients, the AcousticPRB integrator successfully recovers
the target position. Its convergence behavior similar to that of the AcousticADReparam
integrator, which uses reparameterization-based discontinuity handling [Loubet et al.,
2019; Bangaru et al., 2020]. On the one hand, this can certainly be attributed to the
AcousticPRB integrator using the surface parameterization, as it is free of some discontinuities [Zhang et al., 2020] (also see Chapter 5). On the other hand, resolving the time
dimension and tracking variations of propagation time might be the dominant factor in
time-resolved optimizations – this is correctly handled by our path replay algorithm.
Optimizing Ceiling Reflector Angles We now move on to a more realistic scenario,
which addresses a common challenge in the acoustic design of rooms for speech and

1
Figure 6.12: Optimization of the ceiling reflector angles in an auditorium. The loss L
converges to a minimum and the gradients vanish as the scene converges from the initial
configuration (bottom left) to the final configuration (bottom right). The reflectors are
numbered 1 to 4, and the colors indicate the corresponding reflectors.
music: achieving both high intelligibility and clarity, while providing a uniform listener
experience. Typically, it is desirable to maximize clarity measures such as 𝐶 80 , i.e. to
maximize the energy within the first 80 ms after the direct sound, while minimizing
the variation in energy across listener positions. In concert halls, ceiling reflectors are
often used to direct sound to the audience. Finding the optimal shape and positioning of
these reflectors is not a trivial task, as the reflected sound reaches the audience not only
via first-order reflections, but also via later, higher-order reflections. We demonstrate
that our method can, in this complex setting, simultaneously optimize the angle of one
reflector positioned above the stage and three additional reflectors positioned above the
audience. The scene is a lecture hall, taken from the BRAS Benchmark [Brinkmann
et al., 2021; Aspöck et al., 2020, CR4] (Fig. 6.12, top left).
The objective function that both maximizes the clarity and minimizes the variance
across listener positions can be formulated as a joint loss function, as proposed by [IEC
60268-16, 2020]:
L (𝐸) = std(𝐸 early ) − mean(𝐸 early ).
(6.29)
This formulation assumes that the clarity depends only on the early energy 𝐸 early , which
arrives within 80 ms after the direct sound and the late energy remains unchanged. This
is a reasonable assumption since we only adjust the position of reflectors and don’t introduce additional absorptive surfaces. This allows us to restrict the maximum rendering
length, which speeds up computation times.
6.5. Conclusion
125
The optimization process, shown in Fig. 6.12, redirects early sound energy towards
the back of the audience, where the ratio between early and late reverberant energy is
comparatively low. This results in an overall increase in clarity across the audience (from
3.7 dB to 4.3 dB), while the standard deviation of early energy across listener positions
is largely maintained (increasing slightly from 0.94 dB to 0.96 dB). The most significant
improvements can be observed at individual receiver positions, where clarity increases
by up to 2.9 dB in the higher ranks, which is clearly above the just-noticeable difference
of approximately 1 dB [ISO 3382-1, 2009].
6.5
Conclusion
This chapter has transferred concepts of differentiable path tracing from light transport
to the acoustic setting, leading to time-resolved path replay backpropagation, an efficient
backpropagation algorithm for time-dependent energy transfer. It extends path replay
backpropagation [Vicini et al., 2021] to time-resolved rendering, thereby addressing an
open problem previously stated in the context of differentiable transient rendering [Yi
et al., 2021; Wu et al., 2021]. Our experiments demonstrate that differentiable path tracing of sound propagation is feasible and enables the optimization of various scene parameters, including material properties and geometry, to achieve desired acoustic characteristics in room environments. This opens up applications that appear to go significantly
beyond traditional techniques in acoustics. Our implementation can be easily extended
to model properties that are currently not supported, such as sources or receivers with
arbitrary directional characteristics.
Biased gradients An apparent limitation is that our AcousticPRB integrator produces biased gradients when visibility is affected by scene parameters. As shown in
Chapter 5, there is currently no implementation that simultaneously handles visibility
discontinuities and is PRB-based. A combination of the two will likely benefit differentiable acoustic path tracing, although we have shown that geometry optimization is still
feasible without it (Section 6.4.2).
Efficient Differentiation for Transient Rendering Our approach is connected to
differentiable transient rendering, which is commonly derived from a path space formulation [Wu et al., 2021; Yi et al., 2021]: although our formalism is based on the classical
rendering equation with a spherical integral, our implementation uses a surface integral,
whose derivatives are closely related to the interior derivatives of the path-space form.
We see an exciting avenue in combining our insights with the path-space formalism
of transient rendering, which may lead to more robust discontinuity handling in differentiable acoustic simulations on the one hand, and more efficient differentiable transient
rendering on the other hand.
Wave effects Our current implementation is based on path tracing, which ignores all
effects related to the wave nature of sound. While this is consistent with ray tracing
techniques used in acoustics, it is nonetheless a more serious simplification of physical
126
Chapter 6. Time-Resolved Path Replay Backpropagation
phenomena compared to the simulation of light transport. The literature in acoustics,
but also in graphics, has shown many efficient techniques for incorporating wave effects into the simulation [Kirsch and Ewert, 2023; Svensson and Calamia, 2006; Pohl and
Stephenson, 2014; Steinberg et al., 2024]. It will be worthwhile to explore how such approaches could be integrated into the Monte Carlo simulation framework. For improved
fidelity and simulation in arbitrary frequency bands, full spectral path tracing could be
implemented as suggested by Radziszewski et al. [2009]. Spectral rendering with arbitrary sensitivity spectra and logarithmic sampling in octave bands has already been
successfully applied to the forward rendering pass [Jüterbock et al., 2024].
Chapter 7
Neural Deferred Shading
Figure 7.1: We reconstruct an object from images by simultaneously deforming a triangle mesh and optimizing a neural shader, comparing the renderings to the input images.
The previous chapters have been mainly concerned with differentiating traditional rendering systems: Chapter 3 and Chapter 4 focused on rasterization-based rendering with
approximations from real-time graphics, whereas Chapter 5 and Chapter 6 were concerned with physically-based rendering using Monte Carlo methods to simulate energy
transport. The resulting tools are general and can be applied in a wide range of applications. However, by catering to all possible applications these tools might not be the
optimal choice in terms of effectiveness or efficiency for any single application.
For example, suppose that a set of images is given, all showing the same object from
different viewpoints. The task is to estimate its 3D geometry, i.e., a virtual representation of the surface. If one were to approach this task by using differentiable rendering
to minimize an objective that measures the difference between rendered images and
target images, then it might be reasonable to use a physically-based differentiable renderer: it is less restrictive and can model the various light transport effects observed
in general capture settings. However, since traditional rendering systems simulate (or
approximate) light transport in a virtual scene, one must know many “meta” parameters of the scene a priori: the emitter model, the number of emitters, a suitable material
model for the object, etc. Many of these parameters can be known in well-calibrated
capture setups [Schreer et al., 2019], but generally they are not. Even if one would take
an educated guess and select reasonable models, this choice and the optimized model
parameters would really only be relevant for the optimization procedure and not the
final result – after all, the only relevant output is the estimated geometry.
128
Chapter 7. Neural Deferred Shading
In that regard, inverse applications open up a design space for differentiable rendering systems that are tailored to the optimization objective. A recent example is 3D
Gaussian splatting [Kerbl et al., 2023]: even for target images showing scenes with significant geometric and visual complexity, it requires no coherent model for surfaces,
materials, or illumination; and substantial approximations in rendering suffice [Celarek
et al., 2025]. This works because the application is novel view synthesis: one is neither
interested in recovering accurate 3D geometry, nor in estimating surface materials.
The same principle can be applied in the multi-view reconstruction setting above:
instead of fixing material and illumination models to simulate their interaction, it is sufficient to model the appearance of the object, which generally is view-dependent and
spatially-varying. This idea is popular in modern 3D reconstruction pipelines because it
requires minimal prior knowledge about the material of objects or the environment in
which the input images were captured [Niemeyer et al., 2020; Yariv et al., 2020; Oechsle
et al., 2021; Li et al., 2023]. This also contrasts traditional approaches, which often rely on
photo-consistency between images [Fua and Leclerc, 1995; Furukawa and Ponce, 2010],
assuming Lambertian surfaces and constant illumination. Instead, neural networks commonly serve as a flexible representation of appearance and geometry [Niemeyer et al.,
2020; Yariv et al., 2020], both jointly optimized: one set of network weights models appearance, i.e., the interaction of geometry, material and light, and another set of (potentially shared) weights models the geometry, often as an implicit surface [Park et al., 2019;
Liu et al., 2019b]. Although fully neural approaches are general, they exhibit excessive
runtime, making them impractical for reconstruction of large object collections or 3D
reconstruction from multi-view video (e.g., of human performances [Starck and Hilton,
2007; Vlasic et al., 2009; Guo et al., 2019; Collet et al., 2015; Schreer et al., 2019]).
In this chapter, we propose neural deferred shading (NDS) an efficient differentiable
rendering approach tailored to 3D surface reconstruction from multi-view images. Our
idea is to combine the best from real-time graphics and 3D vision: represent surfaces
as triangle meshes and model appearance using neural networks. This provides a general and efficient tool for reconstruction tasks because (1) triangle meshes can be efficiently rasterized, whereas neural implicit surfaces require expensive ray marching, and
(2) the renderer can reproduce arbitrary appearance, including global illumination, without costly light transport simulations. The design is inspired by real-time rendering with
deferred shading [Deering et al., 1988]: a differentiable rasterizer generates per-pixel attributes from a triangle mesh (e.g., the world-space position), and a neural shader then
processes these attributes to generate the final color. Similar to previous work [Yariv
et al., 2020], our shader is a shallow multi-layer perceptron (MLP), a representation that
is also common for neural material models [Sztrajman et al., 2021; Kuznetsov et al., 2022;
Zeltner et al., 2024]. Although in a different context, Thies et al. [2019] and Nalbach et al.
[2017] also present pipelines for mesh rendering with neural deferred shading. However,
unlike our MLP shader, their convolutional networks can “hallucinate” colors at image
regions not covered by geometry – this interferes with the goal of optimizing geometry
to represent the observations. We use our differentiable renderer to jointly optimize a
triangle mesh and the shader in a 3D reconstruction setting, and demonstrate on a public dataset that the reconstruction accuracy is close to that of traditional baselines and
neural approaches, while significantly surpassing them in optimization runtime.
7.1. Method
7.1
129
Method
Figure 7.2: Overview of the 3D reconstruction pipeline. We use a differentiable rasterizer
to obtain per-pixel attributes from a triangle mesh and shade the results with a neural
network to synthesize an image for each input view. The shader is updated based on
the difference between rendered and input images, whereas the mesh vertices are also
updated based on the silhouette and a geometric regularization term.
Given a set of images I = {𝐼 1, · · · , 𝐼𝑛 } from calibrated cameras and corresponding
masks M = {𝑀1, · · · , 𝑀𝑛 }, we want to estimate the 3D surface of an opaque object
shown in the images. To this end, we follow an analysis-by-synthesis approach: we find
a surface that reproduces the images when rendered from the perspective of the input
views. In this work, the surface is represented by a triangle mesh G = (V, E, T ), consisting of vertex positions V, a set of edges E, and a set of faces T . Faithfully reproducing
the images via rendering requires an estimate of the surface material and illumination
if we simulate light transport, e.g., with a differentiable path tracer [Li et al., 2018a;
Nimier-David et al., 2019]. However, as initially stated, our focus is on geometry reconstruction. Therefore, we combine the interaction of material and illumination, and only
estimate appearance, which avoids restrictions imposed by opting for specific material
and light models: our rendering pipeline uses differentiable rasterization and a neural
shader, which can represent arbitrary appearance (Section 7.1.1). We solve the optimization problem using gradient descent, gradually deforming the mesh and adjusting the
weights of the neural shader using an objective function that compares renderings of the
mesh to the input images (Section 7.1.2). Fig. 7.2 shows an overview of our approach.
7.1.1
Neural Deferred Shading
Our differentiable mesh renderer follows the structure of a deferred shading pipeline
from real-time graphics: Given a camera 𝑖, the mesh is rasterized in a first pass, yielding
a triangle index and barycentric coordinates per pixel. This information is used to interpolate both vertex positions and vertex normals, creating a geometry buffer (G-buffer)
with per-pixel positions and normals. In a second pass, the G-buffer is processed by a

Separating the shading from the silhouette objective mainly has performance reasons: For a camera view 𝑖, the rasterization considers all pixels in the image, therefore
˜ 𝑖 is cheap. However, shading is more involved and requires invokcomputing the mask 𝑀

In practice, we only shade a subset of pixels inside the intersection of input and rendered masks while comparing the silhouette for all pixels. Additionally, we also limit
the number of camera views considered in each gradient descent iteration.
Geometry Regularization
Naively moving the vertices unconstrained in each iteration quickly leads to undesirable
meshes with degenerate triangles and self-intersections. We use a geometry regularization term that favors smooth solutions and is inspired by Luan et al. [2021]:
𝐿geometry = 𝐿laplacian + 𝐿normal .
(7.6)

While some prior work (e.g., [Liu et al., 2018; Luan et al., 2021]) uses El Topo [Brochu
and Bridson, 2009] for robust mesh evolution, we found that our geometric regularization sufficiently avoids degenerate vertex configurations. Without El Topo, we are unable to handle topology changes, but also avoid its impact on runtime.

7.1.3
Optimization Schedule
Our optimization starts from an initial mesh that is computed from the masks and resembles a visual hull [Laurentini, 1994]. Alternatively, it can start from a custom mesh.
Similar to prior work, we use a coarse-to-fine scheme for the geometry: Starting from
a coarsely triangulated mesh, we progressively increase its resolution during optimization. Inspired by Nicolet et al. [2021] we remesh the surface with the method of Botsch
and Kobbelt [2004], halving the average edge length multiple times at fixed iterations.
After mesh upsampling, we also increase the weights of the regularization terms by 4
and decrease the gradient descent step size for the vertices by 25 %, which we empirically
found helps to improve the smoothness for highly tessellated meshes.
Since some quantities in geometry regularization (e.g., graph Laplacian) only depend
on the connectivity of the mesh, we save time by precomputing them once after upsampling and reusing them in the iterations after.
7.2
Experiments
We have implemented our method on top of the automatic differentiation framework
PyTorch [Paszke et al., 2019] and use the Adam [Kingma and Ba, 2017] optimizer for
momentum-based gradient descent. Our differentiable rendering pipeline uses the highperformance primitives by Laine et al. [2020]. In our experiments, we run 2000 gradient
descent iterations and remesh after 500, 1000, and 1500 iterations. We randomly select
one view per iteration to compute the appearance term and shade 75 % of mask pixels.
The individual objective terms are weighted with 𝜆shading = 1, 𝜆silhouette = 2, 𝜆laplacian =
40, and 𝜆normal = 0.1. All time measurements were taken on a workstation with an Intel
Xeon 32×2.1 GHz CPU, 128 GB RAM, and an NVIDIA Titan RTX GPU with 24 GB VRAM.
7.2.1
3D Reconstruction
We demonstrate that our method can be used for multi-view 3D reconstruction. Starting
from a coarse visual hull-like mesh, it quickly converges to a reasonable estimate of
the object surface. We test our method on the DTU multi-view dataset [Jensen et al.,

Figure 7.4 shows qualitative results for two objects from the DTU dataset and Table 7.1 contains quantitative results for all objects. We used the official DTU evaluation
script to generate the Chamfer-L1 scores and benchmarked all workflows for the time
measurements (including data loading times). For IDR and our method we disabled any
intermediate visualizations.
In absolute terms (millimeters), the reconstruction accuracy of our method is close to
both the traditional baseline and the state-of-the-art neural method. Although Colmap
reconstructs many surfaces accurately, only IDR and our method properly handle regions dominated by view-dependent effects (e.g., non-Lambertian materials) and produce watertight surfaces that can remain untrimmed. When Colmap is used without
trimming, the reconstruction becomes more complete but it is less accurate than ours
for some objects. Our method is limited by the topology and genus of the initial mesh and
therefore cannot capture some geometric details that can be recovered with more flexible surface representations. We also observe that our surfaces are often not as smooth
as IDR’s and concave regions are not as prominent. The latter is potentially related to

(see Figure 7.5). We noticed that after mesh upsampling finer geometric details quickly
emerge. Thus, the fast convergence time might partially be related to the fact that we can
locally increase the geometric freedom with a finer tessellation, while IDR and similar
methods have no explicit control over geometric resolution.

Analysis of the Neural Shader
Although recovering geometry is the main focus of our work, investigating the neural
shader after optimization can potentially give insights into the reconstruction process

Interactive Novel View Synthesis Figure 7.6 shows that rendering with a trained
shader can be used for basic view synthesis, producing reasonable results for input and
novel views. Thus, the shader seems to learn a meaningful representation of appearance,
disentangled from the geometry. If desired, the view synthesis quality can be further
improved by continuing the optimization of the shader while keeping the mesh fixed.

Figure 7.7: A principal component analysis of the shader’s positional features reveals
a connection to materials. We show the projection to the two largest principal components. Replacing features in the positional latent space before computing the viewdependent color enables simple material editing. The feature vector at the yellow square
(pants material) determines regions that are replaced with the feature vector at the
green square (beard material).
The neural shader can be readily integrated into other real-time rendering pipelines
after training, enabling interactive synthesis of novel views. As a demonstration, we
have implemented a “neural” viewer that uses OpenGL to rasterize positions and normals. Then, we use OpenGL-CUDA interoperability and PyTorch to shade the buffers
with a pre-trained neural shader.

Positional Features The neural shader is a composite of two functions (Figure 7.3)
(︁
)︁
𝑓𝜽 (x, n, 𝝎 𝑜 ) = 𝑐 ℎ(x), n, 𝝎 𝑜 ,
(7.10)
where ℎ : R3 → R256 transforms points in 3D space to positional features and 𝑐 :
(R256 × S2 × R3 ) → [0, 1] 3 then extracts view-dependent colors. Both functions depend on parameters 𝜽 ℎ and 𝜽 𝑐 , respectively. To further analyze the shader, we perform
a principal component analysis of the positional features from ℎ and project them to the
two dominant components (Figure 7.7): The shader naturally learns similar positional
features for regions with similar material, despite their distance in space. Variations in illumination also seem to be encoded in the positional features because shadowed regions
have slightly different features than exposed regions of the same material.
We also investigate the behavior of the view-dependent part by replacing feature vectors in the positional latent space and then extracting colors with the function 𝑐. More
specifically, we replace all features representing one material by a feature representing
another material (Figure 7.7). The results suggest that the function 𝑐 reasonably generalizes to different view and normal directions, which in this example leads to geometric
features of the mesh still being perceivable. This allows simple material editing.
7.2.3
Ablation Studies
The method has several hyperparameters, which we ablate in the following.
Objective Function We investigate the influence of the individual terms of our objective function (Figure 7.8). Without the Laplacian term, we observe noticeable bumps
and crack-like artifacts. The normal term has a small influence but improves smoothness around edges. Without the silhouette term, the object boundaries are not properly
reconstructed. Without the shading term, the reconstructed shape resembles a smooth
visual hull, missing almost all details.

Initial Mesh We investigate how initial meshes with different resolutions, topology,
and geometric distance to the target affect the reconstruction and runtime (Figure 7.9).
Very coarse initial meshes result in missing details, while fine meshes provide too much
geometric freedom, which leads to artifacts. Since we do not support topology changes,
holes are only reconstructed if they are present in the initial geometry.
The initial meshes for all DTU objects consist of around 2000 triangles and the output
meshes of around 100000 triangles. In terms of scalability, our method handles highresolution meshes efficiently: in the last 500 iterations (with 2000 iterations in total), the
optimization runs on a mesh with the output resolution and still performs fast updates.
Positional Encoding and Network Architecture We experiment with different encodings and network sizes for the position-dependent part of the neural shader. In Figure 7.10, we show the different results using positional encoding (PE) [Tancik et al., 2020],
Gaussian Fourier features (GFF) [Tancik et al., 2020], sinusoidal activations (SIREN) [Sitz-

Chapter 7. Neural Deferred Shading

(Ours)

Figure 7.11: Different sizes of the positional part of the neural shader (number of layers

mann et al., 2020], and a standard MLP with ReLU activations.
While some of these methods can generate acceptable renderings, they do not necessarily guarantee a more detailed geometry. In particular, we observe that although the
image rendered with SIREN or GFF has sharp features, the geometric detail of the mesh
is less adequate. A possible explanation is that the network quickly overfits and compensates for geometrical inaccuracies using the appearance. Conversely, finding the correct
direction along which to move the mesh vertices might be more difficult without positional encoding. We have obtained accurate reconstructions using positional encoding
with 4 octaves, as opposed to the recommended 10 [Mildenhall et al., 2020].
In Figure 7.11, we show results for different network architectures. While there are
no dramatic differences between the results, we observe that configurations with less
than 2 layers or with more than 512 units per layer result in fewer geometric details.
7.2.4
Failure Cases
Figure 7.12 shows some failure cases of our reconstruction method. Since the reconstruction starts from a visual hull-like mesh, deep concavities require large movements of the
mesh. This movement is driven by relatively weak shading gradients, which cannot recover these concavities before the mesh becomes overly stiff (e.g., because the gradient
descent step size is reduced during remeshing). On the other hand, silhouette gradients
are larger than shading gradients, so the mesh easily “grows” to fill the masks.
We also observed that the remeshing operation sometimes produces tangled meshes
7.3. Conclusion
Deep concavities
139
Failed remeshing
Overexposed regions
Figure 7.12: Failure cases of our reconstruction approach.
for some objects. This failure case is unrecoverable and the reconstruction needs to be
restarted. The reason for this is unclear but it is likely related to the implementation of
the remeshing library [Sellán and Nicolet, 2021].
Some regions show weak structure in the majority of images for a variety of reasons,
for example, because they are overexposed. Since we randomly sample one camera view
per iteration, there is a high probability of selecting an image with low structure information for these regions. Using such a view can drive the regions’ vertices in unexpected
directions away from the actual surface. If such movement occurs at the wrong time (e.g.,
right before remeshing), the optimization often fails to correct those vertices.
7.3
Conclusion
We have presented a fast analysis-by-synthesis approach for 3D surface reconstruction
from multiple images. We jointly optimize a triangle mesh and a neural shader as representations of geometry and appearance to reproduce the input images, using a custom
differentiable renderer that combines the speed of triangle mesh rasterization with the
generality of neural rendering. Our method is comparable to state-of-the-art in reconstruction accuracy, yet is significantly faster with average runtimes below 10 minutes.
Limitations and Future Work. While triangle meshes are a simple and efficient representation, optimizing them robustly in inverse settings remains an open challenge. We
currently avoid undesirable mesh configurations (e.g., self-intersections) with carefully
weighted geometry regularizers that steer the optimization towards smooth solutions,
but this involves a compromise between rendering and regularization objectives. Nicolet et al. [2021] instead precondition the gradient to favor smooth deformations of the
mesh, which would be worth exploring in our setting. For the coarse-to-fine process,
we currently remesh the surface at fixed points during the optimization, which does not
always yield the required resolution necessary for reconstructing fine details. A promising alternative could be continuous remeshing [Palfinger, 2022]. Topology changes are
challenging for most mesh-based methods, including ours, and computationally expensive [Brochu and Bridson, 2009; Liu et al., 2018]. Hybrid approaches appear to be a
reasonable compromise: a more flexible surface representation (e.g., a density field or an
implicit surface) is used in the first part of the optimization to obtain a coarse estimate
of the scene; then a mesh is extracted and refined using differentiable mesh rendering
[Tang et al., 2023].
140
Chapter 7. Neural Deferred Shading
While neural shading is a powerful component of our system, allowing us to handle non-Lambertian surfaces and complex illumination, it is also a major obstruction
to interpretability. We experimentally provided preliminary insights into the black-box
shader but also think that a more thorough analysis is needed. Alternatively, physical
light transport models could be combined with more specialized neural components (e.g.,
irradiance or material) to isolate their effects. In this context, pre-training components
to include learned priors is a promising direction [Boss et al., 2021; Zhang et al., 2021].
Impact Our approach has since inspired several other works. For example, Walker
et al. [2024] replace the explicit vertex deformation with a neural deformation field,
which is more robust and avoids over-smoothing the solution with strong regularization
terms. Bharadwaj et al. [2023] use our framework to reconstruct avatars from monocular
video efficiently. They deform the canonical mesh vertices of a parametric face model
and replace the black-box neural shader with a physically-based shading model and neural illumination to make the avatars relightable. Algan et al. [2025] combine our pipeline
with a diffusion model to stylize and edit reconstructed meshes using text instructions.
Chapter 8
Conclusion
In this thesis, we explored different ways to improve the efficiency of differentiable rendering in terms of runtime and memory requirements. This has led to several methods
and algorithms that are not only faster but also more lightweight than previous alternatives. Efficiency is a central factor that determines whether an approach is practical in
real-world applications or not. We believe that our contributions provide tools that will
broaden the scope of differentiable rendering by enabling approaches that previously
appeared computationally infeasible.
In Chapter 3, we began with the realization that real-time rendering provides many
approximations that could be useful for differentiable rendering. In particular, global
effects like shadows have received less attention, or when they have, the attempt did
not yield derivatives that would correctly account for the variation with scene parameters. We have shown that one of the oldest approximations for shadows, shadow maps,
can be adapted to work in differentiable rendering frameworks. The central idea was to
combine pre-filtered shadow mapping, a framework for softening shadow boundaries,
and differentiable rasterization. Our approach to computing shadows maintains the efficiency of traditional shadow mapping, is differentiable, and orders of magnitude faster
than comparable techniques from physically-based differentiable rendering.
We have then continued this line of work in Chapter 4, driven by the observation that
the approach derived in Chapter 3 was merely a special case of a more general family of
approximations based on the theory of moments. A central takeaway from our previous
efforts, and many other efforts in the existing literature, was that automatically differentiating arbitrary approximations from real-time rendering does not necessarily yield
useful derivatives. Instead of relying on empirical studies to analyze the properties of
these moment-based approximations, we mathematically proved that they are, in fact,
differentiable. The careful theoretical considerations turned out to be useful on the practical side, and led to an implementation that is robust and efficient. On the application
side, the more general moment-based techniques improve over the simpler shadow mapping variant used in Chapter 3, at almost the same cost. They also provide new tools for
approximating transmittance through volumes in differentiable rendering, significantly
surpassing existing approximations in accuracy while being equally fast.
While real-time techniques are central building blocks in many practical differentiable rendering systems, they are fundamentally limited by their approximate nature,
rendering them unsuitable for applications that require precise physical accuracy. In
Chapter 5, we have hence turned our focus to physically-based differentiable rendering,
where approximations are typically avoided, so efficiency must be achieved through
other means. Previous work has shown that the algorithmic level provides significant
142
Chapter 8. Conclusion
leverage: instead of storing huge computational graphs for complex light transport simulations, reverse-mode differentiation can be performed using a different type of light
transport simulation involving differential quantities – with the same time and memory
complexity as the forward simulation. However, we demonstrated that this algorithm
does not account for variations in scene geometry, which renders it unsuitable for a
wide range of applications. We revisited the underlying theory and presented the necessary corrections to address these issues. Using a reparameterization of the rendering
integral, we arrived at a variant of the algorithm that correctly computes derivatives for
scene geometry, yet remains nearly as simple as the original formulation. As a result,
many applications can now benefit from its algorithmic advances.
While differentiable rendering has reached a mature state in computer graphics,
where many of the underlying techniques originated, other fields are only beginning
to adopt similar ideas. In Chapter 6, we remained within the physics-based realm but
turned to the simulation of sound according to the principles of geometric acoustics,
which is widely used in the subfield of room acoustics. In this setting, existing optimization approaches often rely on genetic algorithms, manual parameter tuning, or simplified differentiable simulators. We showed that many techniques developed for differentiable light transport can be transferred, which enables fully-differentiable acoustic
path tracing. A central challenge in differentiating these simulations is efficiently and
correctly treating long transport paths and scene geometry, since variations in transport
time play a fundamental role. While the algorithm introduced in Chapter 5 provides a
natural starting point, it was originally derived for steady-state transport. We therefore extended it to the time-resolved setting. Surprisingly, the resulting algorithm has
only minimal additional overhead over its steady-state counterpart. The methods and
algorithms developed in this chapter open up a new direction for acoustic optimization
that could be as impactful for the acoustics community as differentiable rendering has
been for graphics and vision. At the same time, the algorithm may also benefit graphics
applications, as it should naturally extend to time-resolved rendering of light.
Finally, in Chapter 7 we turned our attention back to graphics. A central observation is that inverse applications do not necessarily need to rely on traditional rendering
algorithms. Instead, much more efficient solutions can often be obtained by tailoring
differentiable rendering algorithms to the specific task at hand. We demonstrated this
principle at the example of 3D reconstruction, by deriving a rendering algorithm that
combines the efficiency of triangle rasterization with the expressiveness of neural rendering. This design enables efficient 3D reconstruction in environments with arbitrary
materials and illumination without explicitly simulating light transport.
8.1
Outlook
General vs. Application-Driven Approximate differentiable rendering remains highly
relevant, as demonstrated by many recent applications. Many rendering algorithms are
tailored to the requirements of a specific task, often introducing approximations motivated by the optimization objective. While such designs can be very effective and efficient in their intended setting, they may not generalize well and are therefore often
difficult to reuse in other contexts.
8.1. Outlook
143
This suggests that approximations in differentiable rendering still offer considerably
unexplored potential. Developing them more systematically could lead to techniques
that remain broadly applicable while retaining their efficiency. In this context, automatically differentiating real-time renderers is an important step [Bangaru et al., 2023], but
as demonstrated in Chapter 3 and Chapter 4, additional care is often required to ensure
that the resulting derivatives are meaningful.
Gradient Variance Since physically-based differentiable rendering relies on Monte
Carlo estimators, the gradients exhibit some form of variance. In practice, gradient-based
optimization often uses only a small number of samples per iteration, yet optimization
procedures still tend to converge. While the relationship between gradient quality and
convergence speed has not been studied extensively, it is reasonable to expect that lowervariance gradient estimates enable more effective steps through the loss landscape and
thus faster convergence Alternatively, variance reduction could allow the same gradient
quality to be achieved with fewer samples, reducing the overall computational cost.
A promising direction in that regard is sample reuse [Bitterli et al., 2020] across optimization iterations as successive iterations in gradient descent are closely related, similar
to consecutive frames in forward rendering. While this idea has been explored previously [Wang et al., 2023b; Chang et al., 2023], several challenges remain, including how
to extend such approaches to global illumination.
Another direction arises from the observation that the rendering integral can be parameterized in multiple ways, which leads to differential formulations with substantially
different variance (Chapter 5). Current approaches typically use a single parameterization along an entire light path, either spherical integrals [Li et al., 2018a; Soroka et al.,
2025] or path integrals [Zhang et al., 2020]. Mixing parameterizations along the path and
selecting those that minimize variance could provide a powerful alternative, though this
raises open questions such as how to combine parameterizations and correctly handle
boundary terms.
Approximations in Physics-based Differentiable Rendering Physically-based differentiable rendering often places strong emphasis on unbiased gradient estimators.
While this property is appealing from a theoretical perspective, it raises the question
of whether strict unbiasedness is always necessary in practice. For example, in Chapter 5 and Chapter 6, we have shown that several optimizations can succeed even when
the boundary term is ignored. Although these are primarily educational scenarios, it
suggests that allowing controlled bias in parts of the derivative, e.g., using approximations, could potentially enable substantial performance improvements while still providing gradients that are sufficiently accurate for optimization.
Differentiable Rendering for Rendering Differentiable rendering is often introduced in the context of inverse problems, where a scene is optimized to match observations, and derivatives are computed with respect to scene parameters. However, the
term “scene parameter” can be misleading: in principle, differentiable rendering allows
optimizing any parameter that influences the rendered image and the resulting loss, including parameters of the renderer. While most existing work focuses on inverse tasks,
144
Chapter 8. Conclusion
comparatively little attention has been paid to how differentiable rendering could be
used to directly improve rendering algorithms in terms of image quality and efficiency.
A possible direction is to optimize a rendering pipeline using image-based objectives.
Although it is possible to replace the entire pipeline with a neural network [Zeng et al.,
2025b], rendering algorithms incorporate extensive domain knowledge that would have
to be rediscovered by data-driven models. Instead, a promising alternative is to retain
the structure of a renderer while optimizing only the individual components within it.
The domain knowledge would then remain encoded in the structure and component
interactions. While components could be optimized in isolation, e.g., the shadow computation [Datta et al., 2022; Hu et al., 2025], differentiable rendering enables their joint
optimization by measuring output quality directly on the generated image. This accounts for the interplay between not only traditional components, but also potentially
ones based on neural networks. For example, one could try to optimize approximations
in a rasterization-based renderer to match the output of a physically-based renderer.

Intermediate Statements
For the main results, some properties regarding differentiability are required. These are
proven here.
Lemma 3. The Hankel matrix H is continuously differentiable in the moments and, if the
moments are strictly positive, its inverse H−1 is also continuously differentiable.
Proof. The moments are the entries of the Hankel matrix, so the first claim is trivially
