true. For the second claim, we have H−1 = det(H)
adj(H) and the determinant det(H) are polynomials in the entries of H. By Theorem 4
from strict positivity follows det(H) > 0, so the second claim holds.
The orthogonal polynomials depend smoothly on the moments:
Lemma 4. The coefficients of the orthogonal polynomials 𝑃𝑘 (𝑥; m) are infinitely differentiable in the moments m.
Proof. Expanding along the last row of the determinant form given in Definition 2, we

It is well known that, if the roots of a polynomial are real and simple, they are continuously differentiable functions of the coefficients. For orthogonal polynomials, the
roots are real and simple, which gives the following result:
Corollary 5. The 𝑛 roots of the orthogonal polynomials 𝑃𝑘 (𝑥; m) are continuously differentiable functions in the moments.

Lemma 5. If 𝑃𝑛 (𝜂; m) ≠ 0, the 𝑛 + 1 roots of 𝜓𝑛+1 (𝑥; 𝜂, m) are continuously differentiable
functions in 𝜂 and m. If 𝑃𝑛 (𝜂; m) = 0, the 𝑛 roots of 𝜓𝑛+1 (𝑥; 𝜂, m) are continuously differentiable in 𝜂 and m.
Proof. Recall that 𝜓𝑛+1 (𝑥; 𝜂) = 𝑃𝑛+1 (𝑥)𝑃𝑛 (𝜂) − 𝑃𝑛+1 (𝜂)𝑃𝑛 (𝑥) (Eq. A.5). Lemma 4 states
that 𝑃𝑛 and 𝑃𝑛+1 are infinitely differentiable in 𝜂 and m, and consequently, so are the coefficients of 𝜓𝑛+1 . Therefore all real and simple roots of 𝜓𝑛+1 are continuously differentiable
in 𝜂 and m.

Proof of Theorem 7
Proof. Without loss of generality, assume (𝜂 0, m0 ) ∈ Ω such that 𝑥 𝑗 (= 𝜂 0 ) > 𝑥 0 approaches 𝑦 𝑗 , as the other case can be treated equally. The roots {𝑥𝑖 (𝜂, m)}𝑛𝑖=0 of the polynomial 𝜓𝑛+1 (Corollary 4) and the roots {𝑦𝑖 (m)}𝑛𝑖=1 of the polynomial 𝑃𝑛 strictly interlace
at (𝜂 0, m0 ) (Theorem 10).
Let (𝜂 1, m1 ) ∈ Λ be a point on the singularity, such that 𝑃𝑛 (𝜂 1, m1 ) = 0. The
roots of 𝑃𝑛 are continuously differentiable functions in (𝜂, m) (Lemma 5), so the order established at (𝜂 0, m0 ) is maintained along a continuously differentiable path from
(𝜂 0, m0 ) to (𝜂 1, m1 ). Each 𝑦 𝑗 can be uniquely identified from which follows that 𝑦 𝑗 (m0 ) <
𝑥 𝑗 (𝜂 0, m0 )(= 𝜂 0 ) < 𝑦 𝑗+1 (m0 ) and 𝑦 𝑗 (m1 ) = 𝜂 1 .
At (𝜂 1, m1 ) ∈ Λ, the roots of 𝜓𝑛+1 are the 𝑛 roots of 𝑃𝑛 (see Eq. (A.5)). The roots of
𝜓𝑛+1 are continuously differentiable functions of 𝜂 and m (Lemma 5), and in particular
they are at (𝜂 1, m1 ). Therefore, if the path is reversed, it is a continuously differentiable
path from the roots of 𝑃𝑛 (m1 ) to 𝑛 of the roots of 𝜓𝑛+1 (𝑥; 𝜂 0, m0 ).
By construction, 𝑥 𝑗 (= 𝜂 0 ) follows a continuously differentiable path to 𝑦 𝑗 (= 𝜂 1 ),
so its reverse is a continuously differentiable path from 𝑦 𝑗 to 𝑥 𝑗 . If 𝑦 𝑗+1 exists, it must
correspond to the root 𝑥 𝑗+1 : it cannot correspond to any root ≤ 𝑥 𝑗 as the roots of 𝜓𝑛+1
are simple on the path (Lemma 9) and it cannot correspond to any root ≥ 𝑥 𝑗+2 because
the 𝑥𝑖 and 𝑦𝑖 strictly interlace on the path and there is 𝑥 𝑗+1 < 𝑦 𝑗+2 < 𝑥 𝑗+2 . A similar
argument holds for 𝑦 𝑗−1 if it exists. This establishes an order such that every 𝑦 𝑗 at (𝜂 1, m1 )
corresponds to 𝑥 𝑗 at (𝜂 0, m0 ).
Therefore, the only root without a corresponding 𝑦 at (𝜂 1, m1 ) is 𝑥 0 . 𝜓𝑛+1 is a degree
𝑛+1 polynomial that is reduced to a degree 𝑛 polynomial on a continuously differentiable
path from (𝜂 0, m0 ) to (𝜂 1, m1 ), so its leading coefficient 𝑎𝑛+1 is taken to zero on the path.
As 𝑎𝑛+1 vanishes, the roots 𝑥 1 < · · · < 𝑥𝑛 remain finite, and therefore the root 𝑥 0 is
unbounded and must go to −∞.

Proof of Lemma 1
Proof. Recall the definition of the weights 𝑤𝑘 = x⊤ H1−1 x . Since the moment-generating

In sample_path_adjoint in Listing 1, we keep track of the three surface points that

line 14. In the next iteration, the recursive term 𝜕𝜃 𝐿o is evaluated with the updated 𝝎.
Therefore, all terms of Eq. (B.9) are computed by sample_path_adjoint.

current point. This means that, similar to the code presented in Chapter 5, the contribution of a single point to the time-dependent derivatives is accumulated over two loop
iterations.

outgoing and incident direction 𝝎 o and 𝝎 i as well as point of reflection x. It is commonly
modeled as a combination of an ideal specular part and an ideal diffuse Lambertian part:

Listing 4 Pseudocode for the adjoint pass of temporal PRB using the three-point form.
The function intersect returns the intersection point of a ray with the scene as a point
in the surface parametrization M, to_ambient transforms a point from the parametrization to ambient space, considering the scene parameters and reparam_det returns the
value of the reparameterization determinant 𝐷.
def sample_path_adjoint(p𝑐𝑎𝑚 , 𝝎, 𝛿𝐻𝐿, 𝛿𝐻 𝜕𝑇 𝐿, 𝛿𝐻 ):
𝛽 = 1, 𝑇 = 0
p𝑝 = p𝑐𝑎𝑚
p𝑐 = detach(intersect(to_ambient(p𝑐𝑎𝑚 ), 𝝎))
for i in range(1, N+1):
x𝑝 = to_ambient(p𝑝 )
x𝑐 = to_ambient(p𝑐 )
𝜏 += time_on_segment(x𝑝 ,x)
𝑇 += detach(𝜏)
# Derivative of emission term
𝛿𝐻 𝐸 = 𝛽 * ⟨𝛿𝐻, 𝐿e (x𝑐 , x𝑐 → x𝑝 ,𝑇 )⟩
𝛿𝜃 += backward_grad(𝛿𝐻 𝐸, 1)
# Derivative of Jacobian determinant
𝐷 = reparam_det(x𝑝 , x𝑐 )
𝛿𝜃 += backward_grad(𝐷, 𝛿𝐿 * 𝐿 / 𝐷)
𝛿𝐻𝐿 -= 𝛿𝐻 𝐸
# Derivative of the BSDF
𝝎 ′ , bsdf_value, bsdf_pdf = sample_bsdf(. . . )
p𝑛 = detach(intersect(x𝑐 , 𝝎 ′ ))
x𝑛 = to_ambient(p𝑛 )
bsdf_value = eval_bsdf(x𝑐 ,x𝑐 → x𝑝 ,x𝑐 → x𝑛 )
𝛿𝜃 += backward_grad(bsdf_value, 𝛿𝐻𝐿 / bsdf_value)
# Backpropagate from time-dependent term
𝛿𝜃 += backward_grad(𝜏, 𝛿𝐻 𝜕𝑇 𝐿)
𝛿𝐻 𝜕𝑇 𝐿 -= 𝛽 * forward_grad(𝑇 , ⟨𝛿𝐻, 𝐿e (. . . )⟩)
𝛽 *= bsdf_value / bsdf_pdf
p𝑝 = p𝑐
p𝑐 = p𝑛
return 𝛿𝜃
C.3. Room Acoustics Metrics
161
The specular energy is reduced according to the scattering coefficient 0 ≤ 𝑠 ≤ 1, denoting the amount of scattered energy, and the absorption coefficient 0 ≤ 𝛼 ≤ 1, denoting the amount of absorbed energy. It is reflected in direction of 𝝎 r , which represents
the reflection vector for 𝝎 o reflected about the surface normal n. The diffuse energy is
weighted by the scattering coefficient and equally distributed across outgoing directions.
However, actual reflection patterns of finite surfaces always show a lobe around the
specular direction [Embrechts et al., 2001]. To account for this, Phong BSDFs have been
utilized in acoustic rendering [Mückl and Dachsbacher, 2020; Binek et al., 2019, 2023].
Binek et al. [2019] report slightly improved simulation accuracy compared to the classical Specular+Lambert BSDF for Phong exponents ranging from 0–1000. Additionaly,
using a Phong BSDF can significantly reduce variance across simulations [Binek et al.,
2023]. This is likely due to the non-zero contributions of the specular component at nonspecular angles, which enable the sampling of the specular component through emitter
sampling / diffuse rain. Notably, this also enables the differentiation of the specular component for detached sampling strategies. When modeling ideal specular reflections, only
the diffuse component can be differentiated. It is therefore crucial for our method to use
a continuous BSDF.
In our validation of the forward simulation we use the classical model (Eq. (C.7)) to
ensure comparability to other acoustic simulation methods. In the optimization tasks
we model the specular part with phong-style BSDFs using the beckmann distribution
proposed by Walter et al. [2007]. Appropriate values for the modeled roughness are
not systematically investigated in this work, but are not a limitation of our method.
The diffuse part and the weighting between specularly and diffusely reflected energy is
modeled according to Eq. (C.7).
C.3
Room Acoustics Metrics
In room acoustic design and architectural acoustics, sound propagation can assumed to
be linear and time-invariant (LTI). Rooms are therefore often described by their Room
Impulse Response (RIR) ℎ(𝑡), which is the response of the room to an acoustic impulse,
such as an idealized popping balloon. The RIR contains information about the energy
and arrival time of direct sound and reflections, and is the central basis for deriving room
acoustic metrics.
Most common room acoustic metrics do not require the phase information of the
impulse response, because they quantify the temporal energy distribution in some way,
so they can be derived from the energy time curve, which is the squared impulse response
𝐻 (𝑡) = ℎ 2 (𝑡). In our method, we simulate the ETC directly (see Eq. (6.5)).
The reverberation time 𝑇60 is arguably the most important room acoustic parameter
and describes the time after which the energy has decayed to a millionth of the incident
energy, corresponding to a 60 dB decrease in the sound pressure level [ISO 3382-1, 2009]:
𝑇60 =
